# SEO Audit Remediation — Whiteboard

## Status

Prepared — awaiting explicit approval.

## Purpose / Big Picture

The 2026-07-20 OpenSEO audit of `goodshepherdinsights.com` (50 pages crawled) found 32 broken internal links, a title/meta-description template bug affecting 26 pages, and a handful of smaller structural issues (duplicate H1, heading-order skips, slow responses, trailing-slash inconsistency, an unconfirmed noindex directive). This plan fixes every issue that has a settled, evidence-backed fix, and explicitly flags the few that still need a human decision before they can ship (see Risks and Decisions). Once implemented, a reader clicking any link on the site lands on a real page instead of a 404 — most visibly, the "schedule a consultation" CTA repeated across nine posts, which currently goes nowhere — and every page has an accurate, uniquely identifying title and description in search results.

All work targets content and templates as they exist on `origin/production` (research R1), not `main`, since that is where the live site's actual content lives — confirmed by `git ls-tree` showing the affected blog posts exist there and nowhere else in this repository's branches.

## User Contract

Numbered acceptance criteria (from `specs/001-seo-audit-remediation/spec.md`):

1. Every internal link that currently 404s resolves to a live, topically-correct page, or is removed with its surrounding sentence rewritten if no replacement content exists anywhere on the site (FR-001–FR-004).
2. The repeated `/consultation` CTA (13 occurrences across 13 posts, confirmed via `git grep` — the spec's original estimate was 9) points at the site's real `/contact/` page.
3. No page's title exceeds ~60 characters without an explicit documented exception (FR-005).
4. The four blog pagination pages, and the taxonomy (tag/category) pages, each have a distinct title and/or meta description instead of sharing one boilerplate string (FR-006/FR-007).
5. The one meta description over 160 characters is trimmed; none are pushed under 70 in the process (FR-008/FR-009).
6. The one page with two H1s has exactly one; the pages with heading-level skips descend one level at a time (FR-010/FR-011).
7. Trailing-slash URL duplicates are linked consistently (FR-012) — **not fully resolved in this pass**, see Risks and Decisions.
8. The taxonomy noindex directive is confirmed intentional or removed (FR-013) — **not fully resolved in this pass**, see Risks and Decisions.
9. Slow-response pages are investigated and fixed at the shared root cause where one exists (FR-014) — **not resolved in this pass, blocked on live-site investigation**, see Risks and Decisions.
10. A follow-up full-site audit shows zero occurrences of every issue type this plan addresses (FR-015 / spec SC-001–SC-006).

**Constraints carried over from the constitution** (`.specify/memory/constitution.md` v1.0.0): zero broken links (Principle I), no Lighthouse regression (Principle II), no hardcoded copy/URLs introduced (Principle III — this plan uses existing `disableTagline`/`metaTitle`/`metaDescription`/`robots` frontmatter fields, never new hardcoded strings in templates), a regression test before the `taglineSeparator` fix ships (Principle IV), no platform-specific code path (Principle V), and the `taglineSeparator` config bug itself is a live violation of Principle VI that this plan resolves.

**Exclusions**: authoring the two pieces of missing content (a "cybersecurity and data stewardship" companion article, an "AI in church management software" pillar/hub page) that two broken links point at — spec FR-003 explicitly scopes this out; those links are removed/rewritten instead.

**Assumptions carried from spec.md**: the ~60-char title / ~70–160-char description thresholds match the audit tool's own thresholds, not an independently chosen standard; the taxonomy `noindex` is presumed intentional pending confirmation (evidence found during this planning pass — see Risks — makes this presumption stronger, not weaker).

## Context and Orientation

- **Content model**: Astro content collections (`content.config.ts`) load Markdown/MDX from `src/content/<collection>/english/*.{md,mdx}` through a shared `basePage` Zod schema that already includes `metaTitle`, `metaDescription`, `disableTagline`, `robots`, and `canonical` as optional fields — every fix in this plan uses one of these existing fields or an existing per-post `<a href>`/markdown-link edit; none require a schema change.
- **Title/description assembly**: every page passes its frontmatter into `<Base>` (`src/layouts/Base.astro`), which forwards to `SEO.astro` (`src/layouts/components/SEO.astro`). `SEO.astro` builds `resolvedTitle` from `metaTitle ?? title ?? config.site.title`, then appends `config.site.tagline` (with a separator) unless `disableTagline` is true.
- **Known-issue comments**: several route files already carry `// ISSUE N` comments (e.g. `// ISSUE 6: CollectionPage schema for blog index listing` in `index.astro`, `// ISSUES 3/5: noindex, follow + ISSUE 6` in `category/[category].astro` and `tag/[tag].astro`) — evidence that these exact gaps were previously identified and partially addressed (the noindex directive itself), which is directly relevant to the noindex-intent question in Risks and Decisions.
- **Two authoring styles coexist** in `src/content/blog/english/`: most posts store their body as a single line of pre-rendered raw HTML (`<p _hash="...">...</p>`, likely from a copy/paste or import tool), while a few (`what-church-management-software-vendors-are-actually-doing-with-ai-in-2026.mdx`, `denominational-ai-statements-church-technology-decisions.mdx`) are genuine multi-line Markdown. This plan's diffs match each file's own existing style rather than converting one to the other.
- **Unfamiliar terms**: "taxonomy pages" = the auto-generated `/blog/category/<slug>/` and `/blog/tag/<slug>/` listing pages (`getStaticPaths` in `category/[category].astro`/`tag/[tag].astro` generates one page per category/tag discovered in post frontmatter — there is no per-category/tag content file to hold a description, hence T024/T025 compute one from `category.name`/`tag.name` instead).

## Pattern Audit and Evidence Ledger

| Decision | Repository or explicit-user evidence | Constraint learned | Reuse or deviation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fix `taglineSeparator` via a new pure function, not an inline conditional | `src/__tests__/getLocalUrlCTM.test.ts:1-2` is the only existing test in the repo, and it imports a plain function from `src/lib/utils/i18nUtils.ts` — there is no Astro-component test harness in `package.json`'s devDependencies | Logic that needs a regression test (Constitution IV) must live in a plain `.ts` module, not inline in a `.astro` component | Reuse: matches the existing one-function-per-concern style already in `src/lib/utils/textConverter.ts` (`slugifyyy`, `markdownify`, `humanize`, `titleify`, `plainify`, `toUpperCase`, `toLowerCase`, `toSentenceCase`, `removeWhitespace`) |
| `disableTagline={true}` placement | `src/pages/[...lang]/blog/category/[category].astro:75-80` and `tag/[tag].astro:76-81` already pass `disableTagline={true}` immediately after the `{...spread}` on `<Base>` | This is the established, working convention for suppressing the tagline on a page type | Reuse exactly — same prop, same position, same value |
| Category/tag description generated from `category.name`/`tag.name`, not new content files | `getStaticPaths` in both files (`category/[category].astro:16-56`, `tag/[tag].astro:16-56`) generates one page per taxonomy term discovered in post frontmatter — there is no content file per category/tag to hold a description | Taxonomy pages have no natural home for authored per-page copy; a description must be *derived*, not authored | Deviation from "content lives in data" only in the narrow sense that there's no data file to put it in — the derivation itself uses the same `category`/`tag` prop data every other part of these files already reads |
| Page-numbered pagination title/description reuses the file's own existing computation | `page/[slug].astro:88-90` (old) already computes `` `${postIndex.data.title} - Page ${currentPage}` `` for its JSON-LD `name` | The "- Page N" string format is this file's own established convention, not invented here | Reuse — the new `pageTitle` variable is the same expression, now also applied to the actual `<title>` |
| Homepage kicker demoted to `<div>`, not restructured | `src/layouts/components/global/SectionTitle.astro:19` renders `<h2>` — every other homepage section (`ServicesSection`, `AboutSection`, `BlogSection`, `CTASection`) already uses `<SectionTitle>` for its real section heading, confirmed via `grep -n "SectionTitle"` across all homepage section components | The homepage's heading structure is otherwise correct; only the pre-H1 kicker is out of order | Smallest compatible change — swap one tag; `src/styles/base.css:27-38`'s bare `h1-h6` selector meant 4 style properties (`font-secondary`, `leading-tight`, `font-bold`, `wrap-break-word`) had to be added explicitly onto the new `<div>` to keep visual output identical, since a `<div>` doesn't match that selector |
| Broken-link replacement targets chosen by anchor-text match, not slug guessing | `git grep` against `origin/production` content for each dead URL's surrounding anchor text (research R3) | A dead slug's *wording* is not reliable evidence of its intended target; the linking sentence's own anchor text is | No deviation — every replacement in this plan was verified this way before being written into a diff |
| Stray `<h1></h1>` removal, not heading renumbering | `grep -o '<h1[^>]*>[^<]*'` across all 6 affected posts found the exact same empty tag, always immediately after the closing `</p>` of the final CTA paragraph | This is a single, repeated content-authoring artifact (likely from whatever tool produced the compact single-line HTML body), not 6 independent structural problems | No deviation — same fix, same location, in every affected file |

## Interfaces and Dependencies

No external API, CLI, or service-to-service interface changes. Internal interfaces touched:

- **`resolveTaglineSeparator(separator: string | undefined): string`** — new exported function, `src/lib/utils/textConverter.ts`. Contract: returns `separator` unchanged if it is defined (including `""`), otherwise returns `" - "`.
- **`SEOProps.disableTagline`** — existing prop on `SEO.astro`/`Base.astro`, now also passed from `[single].astro`, `index.astro`, `page/[slug].astro` (previously only passed from `category/[category].astro`, `tag/[tag].astro`, `404.astro`). No signature change.
- **`basePage` content schema** (`content.config.ts`) — unchanged; `disableTagline: true` is now *set* in `src/content/homepage/english/-index.md` frontmatter, using a field the schema already declares.
- No changes to `astro.config.mjs`, `config.toml` schema, build scripts, or deploy configuration (`netlify.toml`/`vercel.json`/`wrangler.toml`) — Constitution Principle V (Multi-Platform Deploy Parity) is unaffected.


## Exact File Changes

### `src/lib/utils/textConverter.ts`
**Action:** Modify
**Why:** `SEO.astro` needs a pure, unit-testable function to resolve the tagline separator so the `taglineSeparator || " - "` bug (research R2) has a home consistent with this file's existing one-function-per-concern pattern (`slugifyyy`, `markdownify`, `removeWhitespace`, etc., src/lib/utils/textConverter.ts:1-122).
**Impact:** Adds one new named export; no existing export's signature or behavior changes.
**Tasks:** T020

```diff
--- a/src/lib/utils/textConverter.ts
+++ b/src/lib/utils/textConverter.ts
@@ -120,3 +120,9 @@
 export function removeWhitespace(text: string) {
   return text.replace(/\s+/g, " ").trim();
 }
+
+// Resolve the tagline separator, respecting an intentionally empty string
+// (a plain `||` fallback would silently discard "" and always use " - ")
+export function resolveTaglineSeparator(separator: string | undefined) {
+  return separator !== undefined ? separator : " - ";
+}
```

#### Reasoning
- Matches the established pattern of small, independently tested utility functions in this file rather than inlining logic in the `.astro` component, where it could not be unit tested (this project's Jest setup only exercises plain `.ts` modules — src/__tests__/getLocalUrlCTM.test.ts:1-2 — there is no Astro-component test harness in package.json).
- `separator !== undefined ? separator : " - "` (rather than `||`) is the exact, minimal change needed: it preserves the fallback for a genuinely absent separator while respecting an intentionally empty string, which is what config.toml's `taglineSeparator = ""` (src/config/config.toml:6) already expresses but the old `||` check silently discarded.

---

### `src/layouts/components/SEO.astro`
**Action:** Modify
**Why:** Line 49 (`const separator = config.site.taglineSeparator || " - ";`) discards an intentionally empty `config.site.taglineSeparator` and always falls back to `" - "` — a real violation of Constitution Principle VI (Config-Driven Behavior), and part of why appending the tagline reliably overflows page titles (research R2).
**Impact:** Only changes behavior for pages where `config.site.taglineSeparator` is set to something other than undefined (currently just the empty string in config.toml); every other call site is unaffected since the helper's fallback branch is identical to the old default.
**Tasks:** T020

```diff
--- a/src/layouts/components/SEO.astro
+++ b/src/layouts/components/SEO.astro
@@ -1,7 +1,11 @@
 ---
 import { Seo } from "astro-seo-meta";
 import { getLocaleUrlCTM, supportedLanguages } from "@/lib/utils/i18nUtils.ts";
-import { plainify, removeWhitespace } from "@/lib/utils/textConverter";
+import {
+  plainify,
+  removeWhitespace,
+  resolveTaglineSeparator,
+} from "@/lib/utils/textConverter";
 import config from ".astro/config.generated.json";
 
 export interface SEOProps {
@@ -46,7 +50,7 @@
 
 // Add tagline if not disabled
 if (!disableTagline && config.site.tagline) {
-  const separator = config.site.taglineSeparator || " - ";
+  const separator = resolveTaglineSeparator(config.site.taglineSeparator);
   resolvedTitle += separator + config.site.tagline;
 }
 
```

#### Reasoning
- Reuses the new `resolveTaglineSeparator` helper (src/lib/utils/textConverter.ts) instead of inlining an equivalent conditional here, keeping this component focused on assembling `resolvedTitle`/`resolvedDescription` rather than owning the fallback rule itself.
- Import line wrapped onto multiple lines to stay within this repo's default Prettier printWidth (80) — `.prettierrc` (root) sets no override, and every existing multi-specifier import in this file already fits under 80 chars on one line, so wrapping only this one is the correct, minimal formatting choice.

---

### `src/__tests__/resolveTaglineSeparator.test.ts`
**Action:** Create
**Why:** Constitution Principle IV (Test-First Regressions) requires a regression test for the `taglineSeparator` bug before the fix (T020) lands; this test must FAIL against the current `src/lib/utils/textConverter.ts` (which has no `resolveTaglineSeparator` export yet) and PASS once T020's textConverter.ts change and SEO.astro change are both applied.
**Impact:** New test file only; does not affect runtime behavior.
**Tasks:** T019

```diff
--- a/src/__tests__/resolveTaglineSeparator.test.ts
+++ b/src/__tests__/resolveTaglineSeparator.test.ts
@@ -0,0 +1,18 @@
+import { resolveTaglineSeparator } from "../lib/utils/textConverter";
+
+describe("resolveTaglineSeparator", () => {
+  test("Respects an intentionally empty separator instead of falling back", () => {
+    const result = resolveTaglineSeparator("");
+    expect(result).toBe("");
+  });
+
+  test("Falls back to ' - ' when the separator is undefined", () => {
+    const result = resolveTaglineSeparator(undefined);
+    expect(result).toBe(" - ");
+  });
+
+  test("Passes through a custom non-empty separator unchanged", () => {
+    const result = resolveTaglineSeparator(" | ");
+    expect(result).toBe(" | ");
+  });
+});
```

#### Reasoning
- Follows the exact structure of the one existing test file in this project, src/__tests__/getLocalUrlCTM.test.ts:1-2,54 (relative `../lib/utils/...` import, a top-level `describe("<functionName>", ...)` block, `test("<behavior sentence>", ...)` cases) — this is the only precedent for how this repo writes Jest tests, so the new test matches it rather than introducing a different style.
- Covers exactly the three cases research R2 turns on: an intentionally empty separator (must not become " - "), an undefined separator (must still fall back to " - " — preserving current behavior for every other config value), and a genuine custom separator passed through unchanged.

---

### `src/pages/[...lang]/blog/[single].astro`
**Action:** Modify
**Why:** Every individual blog post spreads its own frontmatter into `<Base>` (line 111) with no `disableTagline`, so every post title gets the ~44-character site tagline appended on top of an already-complete post title, causing 26 pages to exceed the ~60-character guideline (research R2, spec FR-005).
**Impact:** Every individual blog post's rendered `<title>` shortens by the tagline + separator (currently ~47 characters); meta description, OG tags, and structured data (BlogPosting schema built from `post?.data` on lines 86-98) are unaffected since they don't go through the tagline-appending branch.
**Tasks:** T021

```diff
--- a/src/pages/[...lang]/blog/[single].astro
+++ b/src/pages/[...lang]/blog/[single].astro
@@ -108,7 +108,7 @@
 };
 ---
 
-<Base {...post?.data}>
+<Base {...post?.data} disableTagline={true}>
   <PageHeader
     content={{
       badge: post?.data?.categories?.[0],
```

#### Reasoning
- Adds `disableTagline={true}` in the exact same position (immediately after the `{...spread}` on the `<Base>` tag) as the already-shipped precedent in `blog/category/[category].astro:75-80` and `blog/tag/[tag].astro:76-81`, so a future reader sees one consistent convention across all three template families rather than three different styles.

**Impact:** Only the blog index page's title shortens; the CollectionPage JSON-LD schema (lines 34-44) already derives its own `name`/`description` from `postIndex?.data` directly and is unaffected.

### `src/pages/[...lang]/blog/page/[slug].astro`
**Action:** Modify
**Why:** Pages 2+ of the blog listing spread the *same* `postIndex?.data` frontmatter into `<Base>` (line 98) as page 1, with no page-number distinction, so all four pagination pages currently render an identical `<title>` and meta description (research R2, spec FR-006) — the file already computes a page-numbered `name` for its JSON-LD schema (old lines 88-90) but never applied that same distinction to the actual page title.
**Impact:** Pages `/blog/page/2/`, `/blog/page/3/`, `/blog/page/4/` (and any further pages) now render a distinct title (`<index title> - Page N`) and description (existing description + `(Page N)`) instead of duplicating page 1's; the JSON-LD `collectionPageSchema.name` now reuses the same `pageTitle` value instead of recomputing an equivalent expression inline, removing a duplicated ternary.
**Tasks:** T023

```diff
--- a/src/pages/[...lang]/blog/page/[slug].astro
+++ b/src/pages/[...lang]/blog/page/[slug].astro
@@ -81,13 +81,19 @@
 const selfCanonical = absoluteUrl(Astro.url.pathname, Astro);
 
 // ISSUE 6 fix: CollectionPage schema for pagination listing
+const pageTitle = postIndex?.data?.title
+  ? `${postIndex.data.title} - Page ${currentPage}`
+  : `Blog - Page ${currentPage}`;
+const pageDescription = `${
+  postIndex?.data?.metaDescription ||
+  postIndex?.data?.description ||
+  "Ministry technology articles from Good Shepherd Insights."
+} (Page ${currentPage})`;
 const collectionPageSchema = {
   "@context": "https://schema.org",
   "@type": "CollectionPage",
   "@id": `${selfCanonical}#collectionpage`,
-  name: postIndex?.data?.title
-    ? `${postIndex.data.title} - Page ${currentPage}`
-    : `Blog - Page ${currentPage}`,
+  name: pageTitle,
   url: selfCanonical,
   isPartOf: {
     "@id": `${absoluteUrl(Astro.url.pathname.replace(/\/page\/\d+\/?$/, ""), Astro)}#collectionpage`,
@@ -95,7 +101,12 @@
 };
 ---
 
-<Base {...postIndex?.data} canonical={selfCanonical}>
+<Base
+  {...postIndex?.data}
+  canonical={selfCanonical}
+  title={pageTitle}
+  description={pageDescription}
+  disableTagline={true}>
   <PageHeader content={postIndex?.data} contentClass="max-w-3xl! xl:px-6" />
   <BlogsHeroSection />
   <LayoutWrapper
```

#### Reasoning
- Reuses the exact `` `${postIndex.data.title} - Page ${currentPage}` `` string this file already computes for its schema (old lines 88-90) rather than inventing a new title format, so the visible `<title>` and the structured-data `name` say the same thing.
- `disableTagline={true}` added for the same reason as every other blog template in this feature — the page-numbered title is already long enough without the site tagline appended on top.

### `src/pages/[...lang]/blog/category/[category].astro`
**Action:** Modify
**Why:** `const description = postIndex?.data.description;` (old line 61) means every category page reuses the *same* blog-index description ("Strategic technology insights for ministries and churches", 58 chars) regardless of which category it is — the exact cause of the duplicate/too-short meta description issue on this page type (research R2, spec FR-007/FR-009); a `// ISSUE 6` comment already marks this block as a known gap.
**Impact:** Each category page (currently only `/blog/category/technology/`) gets a description naming its own category instead of the shared boilerplate; `title` (line 60, already unique per category via `titleify`) is untouched.
**Tasks:** T024

```diff
--- a/src/pages/[...lang]/blog/category/[category].astro
+++ b/src/pages/[...lang]/blog/category/[category].astro
@@ -58,7 +58,7 @@
 const { category, blogFolder, postIndex } = Astro.props;
 
 let title = titleify(`${category.name} posts`);
-const description = postIndex?.data.description;
+const description = `Church technology insights and guidance tagged ${category.name}, from Good Shepherd Insights.`;
 
 // ISSUE 6: CollectionPage schema for category listing
 const selfCanonical = absoluteUrl(Astro.url.pathname, Astro);
```

#### Reasoning
- `category.name` is already available as a prop (destructured on line 58) and is exactly the piece of information needed to make the description unique per category — no new data source required.
- Phrasing mirrors the site's own established voice ("Good Shepherd Insights", "ministry technology") used throughout `config.toml`'s `site.description` and the existing shared boilerplate, rather than introducing new brand language.

### `src/pages/[...lang]/blog/tag/[tag].astro`
**Action:** Modify
**Why:** Identical issue to T024, one file over: `const description = postIndex?.data.description;` (old line 62) reuses the blog-index description on every tag page (6 pages: strategy, leadership, ministry, technology, stewardship, and any others) instead of a tag-specific one.
**Impact:** Each tag page gets a description naming its own tag instead of the shared boilerplate; `title` (line 61) is untouched.
**Tasks:** T025

```diff
--- a/src/pages/[...lang]/blog/tag/[tag].astro
+++ b/src/pages/[...lang]/blog/tag/[tag].astro
@@ -59,7 +59,7 @@
 
 // Check if blog posts exist in the folder
 let title = titleify(`${tag.name} posts`);

+const description = `Ministry technology articles tagged ${tag.name}, from Good Shepherd Insights.`;
 
 // ISSUE 6: CollectionPage schema for tag listing

```

#### Reasoning
- Same fix shape as T024 for parallel consistency between the two taxonomy templates, using `tag.name` (already destructured on line 58) as the differentiator.

### `src/content/homepage/english/-index.md`
**Action:** Modify
**Why:** The homepage title ("Strategic technology leadership for mid-sized churches | Good Shepherd Insights", 81 chars) is already over the ~60-char guideline before the ~44-character tagline ("Fractional CTO leadership for mid-sized churches") is even appended — and the two phrases are near-duplicates of each other, so keeping the tagline appended here would make the rendered title both oversized and repetitive (research R2, spec FR-005).
**Impact:** Homepage `<title>` shortens from ~130 to 49 characters; `metaDescription` and `pageType` are untouched. `og:site_name` (src/layouts/components/global/Head.astro) already separately carries "Good Shepherd Insights", so dropping the brand suffix from the visible title does not remove it from the page's metadata entirely.
**Tasks:** T026

```diff
--- a/src/content/homepage/english/-index.md
+++ b/src/content/homepage/english/-index.md
@@ -1,5 +1,6 @@
 ---
-title: "Strategic technology leadership for mid-sized churches | Good Shepherd Insights"
+title: "Fractional CTO Leadership for Mid-Sized Churches"
 metaDescription: "Move past scattered decisions and vendor confusion. Get a Fractional CTO partner to build a strategic roadmap for your church's technology."
 pageType: "home"
+disableTagline: true
 ---
```

#### Reasoning
- `disableTagline: true` is set directly in this content file's frontmatter (the schema already supports it — `disableTagline: z.boolean().optional()` in `content.config.ts`) rather than hardcoded in `src/pages/[...lang]/index.astro`, keeping the override with the content it affects, consistent with Constitution Principle III (Content Lives in Data, Not Templates).
- FLAGGED FOR REVIEW (see Risks and Decisions): the exact replacement title text is a marketing/brand-copy decision, not a mechanical fix — this is a proposed, defensible shortening, not a unilateral final call.

### `src/layouts/components/sections/HeroSection.astro`
**Action:** Modify
**Why:** The homepage's only heading-order defect (research into `src/pages/[...lang]/index.astro`'s full section chain shows `ServicesSection`, `AboutSection`, and `BlogSection` all correctly render their section title via the shared `SectionTitle` component, which itself renders an `<h2>` — src/layouts/components/global/SectionTitle.astro:19) is that `HeroSection`'s decorative "kicker" line above the page's real `<h1>` (old line 105) is itself marked up as an `<h3>`, so the very first heading the page renders is an H3, before any H1 exists yet.
**Impact:** The kicker no longer participates in the document's heading outline. Visual appearance is preserved exactly: `src/styles/base.css:27-38` applies `font-secondary text-dark leading-tight font-bold tracking-wide wrap-break-word` to every bare `h1`-`h6` tag site-wide, plus `text-h3-sm md:text-h3` to `h3` specifically — removing the `<h3>` tag would silently drop `font-secondary`, `leading-tight`, `font-bold`, and `wrap-break-word` (the element's own classes already separately cover `text-dark`, sizing, and `tracking-wider`, so only those four properties were actually at risk). All four are now added explicitly onto the `<div>` so nothing changes visually.
**Tasks:** T029

```diff
--- a/src/layouts/components/sections/HeroSection.astro
+++ b/src/layouts/components/sections/HeroSection.astro
@@ -102,10 +102,10 @@
           data-aos="reveal-anim-left"
           data-aos-offset="0"
           data-aos-duration="600">
-          <h3 class="text-dark text-h6 sm:text-h5-sm md:text-h4-md lg:text-h3 inline-block tracking-wider">
+          <div class="text-dark font-secondary wrap-break-word inline-block text-h6 leading-tight font-bold tracking-wider sm:text-h5-sm md:text-h4-md lg:text-h3">
             {markdownify(subTitle)}
             <div class="border-dark/60 h-1 border-y" />
-          </h3>
+          </div>
         </div>
       )
     }
```

#### Reasoning
- The kicker is decorative label text over a large `<h1>`, not independent outline content — demoting it to a non-heading `<div>` is the smallest change that removes the out-of-order heading without inventing a new heading level or restructuring the section.
- `font-secondary`, `leading-tight`, `font-bold`, and `wrap-break-word` are added explicitly to replace exactly the styling that was previously supplied by the global bare-tag `h1,h2,h3,h4,h5,h6` selector in `src/styles/base.css:27-33`, which no longer matches once the tag is a `<div>`. `tracking-wide` from that same global rule is intentionally NOT added: the element's own `tracking-wider` class was already the one taking visual effect (Tailwind v4's `utilities` cascade layer outranks the `base` layer regardless of source order or tag), so `tracking-wide` was never visually contributing and adding it now would do nothing.
- Recommend a visual smoke-test on a preview deploy in addition to the automated checks before this ships, since it's the homepage hero — but the styling itself is no longer a known gap.

**Action:** Modify
**Why:** Five hardcoded `<a href>` values in this post's body point at dead URLs (research R3: anchor-text-matched against real `origin/production` content), and the body ends with a stray, content-less `<h1></h1>` tag appended right after the closing CTA paragraph, which is this page's specific cause of the audit's heading-order-skip finding.
**Impact:** Five internal links now resolve to the live pages their anchor text actually describes; the `/consultation` CTA now goes to the real `/contact/` page; the trailing empty H1 is removed, so the page's only H1 is the one the layout renders from frontmatter `title`.
**Tasks:** T004, T033

```diff
--- a/src/content/blog/english/church-management-software-ai-data-privacy.mdx
+++ b/src/content/blog/english/church-management-software-ai-data-privacy.mdx
@@ -27,4 +27,4 @@
 draft: false
 ---
 
-<p _hash="MDCN99"><strong>TL;DR:</strong> Most ChMS vendors do not clearly state whether congregant data trains their AI models, and the ones that do are the exception, not the rule. The gap between concern and governance is wide: 83% of church leaders worry about AI data privacy, yet only 5% have formal guidelines in place. Knowing what to ask, where to look in the contract, and what the answer must actually say is the practical starting point for every ministry evaluating AI features right now.</p><p _hash="snAJwV"><strong>What You Need to Know About ChMS AI and Congregant Data:</strong></p><ul _hash="nMRcCq"><li _hash="fPDI8W"><p _hash="RkhCkk">Some vendors explicitly prohibit training on congregant data; others do not, and the difference lives in the contract, not the demo.</p></li><li _hash="TXK7q8"><p _hash="jmGQtc">Once data enters an AI training pipeline, it <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.wardandsmith.com/article/the-risk-landscape-legal-operational-and-ethical-risks-in-ai-vendor-engagements">cannot be extracted</a>, making upfront contractual protection the only meaningful safeguard.</p></li><li _hash="Ubdv3F"><p _hash="tVj3jn">Enterprise-grade AI accounts differ from consumer-tier tools in one critical way: a binding Data Processing Agreement that prohibits training on your data.</p></li><li _hash="4eY5vo"><p _hash="UMU8Qf">Staff informally using free consumer AI tools with congregant data is the highest-risk scenario in most churches today.</p></li><li _hash="CaqDUf"><p _hash="5uGWKy">Four contract-level questions (training prohibition, subprocessor list, deletion terms, breach liability) are the minimum evaluation framework before any AI feature goes live.</p></li><li _hash="khn3ZY"><p _hash="X4w2SR">The Lausanne Movement's proposed AITAC certification framework offers a useful self-assessment benchmark today, even before the certification body is operational.</p></li></ul><p _hash="L9JF4n">Does your church management software vendor use congregant data to train its AI models? The direct answer is: it depends on the vendor, the product tier, and specific terms buried in your contract. According to <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">a March 2026 Barna Group survey of 1,306 church leaders</a>, 83% of church leaders are concerned about AI data privacy, and that concern is well-founded. Some ChMS vendors explicitly commit that congregant data never trains AI models. Others have made no such commitment. And a significant number of church staff use free consumer AI tools daily that carry no contractual protections at all. The framework below helps you tell the difference.</p><p _hash="SqKNAE">In this guide, we walk through what ChMS vendors' AI terms actually say about congregant data, why the distinction between consumer-grade and enterprise-grade AI matters more than most church leaders realize, and what the contract language must say before you click "enable" on any AI feature. We draw on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">a March 2026 Barna Group survey of 1,306 church leaders</a>, a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.wardandsmith.com/article/the-risk-landscape-legal-operational-and-ethical-risks-in-ai-vendor-engagements">June 2026 legal analysis from Ward and Smith, P.A.</a>, the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://lausanne.org/global-analysis/governing-ai-in-gods-house">Lausanne Movement's AITAC governance paper</a>, and a current snapshot of what the four largest ChMS AI platforms (Pushpay, ACST, Tithely, and Gloo) have actually committed to in their terms. If your ministry is in the middle of a renewal decision, evaluating a new platform, or simply trying to understand what your current vendor is doing with congregant data, this is where we start. For a wider view of the category, see <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/ai-in-church-management-software-2026">our overview of what ChMS vendors are actually doing with AI in 2026</a>.</p><h2 _hash="wiOGVZ">Table of Contents</h2><ul _hash="Y02dnw"><li _hash="NrvW5d"><p _hash="gZXh0D">Why 83% of Church Leaders Are Right to Be Concerned</p></li><li _hash="jIqJIR"><p _hash="YQoVN1">The Risk Most Ministries Miss: When Congregant Data Enters an AI Training Pipeline</p></li><li _hash="iDZury"><p _hash="HyMYPM">What Enterprise-Grade AI Actually Means for Church Data</p></li><li _hash="lkZegn"><p _hash="0MXH9M">What the ChMS Vendor Landscape Looks Like Right Now</p></li><li _hash="geo9LX"><p _hash="uURHTF">Four Contract-Level Questions to Ask Before You Sign or Renew</p></li><li _hash="zHacro"><p _hash="IHcp8c">What AITAC Is, and Why It Will Change How We Evaluate Vendors</p></li><li _hash="5TKALG"><p _hash="lT8ivg">Frequently Asked Questions</p></li><li _hash="itjnUF"><p _hash="hA5v75">Key Takeaways</p></li><li _hash="tKxVJh"><p _hash="OJPTWE">Sources and References</p></li><li _hash="rWcEs8"><p _hash="YocGba">Where We Go From Here</p></li></ul><h2 _hash="1RYU9x">Why 83% of Church Leaders Are Right to Be Concerned</h2><p _hash="MXdl19">The concern is not abstract. The same <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">March 2026 Barna Group study</a> found that 83% of church leaders are concerned about AI data privacy, yet only 5% of churches have formal AI guidelines in place. That means most ministries are navigating this risk without a policy, a framework, or a clear understanding of what their vendor has actually committed to.</p><p _hash="J2pz6r">Barna also reports that 33% of churches are currently using AI in some part of ministry operations. Put those numbers together and the picture is clear: a substantial share of churches with no policy are already in active use. The concern is correct, the governance is not catching up, and the gap between the two is where exposure accumulates.</p><p _hash="kmqUHg">Church data is not generic business data. Prayer requests, giving records, attendance patterns, counseling notes, and family composition information occupy a uniquely sensitive category. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://lausanne.org/global-analysis/governing-ai-in-gods-house">Lausanne Movement's AITAC paper</a> makes the point sharply: data from prayer requests "could be interpreted by some AI systems as mental health indicators." Most church leaders never consider that when they enable AI features on their ChMS.</p><p _hash="hooe8Y">We say this calmly because the right response is a framework, not anxiety. The concern is legitimate. What we owe ourselves is a clear way to evaluate vendors, ask the right questions, and document the answers.</p><p _hash="6iFnMU"><strong>Key Point:</strong> 83% of church leaders are concerned about AI data privacy, but only 5% have formal guidelines. The gap between concern and governance is where the real risk lives.</p><h2 _hash="Rh8TTc">The Risk Most Ministries Miss: When Congregant Data Enters an AI Training Pipeline</h2><p _hash="mFErOY">When a vendor uses your ministry's data to train an AI model, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.wardandsmith.com/article/the-risk-landscape-legal-operational-and-ethical-risks-in-ai-vendor-engagements">Ward and Smith, P.A. notes in a June 2026 legal analysis</a>, "once data enters a model's training pipeline, it may be impossible to extract." The practical effect of training-based data use is permanent, not reversible upon request.</p><p _hash="nLBJjz">It helps to say plainly what AI model training means in this context. The vendor uses examples drawn from your congregants' data to improve or build the AI's responses. Once that happens, the data is distributed across millions of model parameters. There is no "delete congregant Jane Smith's data from the model" option. The information has been absorbed into the system's underlying weights.</p><p _hash="wb2LA1">The Lausanne paper frames the human side of this honestly: "Churches are applying AI without the necessary level of transparency or meaningful consent from their congregants." That framing is not accusatory toward vendors. It is honest about the gap that has opened between what congregants assume and what is actually happening with their data.</p><p _hash="ZSUq02">There is also a security dimension. Ward and Smith identify "model inversion and data extraction attacks" as a specific category of risk. In some cases, adversaries can reverse-engineer training data from model outputs. Congregant data embedded in a training set could theoretically be extracted by third parties later, even if the original vendor never intended that exposure.</p><p _hash="EobcKh">None of this appears on a vendor demo. It appears in the terms of service, the Data Processing Agreement, and the subprocessor list. Those three documents are where the question gets answered.</p><p _hash="hV4su5"><strong>Key Point:</strong> Once congregant data enters an AI training pipeline, it cannot be extracted. The protection must be contractual and in place before the data ever leaves your system.</p><h2 _hash="y2xRrH">What Enterprise-Grade AI Actually Means for Church Data</h2><p _hash="1R1KMC">The distinction between consumer-tier and enterprise-tier AI is not a marketing label. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.progressivechurchmedia.com/church-ai-policy/">Progressive Church Media's April 2026 analysis</a> documents that free consumer accounts (ChatGPT free tier, free Gemini, free Claude) may use inputs to train models, provide no Data Processing Agreements, and offer no organizational controls. Enterprise accounts contractually prohibit training on customer data, provide DPAs, offer organizational access controls, and often include audit logs.</p><p _hash="qRx7vL">Two tiers, in plain language:</p><p _hash="UXyPdW">The consumer tier covers free accounts on public AI platforms. There are no contract protections. There is no DPA. There are no organizational controls. Staff using these accounts with congregant data, even informally, is the highest-risk scenario in most churches today.</p><p _hash="MJaIKH">The enterprise tier covers paid business accounts (ChatGPT Team or Enterprise, Microsoft 365 Copilot with Enterprise Data Protection, Claude for Work). There is a contractual prohibition on training. A DPA is in place. Organizational controls and audit logs are part of the product.</p><p _hash="Nfa096">This distinction also reaches into the ChMS layer. A ChMS that has built its AI on top of consumer-tier API access, or that uses a foundation model without contractual training prohibitions at the foundation layer, may expose church data even when the ChMS vendor itself has not explicitly said "we train on your data." The contract chain matters, not just the headline assurance.</p><p _hash="F1JkJK">One regulatory point belongs here. The FTC's <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.progressivechurchmedia.com/church-ai-policy/">updated COPPA rule, effective April 22, 2026</a>, now treats biometric identifiers as personal information and requires separate verifiable parental consent for AI training uses, with penalties up to $53,088 per violation. Churches with children's ministry data in their ChMS should review their COPPA posture as part of any AI evaluation.</p><p _hash="ph8PzQ">The question to ask any ChMS vendor sounds technical, but it is the right one: "Is your AI integration governed by a Data Processing Agreement with your foundation model provider that prohibits training on our data?" If the answer is yes, ask to see the documentation. If the answer is unclear, that is itself useful information.</p><p _hash="gYIb2r"><strong>Key Point:</strong> "Enterprise-grade AI" means a contractual prohibition on training, a Data Processing Agreement, and organizational controls. It is not just a premium price point. Ask for the DPA documentation before enabling any AI feature.</p><h2 _hash="2myzzB">What the ChMS Vendor Landscape Looks Like Right Now</h2><p _hash="j13ug4">The major established ChMS vendors (Pushpay and ACST) have made explicit public commitments that congregant data is not used to train external AI models. Gloo's terms of service, as reported by MIT Technology Review in August 2025, reserve the right to ingest health and wellness information from prayer requests and wellness assessments. That is a meaningfully different data posture.</p><p _hash="gT86Rt">Here is what a current reading of stated terms and independent reporting shows. We present this as a factual snapshot, not as an endorsement or an attack on any vendor.</p><p _hash="jcdxlA"><strong>Pushpay.</strong> AI is built as a "query builder" that interacts with a structured semantic layer, not raw congregant information. The company has stated that member data "is strictly never utilized to train external (foundation) models." Sequential safety, accuracy, and transparency validation checks are part of the architecture, as reported by <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">Church Tech Today in June 2026</a>.</p><p _hash="Q1c3Bk"><strong>ACST (Realm and Ministry Platform).</strong> Ministry Platform AI, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">launched May 2026</a>, explicitly commits that "congregation data queried through Ministry Platform AI is retrieved live, securely returned, and not stored or used to train AI models." It is built on the open MCP standard. Role-based permissions, audit trails, and table-level controls are part of the launch architecture.</p><p _hash="2gBOXg"><strong>Tithely (TithelyAI).</strong> Public materials claim privacy and role-based access, but no formal DPA statement was located in publicly available sources as of June 2026. The product is still in pilot or early-launch phase. Church leaders evaluating Tithely should request a written DPA directly before enabling any AI feature.</p><p _hash="5h2qmZ"><strong>Gloo.</strong> <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.technologyreview.com/">MIT Technology Review reported in August 2025</a> that Gloo's terms of service reserve the right to ingest "health and wellness information" from prayer requests and wellness assessments for its data engine. Broad data enrichment partner exceptions are also present. Binding arbitration clauses limit congregant legal recourse. This is the clearest case in the current landscape where independent reporting and the terms of service diverge from what a church leader would typically expect.</p><p _hash="YszJZw"><strong>Consumer AI tools used informally by staff.</strong> The highest-risk category is not any named ChMS vendor. It is church staff using free consumer AI tools with congregant data informally, with no DPA, no controls, and no policy. This is where most churches are most exposed right now, and the issue is connected to <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/ai-rollout-staff-trust">rolling out AI to church staff in a way that preserves trust</a>.</p><p _hash="KVSzDI">What this snapshot is for: it helps church leaders ask better questions, not make legal determinations. The terms shift, products evolve, and vendor documentation should always be re-read at the moment of decision.</p><p _hash="f6ljFf"><strong>Key Point:</strong> The vendor distinctions that matter most (training prohibitions, DPA terms, subprocessor disclosures) do not appear in demo calls. They live in the contract. Request the documentation before enabling AI features.</p><h2 _hash="Jq6Cso">Four Contract-Level Questions to Ask Before You Sign or Renew</h2><p _hash="eQ8z6n"><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.wardandsmith.com/article/the-risk-landscape-legal-operational-and-ethical-risks-in-ai-vendor-engagements">Ward and Smith, P.A.'s June 2026 legal analysis</a> identifies four non-negotiable contract provisions that every church should verify before signing any agreement with a ChMS or AI vendor: a prohibition on training on customer data, meaningful data breach indemnities, transparency on subprocessors, and deletion and portability rights at exit.</p><p _hash="ngYk8I">This builds on the surface-level evaluation in <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/chms-vendor-ai-renewal-questions">the five questions we recommend church leaders raise at renewal</a>. That companion piece surfaces the questions. The four below are about what the answers must actually say.</p><p _hash="iXMAd2"><strong>1. "Does your contract prohibit using our congregant data to train AI models, and is that in writing?"</strong> This is the foundational question. A verbal reassurance from a sales representative is not the same as a contractual commitment. The answer must appear in the DPA or the Terms of Service, not just in a sales conversation. If a vendor cannot point to specific contract language, the commitment is not yet real.</p><p _hash="YkIwJy"><strong>2. "Who are your subprocessors, and what are their data training policies?"</strong> Ward and Smith call subprocessor transparency non-negotiable because a vendor can prohibit training on their own end while passing data to a foundation model provider whose terms allow training. Ask for the full subprocessor list and ask what DPA governs each subprocessor relationship. A vendor that cannot answer this question fully has not done the work yet themselves.</p><p _hash="KL7ULs"><strong>3. "What happens to our congregant data if we cancel?"</strong> <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">Church Tech Today's June 2026 piece</a> identifies this as a critical and frequently unanswered question. The answers you want: a defined deletion timeline (30 to 60 days is reasonable), written confirmation of deletion when it happens, and a data export mechanism so you can take your data with you. Ask for this before you need it, not after.</p><p _hash="R8G48X"><strong>4. "What is your liability if our data is breached through your AI systems?"</strong> Ward and Smith describe this as a "meaningful data breach indemnity." Many vendor contracts cap liability at a low fixed dollar amount, or at the fees you have paid, which may be a small fraction of the actual harm to congregants. Ask what the vendor is prepared to stand behind in the event of a real incident.</p><p _hash="9j2Fgh"><strong>Key Point:</strong> Ask for the Data Processing Agreement and the subprocessor list before signing or renewing. If a vendor cannot produce both, that is itself important information about their data governance maturity.</p><h2 _hash="b1Jcjy">What AITAC Is, and Why It Will Change How We Evaluate Vendors</h2><p _hash="TlVr3m">The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://lausanne.org/global-analysis/governing-ai-in-gods-house">Lausanne Movement's October 2025 AITAC governance paper</a> proposes an AI Trust and Accountability Consortium modeled on the ECFA: a certification body that would independently verify whether AI vendors serving churches meet a defined standard of data stewardship, transparency, and congregant consent.</p><p _hash="oTMvdh">A short history helps explain why this matters. Before ECFA, churches had no independent way to evaluate a ministry's financial stewardship. Donors had to rely on the ministry's own representations. ECFA created a third-party standard that turned trust into something verifiable. AITAC is attempting to solve the same problem for AI: turning vendor claims about data handling into something a church leader can verify without becoming a privacy lawyer.</p><p _hash="1EO3IY">AITAC is not yet operational, so it cannot be used as a vendor filter today. The framework it is building, however, is already useful: clear disclosures of data collection practices, plain-language explanations of data use, meaningful consent processes for congregants. Those criteria can be applied to any vendor documentation right now, even without a certification stamp.</p><p _hash="6o01Aj">The forward-looking guidance is straightforward. As AITAC moves from proposal to implementation, it will likely become the standard certification that discerning church leaders look for in ChMS AI vendors. This connects to broader institutional trends explored in <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/denominational-ai-statements">how denominations are beginning to address AI governance at the institutional level</a>. Asking your current vendor whether they intend to pursue AITAC certification is a reasonable part of any future renewal conversation.</p><p _hash="7TZrOi"><strong>Key Point:</strong> AITAC is not yet operational, but its proposed standard (independent verification of AI data stewardship, transparency, and consent practices) is the right benchmark. Church leaders can use the AITAC framework as a self-assessment tool for vendor evaluation today.</p><h2 _hash="rX5e6j">Frequently Asked Questions</h2><h3 _hash="ccyga1">Can prayer request data be used to train AI models?</h3><p _hash="49AH1O">It depends entirely on the vendor's terms and architecture. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://lausanne.org/global-analysis/governing-ai-in-gods-house">Lausanne Movement's AITAC paper</a> notes that prayer request data "could be interpreted by some AI systems as mental health indicators," a consequence most church leaders do not anticipate when enabling AI features. Whether a vendor uses that data for training is governed by their DPA and terms of service, not their marketing materials. Asking specifically about unstructured text fields (prayer notes, counseling notes) is important.</p><h3 _hash="h29Gvn">What is a Data Processing Agreement, and why does my church need one?</h3><p _hash="9BakvN">A Data Processing Agreement is a binding contract between your church (the data controller) and the vendor (the data processor) that specifies exactly how your congregants' data can be used, stored, and deleted. Without one, you have only the vendor's goodwill, not a legal obligation, governing data handling. Any ChMS vendor deploying AI features should be able to provide a DPA on request. If they cannot, treat that as a significant signal about their governance maturity.</p><h3 _hash="h5b617">Does my church need congregant consent before using AI features in our ChMS?</h3><p _hash="G4qNaE">For most standard AI-assisted features (giving summaries, attendance analytics, search), congregant consent is not typically required beyond your existing privacy policy disclosures, provided the vendor is not using that data for model training. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.progressivechurchmedia.com/church-ai-policy/">FTC's updated COPPA rule, effective April 22, 2026</a>, requires separate verifiable parental consent for any AI training on data from children under 13, with penalties up to $53,088 per violation. Churches with children's ministry data in their ChMS should review their COPPA compliance posture.</p><h3 _hash="FbluKp">What should we do if our ChMS vendor will not answer these questions directly?</h3><p _hash="vN7iBO">Ask in writing first, since email creates a record. If the vendor still does not provide a written response on data training and DPA availability, escalate to their data privacy officer or legal contact. If written confirmation is still not forthcoming, that response is itself meaningful information about the vendor's governance posture. At that point, we recommend involving your church's legal counsel before renewing or enabling AI features.</p><h3 _hash="KG6nB4">Is free ChatGPT safe to use for church communications?</h3><p _hash="dXfj9g">Free consumer-tier accounts on any major AI platform (ChatGPT free, Gemini free, Claude free) may use inputs to improve their models, provide no DPA, and offer no organizational controls. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.progressivechurchmedia.com/church-ai-policy/">Progressive Church Media's April 2026 analysis</a> is clear on this point. For drafting generic communications with no congregant data included, the risk is lower. For any task that involves member names, giving records, prayer requests, or other ministry data, consumer-tier AI tools are not appropriate. Enterprise accounts with DPAs are the minimum standard.</p><h3 _hash="y7SpDf">How do we know if our ChMS vendor's "no training" commitment is real?</h3><p _hash="GG9PNe">Currently, we do not have an independent way to verify these commitments, since vendor statements are self-reported. The Lausanne AITAC proposal is designed to solve this through independent certification, but it is not yet operational. The practical step today is to obtain the commitment in writing in the DPA, understand what subprocessors the vendor uses, and ask whether the vendor's foundation model provider's own terms prohibit training on enterprise customer data. Three layers of documentation are stronger than one.</p><h3 _hash="n3taAk">What happens to our data if we cancel our ChMS subscription?</h3><p _hash="D7LMrR">This depends entirely on the vendor's terms. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">Church Tech Today's June 2026 piece</a> identifies this as one of the most important and frequently unanswered questions in ChMS evaluation. We recommend asking for a specific deletion timeline (30 to 60 days is reasonable), a written confirmation of deletion when it occurs, and a data export mechanism before you need it, not after you have decided to cancel.</p><h3 _hash="m8lBqi">What is the biggest AI data risk most churches are not thinking about?</h3><p _hash="Y9Jhh0">Staff using free consumer AI tools with congregant data informally, without a policy, without a DPA, and without organizational awareness. Most of the attention in this conversation focuses on what ChMS vendors are doing, which is right and important. But the largest practical exposure for most churches right now is a staff member pasting a list of prayer requests or member contact information into a free AI tool to draft a communication. A simple, clear staff AI use policy is often the most impactful first step a church can take.</p><h2 _hash="GAl7Yi">Key Takeaways</h2><ul _hash="WJ2LVt"><li _hash="akxkyz"><p _hash="lBlRrZ"><strong>83% of church leaders are concerned about AI data privacy, but only 5% have formal AI guidelines.</strong> The governance gap is where real exposure lives, not just the technology itself.</p></li><li _hash="hdruXM"><p _hash="12cLiB"><strong>Once congregant data enters an AI training pipeline, it cannot be extracted.</strong> The protection must be contractual and verified before any AI feature is enabled.</p></li><li _hash="KMMDKX"><p _hash="JglRJz"><strong>Consumer-tier AI tools (free ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude) are not appropriate for church data.</strong> Enterprise accounts with Data Processing Agreements are the minimum standard for any use involving congregant information.</p></li><li _hash="1LANQA"><p _hash="AdIkDN"><strong>The major established ChMS vendors have made explicit "no external training" commitments, but not every vendor has.</strong> The differences live in contracts and terms of service, not in demo calls.</p></li><li _hash="rXfa8q"><p _hash="k9Bg1E"><strong>Four contract-level questions every church should ask:</strong> a no-training commitment in writing, a subprocessor list with their DPAs, data deletion terms at cancellation, and breach liability provisions.</p></li><li _hash="zFI7IU"><p _hash="p7qHkA"><strong>AITAC, an independent AI certification body for faith contexts, is in development</strong> and will provide a future standard for vendor evaluation. Its proposed framework is a useful self-assessment template today.</p></li><li _hash="sTVBcn"><p _hash="lzBVll"><strong>A clear staff AI use policy is often the most impactful immediate step.</strong> Informal use of consumer AI tools by staff is the highest-risk scenario for most churches right now.</p></li></ul><h2 _hash="75LNXw">Sources and References</h2><ul _hash="4q2YIi"><li _hash="RaT4OR"><p href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">barna.com</a></p></li><li _hash="tKBoQ6"><p _hash="fRJDTw">Ward and Smith, P.A., "The Risk Landscape: Legal, Operational, and Ethical Risks in AI Vendor Engagements," June 2026. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.wardandsmith.com/article/the-risk-landscape-legal-operational-and-ethical-risks-in-ai-vendor-engagements">wardandsmith.com</a></p></li><li _hash="XS39Pq"><p _hash="U8l6xX">Lausanne Movement, "Governing AI in God's House" (AITAC governance paper), October 2025. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://lausanne.org/global-analysis/governing-ai-in-gods-house">lausanne.org</a></p></li><li _hash="7cRYSn"><p _hash="TUzJcR">Progressive Church Media, "Church AI Policy: What Leaders Need to Know in 2026," April 2026. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.progressivechurchmedia.com/church-ai-policy/">progressivechurchmedia.com</a></p></li><li
```
