href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">churchtechtoday.com</a></p></li><li _hash="6Fp6XF"><p _hash="yiQGDG">BusinessWire, "ACST Launches Ministry Platform AI, Bringing Secure, Ministry-Aware AI to Churches," May 7, 2026. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">businesswire.com</a></p></li><li _hash="feGD5B"><p _hash="hBJIuW">MIT Technology Review reporting on Gloo terms of service, August 2025. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.technologyreview.com/">technologyreview.com</a></p></li><li _hash="SqgTug"><p _hash="eKMoLd">FTC Updated COPPA Rule, effective April 22, 2026 (referenced via Progressive Church Media April 2026 analysis).</p></li></ul><h2 _hash="dEzaPy">Where We Go From Here</h2><p _hash="2zswWj">Choosing the right ChMS vendor has always required careful evaluation, but the rise of AI features has added a new layer of complexity, one that lives in contracts, subprocessor agreements, and data architecture decisions most church leaders have never had to think about before. The framework matters more than any single vendor choice: ask for the documentation, read the DPA, understand the subprocessor chain, and verify the deletion terms. The vendor that welcomes those questions is usually the vendor worth working with. For a wider read on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/ai-in-church-management-software-2026">the current landscape of AI features across church management platforms</a>, the pillar piece is the place to start.</p><p _hash="j3N4HC">The evidence here points in one direction: congregant data deserves the same stewardship standard we apply to everything else the church holds in trust. The practical question most ministry leaders face now is where to start, whether that is reviewing a current vendor contract, building a staff AI use policy, or evaluating a new platform. If any of those decisions are in front of your ministry, we would be glad to think it through with you. We offer no-pressure consultations where we listen first, then share what we have learned helping ministries navigate the same questions. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/consultation">Schedule a consultation</a>.</p><h1></h1>
+<p _hash="MDCN99"><strong>TL;DR:</strong> Most ChMS vendors do not clearly state whether congregant data trains their AI models, and the ones that do are the exception, not the rule. The gap between concern and governance is wide: 83% of church leaders worry about AI data privacy, yet only 5% have formal guidelines in place. Knowing what to ask, where to look in the contract, and what the answer must actually say is the practical starting point for every ministry evaluating AI features right now.</p><p _hash="snAJwV"><strong>What You Need to Know About ChMS AI and Congregant Data:</strong></p><ul _hash="nMRcCq"><li _hash="fPDI8W"><p _hash="RkhCkk">Some vendors explicitly prohibit training on congregant data; others do not, and the difference lives in the contract, not the demo.</p></li><li _hash="TXK7q8"><p _hash="jmGQtc">Once data enters an AI training pipeline, it <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.wardandsmith.com/article/the-risk-landscape-legal-operational-and-ethical-risks-in-ai-vendor-engagements">cannot be extracted</a>, making upfront contractual protection the only meaningful safeguard.</p></li><li _hash="Ubdv3F"><p _hash="tVj3jn">Enterprise-grade AI accounts differ from consumer-tier tools in one critical way: a binding Data Processing Agreement that prohibits training on your data.</p></li><li _hash="4eY5vo"><p _hash="UMU8Qf">Staff informally using free consumer AI tools with congregant data is the highest-risk scenario in most churches today.</p></li><li _hash="CaqDUf"><p _hash="5uGWKy">Four contract-level questions (training prohibition, subprocessor list, deletion terms, breach liability) are the minimum evaluation framework before any AI feature goes live.</p></li><li _hash="khn3ZY"><p _hash="X4w2SR">The Lausanne Movement's proposed AITAC certification framework offers a useful self-assessment benchmark today, even before the certification body is operational.</p></li></ul><p _hash="L9JF4n">Does your church management software vendor use congregant data to train its AI models? The direct answer is: it depends on the vendor, the product tier, and specific terms buried in your contract. According to <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">a March 2026 Barna Group survey of 1,306 church leaders</a>, 83% of church leaders are concerned about AI data privacy, and that concern is well-founded. Some ChMS vendors explicitly commit that congregant data never trains AI models. Others have made no such commitment. And a significant number of church staff use free consumer AI tools daily that carry no contractual protections at all. The framework below helps you tell the difference.</p><p _hash="SqKNAE">In this guide, we walk through what ChMS vendors' AI terms actually say about congregant data, why the distinction between consumer-grade and enterprise-grade AI matters more than most church leaders realize, and what the contract language must say before you click "enable" on any AI feature. We draw on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">a March 2026 Barna Group survey of 1,306 church leaders</a>, a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.wardandsmith.com/article/the-risk-landscape-legal-operational-and-ethical-risks-in-ai-vendor-engagements">June 2026 legal analysis from Ward and Smith, P.A.</a>, the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://lausanne.org/global-analysis/governing-ai-in-gods-house">Lausanne Movement's AITAC governance paper</a>, and a current snapshot of what the four largest ChMS AI platforms (Pushpay, ACST, Tithely, and Gloo) have actually committed to in their terms. If your ministry is in the middle of a renewal decision, evaluating a new platform, or simply trying to understand what your current vendor is doing with congregant data, this is where we start. For a wider view of the category, see <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/what-church-management-software-vendors-are-actually-doing-with-ai-in-2026/">our overview of what ChMS vendors are actually doing with AI in 2026</a>.</p><h2 _hash="wiOGVZ">Table of Contents</h2><ul _hash="Y02dnw"><li _hash="NrvW5d"><p _hash="gZXh0D">Why 83% of Church Leaders Are Right to Be Concerned</p></li><li _hash="jIqJIR"><p _hash="YQoVN1">The Risk Most Ministries Miss: When Congregant Data Enters an AI Training Pipeline</p></li><li _hash="iDZury"><p _hash="HyMYPM">What Enterprise-Grade AI Actually Means for Church Data</p></li><li _hash="lkZegn"><p _hash="0MXH9M">What the ChMS Vendor Landscape Looks Like Right Now</p></li><li _hash="geo9LX"><p _hash="uURHTF">Four Contract-Level Questions to Ask Before You Sign or Renew</p></li><li _hash="zHacro"><p _hash="IHcp8c">What AITAC Is, and Why It Will Change How We Evaluate Vendors</p></li><li _hash="5TKALG"><p _hash="lT8ivg">Frequently Asked Questions</p></li><li _hash="itjnUF"><p _hash="hA5v75">Key Takeaways</p></li><li _hash="tKxVJh"><p _hash="OJPTWE">Sources and References</p></li><li _hash="rWcEs8"><p _hash="YocGba">Where We Go From Here</p></li></ul><h2 _hash="1RYU9x">Why 83% of Church Leaders Are Right to Be Concerned</h2><p _hash="MXdl19">The concern is not abstract. The same <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">March 2026 Barna Group study</a> found that 83% of church leaders are concerned about AI data privacy, yet only 5% of churches have formal AI guidelines in place. That means most ministries are navigating this risk without a policy, a framework, or a clear understanding of what their vendor has actually committed to.</p><p _hash="J2pz6r">Barna also reports that 33% of churches are currently using AI in some part of ministry operations. Put those numbers together and the picture is clear: a substantial share of churches with no policy are already in active use. The concern is correct, the governance is not catching up, and the gap between the two is where exposure accumulates.</p><p _hash="kmqUHg">Church data is not generic business data. Prayer requests, giving records, attendance patterns, counseling notes, and family composition information occupy a uniquely sensitive category. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://lausanne.org/global-analysis/governing-ai-in-gods-house">Lausanne Movement's AITAC paper</a> makes the point sharply: data from prayer requests "could be interpreted by some AI systems as mental health indicators." Most church leaders never consider that when they enable AI features on their ChMS.</p><p _hash="hooe8Y">We say this calmly because the right response is a framework, not anxiety. The concern is legitimate. What we owe ourselves is a clear way to evaluate vendors, ask the right questions, and document the answers.</p><p _hash="6iFnMU"><strong>Key Point:</strong> 83% of church leaders are concerned about AI data privacy, but only 5% have formal guidelines. The gap between concern and governance is where the real risk lives.</p><h2 _hash="Rh8TTc">The Risk Most Ministries Miss: When Congregant Data Enters an AI Training Pipeline</h2><p _hash="mFErOY">When a vendor uses your ministry's data to train an AI model, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.wardandsmith.com/article/the-risk-landscape-legal-operational-and-ethical-risks-in-ai-vendor-engagements">Ward and Smith, P.A. notes in a June 2026 legal analysis</a>, "once data enters a model's training pipeline, it may be impossible to extract." The practical effect of training-based data use is permanent, not reversible upon request.</p><p _hash="nLBJjz">It helps to say plainly what AI model training means in this context. The vendor uses examples drawn from your congregants' data to improve or build the AI's responses. Once that happens, the data is distributed across millions of model parameters. There is no "delete congregant Jane Smith's data from the model" option. The information has been absorbed into the system's underlying weights.</p><p _hash="wb2LA1">The Lausanne paper frames the human side of this honestly: "Churches are applying AI without the necessary level of transparency or meaningful consent from their congregants." That framing is not accusatory toward vendors. It is honest about the gap that has opened between what congregants assume and what is actually happening with their data.</p><p _hash="ZSUq02">There is also a security dimension. Ward and Smith identify "model inversion and data extraction attacks" as a specific category of risk. In some cases, adversaries can reverse-engineer training data from model outputs. Congregant data embedded in a training set could theoretically be extracted by third parties later, even if the original vendor never intended that exposure.</p><p _hash="EobcKh">None of this appears on a vendor demo. It appears in the terms of service, the Data Processing Agreement, and the subprocessor list. Those three documents are where the question gets answered.</p><p _hash="hV4su5"><strong>Key Point:</strong> Once congregant data enters an AI training pipeline, it cannot be extracted. The protection must be contractual and in place before the data ever leaves your system.</p><h2 _hash="y2xRrH">What Enterprise-Grade AI Actually Means for Church Data</h2><p _hash="1R1KMC">The distinction between consumer-tier and enterprise-tier AI is not a marketing label. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.progressivechurchmedia.com/church-ai-policy/">Progressive Church Media's April 2026 analysis</a> documents that free consumer accounts (ChatGPT free tier, free Gemini, free Claude) may use inputs to train models, provide no Data Processing Agreements, and offer no organizational controls. Enterprise accounts contractually prohibit training on customer data, provide DPAs, offer organizational access controls, and often include audit logs.</p><p _hash="qRx7vL">Two tiers, in plain language:</p><p _hash="UXyPdW">The consumer tier covers free accounts on public AI platforms. There are no contract protections. There is no DPA. There are no organizational controls. Staff using these accounts with congregant data, even informally, is the highest-risk scenario in most churches today.</p><p _hash="MJaIKH">The enterprise tier covers paid business accounts (ChatGPT Team or Enterprise, Microsoft 365 Copilot with Enterprise Data Protection, Claude for Work). There is a contractual prohibition on training. A DPA is in place. Organizational controls and audit logs are part of the product.</p><p _hash="Nfa096">This distinction also reaches into the ChMS layer. A ChMS that has built its AI on top of consumer-tier API access, or that uses a foundation model without contractual training prohibitions at the foundation layer, may expose church data even when the ChMS vendor itself has not explicitly said "we train on your data." The contract chain matters, not just the headline assurance.</p><p _hash="F1JkJK">One regulatory point belongs here. The FTC's <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.progressivechurchmedia.com/church-ai-policy/">updated COPPA rule, effective April 22, 2026</a>, now treats biometric identifiers as personal information and requires separate verifiable parental consent for AI training uses, with penalties up to $53,088 per violation. Churches with children's ministry data in their ChMS should review their COPPA posture as part of any AI evaluation.</p><p _hash="ph8PzQ">The question to ask any ChMS vendor sounds technical, but it is the right one: "Is your AI integration governed by a Data Processing Agreement with your foundation model provider that prohibits training on our data?" If the answer is yes, ask to see the documentation. If the answer is unclear, that is itself useful information.</p><p _hash="gYIb2r"><strong>Key Point:</strong> "Enterprise-grade AI" means a contractual prohibition on training, a Data Processing Agreement, and organizational controls. It is not just a premium price point. Ask for the DPA documentation before enabling any AI feature.</p><h2 _hash="2myzzB">What the ChMS Vendor Landscape Looks Like Right Now</h2><p _hash="j13ug4">The major established ChMS vendors (Pushpay and ACST) have made explicit public commitments that congregant data is not used to train external AI models. Gloo's terms of service, as reported by MIT Technology Review in August 2025, reserve the right to ingest health and wellness information from prayer requests and wellness assessments. That is a meaningfully different data posture.</p><p _hash="gT86Rt">Here is what a current reading of stated terms and independent reporting shows. We present this as a factual snapshot, not as an endorsement or an attack on any vendor.</p><p _hash="jcdxlA"><strong>Pushpay.</strong> AI is built as a "query builder" that interacts with a structured semantic layer, not raw congregant information. The company has stated that member data "is strictly never utilized to train external (foundation) models." Sequential safety, accuracy, and transparency validation checks are part of the architecture, as reported by <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">Church Tech Today in June 2026</a>.</p><p _hash="Q1c3Bk"><strong>ACST (Realm and Ministry Platform).</strong> Ministry Platform AI, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">launched May 2026</a>, explicitly commits that "congregation data queried through Ministry Platform AI is retrieved live, securely returned, and not stored or used to train AI models." It is built on the open MCP standard. Role-based permissions, audit trails, and table-level controls are part of the launch architecture.</p><p _hash="2gBOXg"><strong>Tithely (TithelyAI).</strong> Public materials claim privacy and role-based access, but no formal DPA statement was located in publicly available sources as of June 2026. The product is still in pilot or early-launch phase. Church leaders evaluating Tithely should request a written DPA directly before enabling any AI feature.</p><p _hash="5h2qmZ"><strong>Gloo.</strong> <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.technologyreview.com/">MIT Technology Review reported in August 2025</a> that Gloo's terms of service reserve the right to ingest "health and wellness information" from prayer requests and wellness assessments for its data engine. Broad data enrichment partner exceptions are also present. Binding arbitration clauses limit congregant legal recourse. This is the clearest case in the current landscape where independent reporting and the terms of service diverge from what a church leader would typically expect.</p><p _hash="YszJZw"><strong>Consumer AI tools used informally by staff.</strong> The highest-risk category is not any named ChMS vendor. It is church staff using free consumer AI tools with congregant data informally, with no DPA, no controls, and no policy. This is where most churches are most exposed right now, and the issue is connected to <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/rolling-out-ai-in-church-management-software/">rolling out AI to church staff in a way that preserves trust</a>.</p><p _hash="KVSzDI">What this snapshot is for: it helps church leaders ask better questions, not make legal determinations. The terms shift, products evolve, and vendor documentation should always be re-read at the moment of decision.</p><p _hash="f6ljFf"><strong>Key Point:</strong> The vendor distinctions that matter most (training prohibitions, DPA terms, subprocessor disclosures) do not appear in demo calls. They live in the contract. Request the documentation before enabling AI features.</p><h2 _hash="Jq6Cso">Four Contract-Level Questions to Ask Before You Sign or Renew</h2><p _hash="eQ8z6n"><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.wardandsmith.com/article/the-risk-landscape-legal-operational-and-ethical-risks-in-ai-vendor-engagements">Ward and Smith, P.A.'s June 2026 legal analysis</a> identifies four non-negotiable contract provisions that every church should verify before signing any agreement with a ChMS or AI vendor: a prohibition on training on customer data, meaningful data breach indemnities, transparency on subprocessors, and deletion and portability rights at exit.</p><p _hash="ngYk8I">This builds on the surface-level evaluation in <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/chms-vendor-ai-questions-before-renewal/">the five questions we recommend church leaders raise at renewal</a>. That companion piece surfaces the questions. The four below are about what the answers must actually say.</p><p _hash="iXMAd2"><strong>1. "Does your contract prohibit using our congregant data to train AI models, and is that in writing?"</strong> This is the foundational question. A verbal reassurance from a sales representative is not the same as a contractual commitment. The answer must appear in the DPA or the Terms of Service, not just in a sales conversation. If a vendor cannot point to specific contract language, the commitment is not yet real.</p><p _hash="YkIwJy"><strong>2. "Who are your subprocessors, and what are their data training policies?"</strong> Ward and Smith call subprocessor transparency non-negotiable because a vendor can prohibit training on their own end while passing data to a foundation model provider whose terms allow training. Ask for the full subprocessor list and ask what DPA governs each subprocessor relationship. A vendor that cannot answer this question fully has not done the work yet themselves.</p><p _hash="KL7ULs"><strong>3. "What happens to our congregant data if we cancel?"</strong> <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">Church Tech Today's June 2026 piece</a> identifies this as a critical and frequently unanswered question. The answers you want: a defined deletion timeline (30 to 60 days is reasonable), written confirmation of deletion when it happens, and a data export mechanism so you can take your data with you. Ask for this before you need it, not after.</p><p _hash="R8G48X"><strong>4. "What is your liability if our data is breached through your AI systems?"</strong> Ward and Smith describe this as a "meaningful data breach indemnity." Many vendor contracts cap liability at a low fixed dollar amount, or at the fees you have paid, which may be a small fraction of the actual harm to congregants. Ask what the vendor is prepared to stand behind in the event of a real incident.</p><p _hash="9j2Fgh"><strong>Key Point:</strong> Ask for the Data Processing Agreement and the subprocessor list before signing or renewing. If a vendor cannot produce both, that is itself important information about their data governance maturity.</p><h2 _hash="b1Jcjy">What AITAC Is, and Why It Will Change How We Evaluate Vendors</h2><p _hash="TlVr3m">The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://lausanne.org/global-analysis/governing-ai-in-gods-house">Lausanne Movement's October 2025 AITAC governance paper</a> proposes an AI Trust and Accountability Consortium modeled on the ECFA: a certification body that would independently verify whether AI vendors serving churches meet a defined standard of data stewardship, transparency, and congregant consent.</p><p _hash="oTMvdh">A short history helps explain why this matters. Before ECFA, churches had no independent way to evaluate a ministry's financial stewardship. Donors had to rely on the ministry's own representations. ECFA created a third-party standard that turned trust into something verifiable. AITAC is attempting to solve the same problem for AI: turning vendor claims about data handling into something a church leader can verify without becoming a privacy lawyer.</p><p _hash="1EO3IY">AITAC is not yet operational, so it cannot be used as a vendor filter today. The framework it is building, however, is already useful: clear disclosures of data collection practices, plain-language explanations of data use, meaningful consent processes for congregants. Those criteria can be applied to any vendor documentation right now, even without a certification stamp.</p><p _hash="6o01Aj">The forward-looking guidance is straightforward. As AITAC moves from proposal to implementation, it will likely become the standard certification that discerning church leaders look for in ChMS AI vendors. This connects to broader institutional trends explored in <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/denominational-ai-statements-church-technology-decisions/">how denominations are beginning to address AI governance at the institutional level</a>. Asking your current vendor whether they intend to pursue AITAC certification is a reasonable part of any future renewal conversation.</p><p _hash="7TZrOi"><strong>Key Point:</strong> AITAC is not yet operational, but its proposed standard (independent verification of AI data stewardship, transparency, and consent practices) is the right benchmark. Church leaders can use the AITAC framework as a self-assessment tool for vendor evaluation today.</p><h2 _hash="rX5e6j">Frequently Asked Questions</h2><h3 _hash="ccyga1">Can prayer request data be used to train AI models?</h3><p _hash="49AH1O">It depends entirely on the vendor's terms and architecture. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://lausanne.org/global-analysis/governing-ai-in-gods-house">Lausanne Movement's AITAC paper</a> notes that prayer request data "could be interpreted by some AI systems as mental health indicators," a consequence most church leaders do not anticipate when enabling AI features. Whether a vendor uses that data for training is governed by their DPA and terms of service, not their marketing materials. Asking specifically about unstructured text fields (prayer notes, counseling notes) is important.</p><h3 _hash="h29Gvn">What is a Data Processing Agreement, and why does my church need one?</h3><p _hash="9BakvN">A Data Processing Agreement is a binding contract between your church (the data controller) and the vendor (the data processor) that specifies exactly how your congregants' data can be used, stored, and deleted. Without one, you have only the vendor's goodwill, not a legal obligation, governing data handling. Any ChMS vendor deploying AI features should be able to provide a DPA on request. If they cannot, treat that as a significant signal about their governance maturity.</p><h3 _hash="h5b617">Does my church need congregant consent before using AI features in our ChMS?</h3><p _hash="G4qNaE">For most standard AI-assisted features (giving summaries, attendance analytics, search), congregant consent is not typically required beyond your existing privacy policy disclosures, provided the vendor is not using that data for model training. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.progressivechurchmedia.com/church-ai-policy/">FTC's updated COPPA rule, effective April 22, 2026</a>, requires separate verifiable parental consent for any AI training on data from children under 13, with penalties up to $53,088 per violation. Churches with children's ministry data in their ChMS should review their COPPA compliance posture.</p><h3 _hash="FbluKp">What should we do if our ChMS vendor will not answer these questions directly?</h3><p _hash="vN7iBO">Ask in writing first, since email creates a record. If the vendor still does not provide a written response on data training and DPA availability, escalate to their data privacy officer or legal contact. If written confirmation is still not forthcoming, that response is itself meaningful information about the vendor's governance posture. At that point, we recommend involving your church's legal counsel before renewing or enabling AI features.</p><h3 _hash="KG6nB4">Is free ChatGPT safe to use for church communications?</h3><p _hash="dXfj9g">Free consumer-tier accounts on any major AI platform (ChatGPT free, Gemini free, Claude free) may use inputs to improve their models, provide no DPA, and offer no organizational controls. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.progressivechurchmedia.com/church-ai-policy/">Progressive Church Media's April 2026 analysis</a> is clear on this point. For drafting generic communications with no congregant data included, the risk is lower. For any task that involves member names, giving records, prayer requests, or other ministry data, consumer-tier AI tools are not appropriate. Enterprise accounts with DPAs are the minimum standard.</p><h3 _hash="y7SpDf">How do we know if our ChMS vendor's "no training" commitment is real?</h3><p _hash="GG9PNe">Currently, we do not have an independent way to verify these commitments, since vendor statements are self-reported. The Lausanne AITAC proposal is designed to solve this through independent certification, but it is not yet operational. The practical step today is to obtain the commitment in writing in the DPA, understand what subprocessors the vendor uses, and ask whether the vendor's foundation model provider's own terms prohibit training on enterprise customer data. Three layers of documentation are stronger than one.</p><h3 _hash="n3taAk">What happens to our data if we cancel our ChMS subscription?</h3><p _hash="D7LMrR">This depends entirely on the vendor's terms. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">Church Tech Today's June 2026 piece</a> identifies this as one of the most important and frequently unanswered questions in ChMS evaluation. We recommend asking for a specific deletion timeline (30 to 60 days is reasonable), a written confirmation of deletion when it occurs, and a data export mechanism before you need it, not after you have decided to cancel.</p><h3 _hash="m8lBqi">What is the biggest AI data risk most churches are not thinking about?</h3><p _hash="Y9Jhh0">Staff using free consumer AI tools with congregant data informally, without a policy, without a DPA, and without organizational awareness. Most of the attention in this conversation focuses on what ChMS vendors are doing, which is right and important. But the largest practical exposure for most churches right now is a staff member pasting a list of prayer requests or member contact information into a free AI tool to draft a communication. A simple, clear staff AI use policy is often the most impactful first step a church can take.</p><h2 _hash="GAl7Yi">Key Takeaways</h2><ul _hash="WJ2LVt"><li _hash="akxkyz"><p _hash="lBlRrZ"><strong>83% of church leaders are concerned about AI data privacy, but only 5% have formal AI guidelines.</strong> The governance gap is where real exposure lives, not just the technology itself.</p></li><li _hash="hdruXM"><p _hash="12cLiB"><strong>Once congregant data enters an AI training pipeline, it cannot be extracted.</strong> The protection must be contractual and verified before any AI feature is enabled.</p></li><li _hash="KMMDKX"><p _hash="JglRJz"><strong>Consumer-tier AI tools (free ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude) are not appropriate for church data.</strong> Enterprise accounts with Data Processing Agreements are the minimum standard for any use involving congregant information.</p></li><li _hash="1LANQA"><p _hash="AdIkDN"><strong>The major established ChMS vendors have made explicit "no external training" commitments, but not every vendor has.</strong> The differences live in contracts and terms of service, not in demo calls.</p></li><li _hash="rXfa8q"><p _hash="k9Bg1E"><strong>Four contract-level questions every church should ask:</strong> a no-training commitment in writing, a subprocessor list with their DPAs, data deletion terms at cancellation, and breach liability provisions.</p></li><li _hash="zFI7IU"><p _hash="p7qHkA"><strong>AITAC, an independent AI certification body for faith contexts, is in development</strong> and will provide a future standard for vendor evaluation. Its proposed framework is a useful self-assessment template today.</p></li><li _hash="sTVBcn"><p _hash="lzBVll"><strong>A clear staff AI use policy is often the most impactful immediate step.</strong> Informal use of consumer AI tools by staff is the highest-risk scenario for most churches right now.</p></li></ul><h2 _hash="75LNXw">Sources and References</h2><ul _hash="4q2YIi"><li _hash="RaT4OR"><p Ethical Risks in AI Vendor Engagements," June 2026. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.wardandsmith.com/article/the-risk-landscape-legal-operational-and-ethical-risks-in-ai-vendor-engagements">wardandsmith.com</a></p></li><li _hash="XS39Pq"><p _hash="U8l6xX">Lausanne AI, Bringing Secure, Ministry-Aware AI to Churches," May 7, 2026. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" April 2026 analysis).</p></li></ul><h2 _hash="dEzaPy">Where We Go From Here</h2><p _hash="2zswWj">Choosing the right ChMS vendor has always required careful evaluation, but the rise of AI features has added a new layer of complexity, one that lives in contracts, subprocessor agreements, and data architecture decisions most church leaders have never had to think about before. The framework matters more than any single vendor choice: ask for the documentation, read the DPA, understand the subprocessor chain, and verify the deletion terms. The vendor that welcomes those questions is usually the vendor worth working with. For a wider read on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/what-church-management-software-vendors-are-actually-doing-with-ai-in-2026/">the current landscape of AI features across church management platforms</a>, the pillar piece is the place to start.</p><p _hash="j3N4HC">The evidence here points in one direction: congregant data deserves the same stewardship standard we apply to everything else the church holds in trust. The practical question most ministry leaders face now is where to start, whether that is reviewing a current vendor contract, building a staff AI use policy, or evaluating a new platform. If any of those decisions are in front of your ministry, we would be glad to think it through with you. We offer no-pressure consultations where we listen first, then share what we have learned helping ministries navigate the same questions. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/contact/">Schedule a consultation</a>.</p>

-<p><strong>TL;DR:</strong> Three of the largest church management software vendors have shipped or announced AI features inside their ChMS in the last sixty days, and the way each handles congregational data is materially different. Before signing a renewal, we recommend five questions that every church leader should get direct, written answers to. The answers determine whether AI inside your ChMS becomes a stewardship asset or a stewardship liability.</p><p><strong>Core Insights:</strong></p><ul><li><p>Vendor AI roadmaps are now part of the renewal cycle, not a separate decision, and most ChMS contracts auto-enable new AI features without granular opt-out.</p></li><li><p>83% of church leaders are very or somewhat concerned about AI data privacy, yet only 5% of churches have a formal AI policy in place (<a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">Barna/Pushpay State of Church Tech 2026</a>).</p></li><li><p>Pushpay, ACST/MinistryPlatform, and Subsplash each represent a distinct data-governance posture, not a feature comparison.</p></li><li><p>Every "we don't train models on your data" claim we have read in 2026 comes from vendor marketing or terms of service, never from an independent third-party audit.</p></li><li><p>The most important question is not what the AI does today, it is what the vendor's roadmap will auto-enable at the next renewal and whether feature-by-feature opt-out is contractually available.</p></li><li><p>64% of church leaders believe their church should have an established AI use policy. Most of that policy work happens at the renewal table, where the leverage actually exists.</p></li></ul><p>Five questions belong on every church leader's desk before signing a church management software renewal in 2026: will our congregational data ever be used to train AI models, what happens to that data if we cancel, who on our staff sees what when the AI generates an answer, is the AI's reasoning auditable, and which roadmap features will be auto-enabled at our next renewal. We walk through each below, grounded in the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">most current vendor disclosures</a> and independent ministry research.</p><h2>Why Vendor AI Decisions Are Now Renewal Decisions</h2><p>AI features inside church management software are no longer optional add-ons that ministries evaluate separately, they are being shipped directly into the products churches already pay for, on the renewal cycle churches already signed. That changes what the renewal conversation needs to cover, and it maps onto <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/chms-vendors-ai-2026">the broader picture of what ChMS vendors are actually doing with AI in 2026</a>.</p><p>Two recent vendor announcements illustrate the pace. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/building-for-the-future/">Pushpay's largest one-time investment ever in its Staq family</a>, unveiled on June 2, 2026, introduced an AI involvement summary feature inside the ChMS and reshaped how giving, ChMS, and engagement data flow together. A month earlier, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">ACST launched MinistryPlatform AI</a> as a layered integration over the existing ChMS, citing a thirty-minute setup that uses existing API credentials. The point is not the features themselves. The point is that these capabilities arrived on the vendor's release calendar, and the church's renewal contract is what governs them.</p><p>That is the question framework leaders are now wrestling with, and it is also the moment where contract leverage actually exists. Once the renewal is signed, the next opportunity to renegotiate scope is twelve months away. AI features ship in the meantime.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> AI features inside the ChMS arrive on the vendor's renewal cycle, not on the church's evaluation cycle. The renewal table is where the leverage exists, and the questions below are how we use it.</p></blockquote><h2>Question One: Will Our Congregational Data Ever Be Used to Train AI Models?</h2><p>The first question on every renewal desk is whether the vendor or any of their partners will use member records, giving history, attendance, pastoral notes, or counseling data to train AI models. We look for a written contractual answer, not a marketing-page assurance.</p><p>The current vendor disclosures are close on language and important to read carefully. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">ACST states that data queried through MinistryPlatform AI is retrieved live, securely returned, and not stored or used to train AI models</a>. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">Pushpay describes member data as staying within their secure partner ecosystem and not used to train external AI foundation models</a>. Both statements answer the headline question. Neither statement answers the question of what happens inside the partner ecosystem, with which named subprocessors, under which exceptions.</p><p>That distinction matters because <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">83% of church leaders are very or somewhat concerned about AI data privacy</a> according to the 2026 Barna and Pushpay State of Church Tech research. The concern is real, the contractual specificity is what addresses it. We ask for the language from the marketing page to be mirrored in the master services agreement, with named partners and named exceptions documented.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Vendor marketing language about model training is consistent across the category. What matters is whether the same language appears in the contract we sign, in writing, with named exceptions and named partners.</p></blockquote><h2>Question Two: What Happens to Our Data If We Cancel?</h2><p>Exit portability matters more after a year of AI features have been generating derived data, summaries, pastoral-care signals, and engagement scores. We ask what the vendor returns, what they retain, and what they delete, on what timeline, in what format. These are <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">the four buyer questions the industry is now coalescing around</a>, and exit terms are central to all four.</p><p>The complication is that most ChMS exit clauses were written before AI features shipped. A standard data export covers member records, giving history, group rosters, and the categories of data the church entered. It does not automatically cover the AI-generated layer: the summaries the assistant produced, the engagement scores it inferred, the pastoral-care flags it surfaced. Those derived artifacts are arguably the church's data and arguably the vendor's product. The contract is what resolves the ambiguity.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">Vendor consolidation is creating new derived-data layers</a> faster than exit clauses are being updated. The June 8 Pushpay and Nurture single point of entry release is one example: a new path that assigns people directly to Nurture Process Queues from inside the ChMS, which generates new categorical data the original 2024 contract probably did not anticipate.</p><p>This is also where <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/how-to-evaluate-chms-platforms">the broader evaluation framework we use</a> comes in. Exit terms are one input among several, but they are the one most often left underspecified at signing.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Exit terms written before AI features shipped do not automatically cover derived AI outputs. We ask for the cancellation clause to name AI-generated artifacts explicitly.</p></blockquote><h2>Question Three: Who on Our Staff Sees What When the AI Generates an Answer?</h2><p>AI assistants inside the ChMS need to respect the same access permissions a human staff member would, or they create a quiet permissions bypass. We look for role-based controls, audit trails, and table-level restrictions, not a single global permission to "use the AI."</p><p>This is one area where the public vendor disclosures are unusually specific. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">ACST's stated role-based permissions, audit trails, table-level controls, and data response inspection</a> describe an architecture in which AI queries inherit the permissions of the staff member running them. That is the correct posture. It also needs to be verified against the actual configuration, because the existence of role-based controls does not guarantee they are configured to the church's standard out of the box.</p><p>The denominational conversation reinforces the same principle. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://news.lifeway.com/2026/06/08/lifeway-sbc-leaders-respond-to-research-findings-with-ai-in-christian-ministry-statement/">The Brentwood Statement on AI in Christian Ministry calls for human oversight in pastoral applications of AI</a>, which in practice means clear access controls and clear audit trails for any AI-generated output that touches counseling, member care, or pastoral notes.</p><p>The vendor side of the work pairs with <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/ai-policy-for-church-staff">the staff-side policy work that complements the vendor-side controls</a>. The two halves are not interchangeable. Vendor controls govern the technology, internal policy governs the people, and both are required.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> A ChMS AI assistant that ignores existing permission structures is a governance problem, not a productivity gain. Role-based access for humans must extend to role-based access for AI queries.</p></blockquote><h2>Question Four: Is the AI's Reasoning Auditable, and Can We Document It?</h2><p>When the AI surfaces a pastoral-care recommendation, a giving pattern flag, or an attendance trend, we need to see how it got there. Auditable reasoning chains and documented validation steps are the difference between a tool we can defend at an elder meeting and a tool we cannot.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">Pushpay's stated safety, accuracy, and transparency sequential check</a> is one example of a vendor articulating the validation sequence in public. The sequence reads in order: safety first, then accuracy, then transparency. That ordering is defensible, and it is also worth pressing the vendor on the specifics of how each check is implemented and surfaced to the staff member receiving the AI output.</p><p>The congregational trust dimension is the other side of the same conversation. Lifeway Research data published alongside the Brentwood Statement indicates that <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://news.lifeway.com/2026/06/08/lifeway-sbc-leaders-respond-to-research-findings-with-ai-in-christian-ministry-statement/">61% of churchgoers are concerned about AI's influence on Christianity</a>. That concern is not abstract. It shows up in the elder meeting where the staff is asked how the AI reached its conclusion. Auditable reasoning chains are how that question gets answered honestly.</p><p>The independent observation matters here too. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://ministrywatch.com/the-new-church-tech-divide-is-missional-not-digital/">Vendor-funded research and vendor-built tooling demand independent scrutiny</a>, and the absence of a third-party audit regime in this category means the church's own diligence is the only audit.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Vendors that can show their work earn trust. Vendors that cannot show their work earn polite distance until they can.</p></blockquote><h2>Question Five: What's in Your AI Roadmap That Will Be Auto-Enabled at Our Next Renewal?</h2><p>Most churches discover new AI features the morning they appear in the product, not the moment the contract is signed. We ask for the roadmap in writing and for feature-by-feature opt-out, not a binary AI toggle.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/building-for-the-future/">The explicit AI involvement summary feature on the Pushpay roadmap</a> is one example of a feature that arrives via release notes rather than via a separate purchasing decision. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">The roadmap path that will assign people directly to Nurture Process Queues from inside the ChMS</a> is another. Both are reasonable product moves. Both are also material changes to how data flows inside the church's stack, and both should be evaluated against the church's own AI policy rather than auto-enabled by default.</p><p>The speed of arrival is a feature of the category. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">ACST's stated thirty-minute setup using existing API credentials</a> is one indicator of how quickly new AI capabilities can move from announcement to production inside a church's ChMS. The renewal contract is what governs that speed, or fails to.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The renewal we sign in 2026 is also the renewal that governs the AI features shipped in 2027. Granular opt-out, written in, is the only durable answer.</p></blockquote><h2>The Three Vendor Postures We Are Watching</h2><p>Pushpay, ACST/MinistryPlatform, and Subsplash represent three different data-governance choices, not three flavors of the same product. We walk through how each one currently positions on the five questions above.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/building-for-the-future/">Pushpay's Staq family</a> brings ChMS, giving, and Nurture engagement under a centralized data flow, with AI features added directly to the renewal cycle. The acquisition-driven product breadth is part of the proposition, and it is also why the exit and roadmap questions carry extra weight: more data categories are flowing through one vendor's infrastructure than before.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">ACST and MinistryPlatform AI</a> is a layered integration over the existing ChMS, built on the open Model Context Protocol. ACST sits under the Vanco parent and serves approximately 50,000 churches. Live retrieval, role-based permissions, and audit trails are part of the stated architecture. The open protocol is the most distinctive element, because it makes the data-query path inspectable rather than proprietary.</p><p>Subsplash, via the Pulpit AI integration, focuses on sermon-content AI rather than ChMS-data AI. The data path is different, the buyer questions are different, and we mention it briefly here because this post is focused on congregational data inside the ChMS, not on sermon and content workflows.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://ministrywatch.com/the-new-church-tech-divide-is-missional-not-digital/">The independent observation that vendor product breadth is reshaping the church-tech decision</a> applies to all three postures. The choice between them is a data-governance choice, and it pairs with <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/ai-policy-for-church-staff">the internal policy work that pairs with whichever vendor posture we choose</a>.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> We are not picking a feature set, we are picking a data-governance posture. The three postures available in 2026 are meaningfully different.</p></blockquote><h2>What We Look for in the Written Answer, Not the Sales Call</h2><p>Every claim that matters belongs in the contract, the data processing addendum, or the security documentation. Sales-call assurances are useful for context, they are not enforceable.</p><p>Five places we push for written specificity:</p><ol><li><p>The data-training language belongs in the master services agreement, not the marketing page. The wording should name the partners, the exceptions, and the auditability of the claim.</p></li><li><p>The exit-portability clause should name AI-generated artifacts explicitly, including derived summaries, scores, and flags, with timelines for return and deletion.</p></li><li><p>The audit-trail and permissions architecture belongs in the security overview document, not described verbally on the sales call. Role-based controls and table-level restrictions should be configurable and inspectable.</p></li><li><p>The roadmap opt-out clause should be written before the next renewal arrives, with feature-by-feature opt-in for new AI capabilities as the default, not a binary toggle.</p></li><li><p>The gap worth naming: as of mid-2026, no independent third-party audit verifies vendor data-handling claims in this category. That makes contractual specificity the church's only durable protection.</p></li></ol><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/ai-ethics-and-the-churchs-most-sensitive-data/">The question set the industry is converging on</a> maps closely to the five above. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">The policy gap between concern and action across the sector</a> is what makes the renewal conversation worth the time. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://ministrywatch.com/the-new-church-tech-divide-is-missional-not-digital/">The independent posture we recommend leaders bring to every vendor claim</a> is to treat the contract as the source of truth, and the sales conversation as the helpful context around it.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> What is in the contract is what we have. What is on the sales call is what we hope for. We negotiate from the first, not the second.</p></blockquote><h2>Frequently Asked Questions</h2><h3>Does my ChMS vendor already use my church's data to train AI?</h3><p>The honest answer is that it depends on the vendor and on the contract you signed. Current vendor disclosures from Pushpay and ACST state that congregational data is not used to train models, but the specifics belong in the master services agreement, not in a marketing page. We recommend asking for the language in writing.</p><h3>What is the most important AI question to ask at a ChMS renewal?</h3><p>The roadmap opt-out question carries the most leverage. AI features arrive automatically on the vendor's release cycle, and most contracts treat AI as a single toggle rather than a feature-by-feature opt-in. The renewal is the moment to negotiate granular control, because the next opportunity is twelve months away.</p><h3>How does the Model Context Protocol matter for church management software?</h3><p>ACST's MinistryPlatform AI is built on the open Model Context Protocol, which standardizes how AI assistants query underlying data. For a church leader, the practical effect is that data is retrieved live rather than pre-indexed into a separate AI store, and the protocol is auditable. We expect more ChMS vendors to adopt similar architectures in 2026.</p><h3>Are there independent audits of how ChMS vendors handle AI data?</h3><p>Not yet. As of mid-2026, no independent third-party audit, certification body, or denominational watchdog has published a comparative review of how the major ChMS vendors handle congregational data inside their AI features. Vendor disclosures are the only public source. That makes contractual specificity the church's primary protection.</p><h3>What should a small church without a tech team prioritize in AI vendor evaluation?</h3><p>Two things. First, get the data-training and cancellation language in writing, even if the rest of the contract feels boilerplate. Second, choose a vendor whose default settings are conservative rather than maximalist, so a small staff is not configuring AI controls they do not have the bandwidth to monitor. Default-off beats default-on for under-resourced teams.</p><h3>How often should we revisit our ChMS AI terms?</h3><p>We revisit at every renewal cycle, which for most ChMS contracts is annually. We also revisit any time the vendor announces a material AI feature or completes an acquisition that changes the data-flow path, regardless of the renewal calendar. The Pushpay and Nurture integration shipped in June 2026 is a recent example of a mid-cycle change worth a fresh look.</p><h3>Does the Brentwood Statement require us to change vendors?</h3><p>No. The Brentwood Statement is a denominational framework calling for human oversight and pastoral guardrails in AI use. It does not name vendors or prescribe vendor changes. It does inform the questions leaders bring to the renewal table, particularly around auditability, human-in-the-loop oversight, and the pastoral applications the AI is allowed to touch.</p><h2>Key Takeaways</h2><ul><li><p><strong>AI inside the ChMS is now a renewal decision, not a separate evaluation.</strong> Vendor roadmaps ship features automatically, and the renewal contract is where leverage lives.</p></li><li><p><strong>Five questions cover the durable ground:</strong> data training, exit portability, staff access permissions, reasoning auditability, and roadmap opt-out.</p></li><li><p><strong>Vendor marketing language is consistent across the category.</strong> Contractual language is not. We negotiate the contract.</p></li><li><p><strong>No independent third-party audit currently verifies vendor data-handling claims</strong> for ChMS AI, which makes written specificity the church's primary protection.</p></li><li><p><strong>Three vendor postures are now distinct:</strong> Pushpay's centralized Staq family, ACST/MinistryPlatform's open-protocol layered integration, and Subsplash's sermon-content focus. Each represents a different data-governance choice.</p></li><li><p><strong>Most churches feel the AI policy gap.</strong> Sixty-four percent of leaders believe a formal AI policy matters, only five percent have one, and most of that policy work happens at the renewal table.</p></li></ul><h2>Where We Go From Here</h2><p>The five questions above are the durable ground for evaluating AI inside any church management software, and they map onto <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/chms-vendors-ai-2026">the vendor landscape we mapped last Friday</a>.</p><p>The next decision most leaders face is whether to raise these questions at the current renewal cycle or wait, and the answer usually depends on how soon the contract auto-renews and what AI features are already in flight.</p><p>If your ministry is working through a ChMS renewal with new AI features on the table, we would be glad to think it through with you. We offer no-pressure consultations where we listen first, then share what we have learned helping ministries navigate the same questions. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/consultation">Schedule a consultation</a>.</p><h2>Sources and References</h2><ol><li><p>ChurchTechToday, "AI Ethics And The Church's Most Sensitive Data," June "Pushpay Introduces a Streamlined Customer Experience Between Pushpay and Nurture.io Platforms," June 8, 2026. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow"
