-<p><strong>TL;DR:</strong> Vendor AI features are no longer something a church is choosing whether to adopt. They are switching on inside the church management system the church already pays for. We have watched the staff-trust failures cluster around the same five missing steps, so we wrote them out as a sequence any church can run, regardless of which ChMS sits underneath.</p><p><strong>Core Insights:</strong></p><ul><li><p>The first real implementation data is in: <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">Pushpay logged 15,185 AI search prompts across 331 accounts during beta</a>, and the win was access, not novelty.</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/the-2026-state-of-ai-in-the-church-93-of-pastors-are-in-now-what/">Only nine percent of churches have a formal AI policy</a>, even though 93.5 percent of leaders are now engaging with AI in some form.</p></li><li><p>The right champion is not the senior pastor; it is the staff member who will use the tool most.</p></li><li><p>A thirty-day pilot with one feature, one team, and a written success metric prevents permanent "we are trying it" limbo.</p></li><li><p>Short, role-relevant training beats generic AI workshops. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/the-2026-state-of-ai-in-the-church-93-of-pastors-are-in-now-what/">Sixty-one percent of leaders say they want tutorials for specific tools, not theory</a>.</p></li></ul><p>A church rolls out AI inside its existing ChMS in five stages, in this order: inventory what staff are already using, write a short shared policy, name a single champion who is not the senior pastor, pilot one feature with one team for thirty days against a written success metric, then expand with short role-relevant trainings. The sequence works because the technical setup is no longer the hard part. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">Pushpay's AI People Search beta logged 15,185 prompts across 331 accounts with five times more staff using complex searches</a>, and <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">ACST's Ministry Platform AI sets up in under thirty minutes</a>. The trust work belongs to the church, not the vendor.</p><h2>Table of Contents</h2><ul><li><p>Why Vendor AI Is Now an Implementation Question, Not an Adoption Question</p></li><li><p>Stage One: Take an Honest Inventory Before You Turn Anything On</p></li><li><p>Stage Two: Write the Thirty-Minute Policy Before the Pilot, Not After</p></li><li><p>Stage Three: Pick One Champion Who Is Not the Senior Pastor</p></li><li><p>Stage Four: Pilot One Feature With One Team for Thirty Days</p></li><li><p>Stage Five: Expand With Short Role-Relevant Trainings, Not Feature Walkthroughs</p></li><li><p>Frequently Asked Questions</p></li><li><p>Key Takeaways</p></li><li><p>Sources and References</p></li><li><p>Where We Go From Here</p></li></ul><h2>Why Vendor AI Is Now an Implementation Question, Not an Adoption Question</h2><p>Through the first half of 2026, AI moved from a thing a church chooses to adopt into a thing that switches on inside the church management system the church already uses. The question on the table is no longer whether to bring AI in. It is how to roll it out without breaking trust with the staff who have to use it.</p><p>The vendor landscape made the shift hard to miss. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">Pushpay opened AI People Search to all ChMS customers in April 2026</a> after a beta that ran across 331 accounts. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">ACST announced Ministry Platform AI in May with a Pioneer Program for the first fifty churches</a>. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.ministrybrands.com/podcasts/ep-173-kira-brown-how-to-use-ai-in-ministry-without-losing-the-human-touch">Ministry Brands shipped its Equip AI tool on June 10, 2026</a>. We are tracking similar movement across Planning Center and Subsplash. For a fuller view, our pillar on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/chms-vendors-ai-2026">what church management software vendors are actually doing with AI in 2026</a> maps the vendor-side picture in depth.</p><p>We will note up front that several of the numbers throughout this piece come from vendor-published research. Pushpay, ACST, and Ministry Brands have an interest in the story landing well. We cite them because the implementation detail they publish is the best available right now, and we read it accordingly.</p><p>The technical setup is no longer the hard part. ACST's Ministry Platform AI configures in under thirty minutes through standard API credentials. Pushpay's People Search activates with a switch inside the existing ChMS. The work that decides whether the rollout holds, or breaks, is the staff conversation around it. That work belongs to the church.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The vendor handles the switch. The church handles the trust. AI rollout in a ChMS is now an implementation problem, not an adoption decision.</p></blockquote><h2>Stage One: Take an Honest Inventory Before You Turn Anything On</h2><p>Before activating any vendor AI feature, find out what AI tools the staff are already using on their own. The inventory is the foundation; without it, a new vendor feature gets layered on top of an unmapped landscape, and the staff who have been quietly using ChatGPT for their newsletter draft go further underground.</p><p>The gap is real. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/church-ai-policy-planning/">Pushpay's research with Barna found that around sixty percent of pastors use AI personally each month, while only five percent of churches have a formal policy in place</a>. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">The same Barna and Pushpay collaboration reported that sixty-four percent of leaders say a policy is important, but only that same five percent actually have one</a>. A majority of staff are using AI somewhere. Almost no one has mapped it.</p><p>The inventory conversation works best as a curious one, not an audit. The four questions we ask:</p><ol><li><p>Which AI tools are you already using in your work, on any device?</p></li><li><p>What tasks are you using them for, week to week?</p></li><li><p>What information do those tools see when you use them?</p></li><li><p>What would make your current AI use easier or safer to do openly?</p></li></ol><p>The point of the inventory is not enforcement. It is the input the next stage needs. The policy cannot account for what the team is doing if the team's actual practice is invisible. As Pushpay frames it, the answers to those questions will often surprise leadership, and that surprise is the value of asking.</p><p>There is a sequencing reason to inventory before the vendor switch. Turning on Pushpay AI People Search or ACST Ministry Platform AI without knowing the existing AI footprint sets up the exact trust failure the rollout is supposed to prevent. The staff member who has been using a personal ChatGPT workflow now has to choose between continuing in the shadow or admitting it after the fact. Neither path builds trust.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Inventory first. The staff are already using AI somewhere. A vendor feature switched on without that map will compete with whatever is already in motion.</p></blockquote><h2>Stage Two: Write the Thirty-Minute Policy Before the Pilot, Not After</h2><p>The policy does not need to be a twelve-page document. It needs to exist. A thirty-minute staff conversation that produces a shared understanding of where AI is appropriate, where it is not, and who is responsible for what is enough to start. Light is acceptable; absent is not.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/church-ai-policy-planning/">Pushpay's writing on church AI policy makes the same point: a thirty-minute staff conversation that produces shared understanding counts as a policy</a>. The bar is shared clarity, not legal documentation. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/the-2026-state-of-ai-in-the-church-93-of-pastors-are-in-now-what/">ChurchTechToday's 2026 State of AI in the Church report found that only nine percent of churches currently have a formal AI policy, while forty-seven percent of leaders say they want ethical guidance from a trusted source</a>. The appetite is there. The document is not.</p><p>There are four decisions the thirty minutes needs to produce:</p><ol><li><p>What data is allowed to touch which AI tools. Congregational records, giving data, pastoral conversations, and counseling notes sit at different sensitivity levels and need different rules.</p></li><li><p>What gets disclosed to the congregation. If AI helped draft a sermon outline, a newsletter, or a follow-up message, is that named?</p></li><li><p>Who has final sign-off on AI-generated communication before it goes out.</p></li><li><p>What gets retained, and what gets deleted, after an AI tool produces output.</p></li></ol><p>Write the answers down, even informally. A shared doc that the team can point to is the asset.</p><p>Policy before pilot matters because retroactive rules feel like a takeaway, not a guardrail. Once a feature is live and a staff member is using it daily, a new rule introduced afterward reads as a correction of that person. Setting the rule before the switch reads as a shared standard. The difference is enormous in a small staff culture.</p><p>Vendor-side guarantees reduce technical risk but do not write the policy for the church. ACST's role-based permissions, audit trails, and assurance that data is not used to train external models address the infrastructure layer. The policy layer, what the team decides to do with the tool, is still on the church to set. For a deeper walk-through, our piece on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/church-ai-policy">the AI policy questions every church staff needs to answer</a> covers the full set we work through with ministries.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> A short, written, shared policy beats a comprehensive policy that never gets written. The policy is a precondition for the pilot, not its output.</p></blockquote><h2>Stage Three: Pick One Champion Who Is Not the Senior Pastor</h2><p>The AI champion should be the staff member who will use the new feature most, not the senior pastor. For ChMS AI features like people search, giving lookups, or attendance trends, that means a connections director, a finance lead, a children's pastor, or a small-groups coordinator. The senior pastor sponsors the rollout; someone closer to the work runs it.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">Pushpay's beta data on AI People Search shows the user pattern clearly</a>. The six query categories that dominated the beta were driven by connections staff, children's ministry leads, finance directors, small-groups coordinators, attendance trackers, and volunteer coordinators. Senior leadership rarely appeared in the heavy-use cohort. The features were built for the people doing the daily lookup work, and they used them.</p><p>The senior pastor is often the wrong champion because the senior pastor is often the lightest user. The champion needs daily use to surface real friction, because friction is what the pilot review needs to evaluate. A leader who tries the tool twice a month will not have the data to make the call at day thirty.</p><p>The champion's job has three parts:</p><ol><li><p>Be the first daily user of the new feature.</p></li><li><p>Collect what is working and what is not, in plain notes.</p></li><li><p>Run the thirty-day pilot review meeting.</p></li></ol><p>The profile we look for: someone trusted by the team, technically curious enough to try things, honest enough to say when something does not work. Title does not matter. Trust does.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.ministrybrands.com/podcasts/ep-173-kira-brown-how-to-use-ai-in-ministry-without-losing-the-human-touch">Ministry Brands' conversation with Kira Brown on using AI in ministry without losing the human touch</a> lands a related point: the people running the tool day to day are the ones who translate vendor framing into specific staff conversations. The champion is the translator.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The champion is the heaviest user, not the most senior leader. Sponsorship and championship are different jobs.</p></blockquote><h2>Stage Four: Pilot One Feature With One Team for Thirty Days</h2><p>A pilot has one feature, one team, a thirty-day window, and a success metric written down before it starts. The old workflow keeps running in parallel during the pilot. The success metric is the question the pilot answers, and it should be specific enough that a yes or no answer is possible at day thirty.</p><p>Pick the feature most aligned with current staff pain. From Pushpay's six beta query categories, the highest-leverage starters are usually connections lookups or giving questions. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">The beta data showed time per complex filter dropping from minutes to about five seconds, with five times more unique users running complex searches than before</a>. The story behind the numbers is access expansion: the staff who could not previously run a complex query suddenly could. That is the kind of outcome a pilot is built to confirm or rule out in a single church's context. Before locking the pilot scope, the questions in our piece on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/chms-vendor-ai-questions">what to ask your ChMS vendor about AI before you renew</a> help clarify what the feature will and will not do.</p><p>One team only. Pulling three teams into a pilot at once dilutes the signal and triples the change-management load. The point of a pilot is a clean read on one variable.</p><p>Define the success metric in writing before turning the feature on. Examples that work:</p><ul><li><p>Our connections director can produce next-step lists for new-attendee follow-up in under ten minutes.</p></li><li><p>Our finance lead can answer board questions about giving without exporting to a spreadsheet.</p></li><li><p>Our children's ministry coordinator can pull current allergy and check-in notes for a Sunday in a single query.</p></li></ul><p>Each of these has a yes or no answer at day thirty. "We are using the new feature" does not, because it measures activity, not value.</p><p>Parallel running is part of the design. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://resources.concordiatechnology.org/resource-center/how-to-train-staff-on-new-church-management-software">Concordia Technology's guidance on training staff through a system transition recommends running old and new workflows side by side, and staying available for ongoing questions for months rather than relying on a single training session</a>. We apply the same principle to AI feature rollouts. The team needs the fallback, and the comparison itself is useful pilot data.</p><p>Where vendors offer structured pilot support, take it. ACST's Pioneer Program for the first fifty churches on Ministry Platform AI offers vendor-supported onboarding for cohort-style pilots, which can shorten the time to a clean review. Whether or not the vendor offers a program, the church's own thirty-day discipline is what produces the answer.</p><p>At day thirty, the champion answers the success metric, yes or no. The team votes on one of three outcomes: expand, extend the pilot by another thirty days with a refined metric, or stop. No fourth option. Drift into permanent trial is the failure mode this stage is designed to prevent.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> One feature, one team, thirty days, one written metric. The pilot's job is to answer a specific question, not to demonstrate enthusiasm.</p></blockquote><h2>Stage Five: Expand With Short Role-Relevant Trainings, Not Feature Walkthroughs</h2><p>Expansion happens only after the pilot review answers yes on its success metric. The training that follows is short, role-relevant, and focused on the three to five things each staff member will actually do with the tool. Generic feature walkthroughs do not stick; specific use cases do.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/the-2026-state-of-ai-in-the-church-93-of-pastors-are-in-now-what/">ChurchTechToday's 2026 report found that sixty-one percent of church leaders want tutorials for specific tools and fifty-nine percent want ministry-specific use cases</a>. They are not asking for AI theory. They are asking for the exact phrasing that gets the connections director the list of new attendees who missed last Sunday.</p><p>The training format we recommend: thirty minutes per role, focused on the three to five queries that staff member runs most often. The session is built around the actual tasks already on that person's calendar. A children's ministry coordinator's session is different from a finance lead's session, and that difference is the point.</p><p>Documentation matters as much as the live session. A one-page reference per role, in plain language, with the exact phrasings that worked during the pilot, gives the team something to return to. Without it, the training fades by the next staff meeting.</p><p>Ongoing support is the part most rollouts underestimate. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://resources.concordiatechnology.org/resource-center/how-to-train-staff-on-new-church-management-software">Concordia Technology's research on staff training through a software change makes clear that questions surface for months, not weeks, as staff hit tasks that only come up periodically</a>. The annual stewardship campaign, the back-to-school children's check-in surge, the year-end giving close, each one is a moment a new question shows up. Build the expectation that the champion stays available for those moments. The lessons here overlap meaningfully with what we wrote in <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/chms-migration-guide">what church management software migration actually involves</a>, because the human side of a feature rollout and a full migration share the same gravity.</p><p>What expansion should not look like: a single all-staff lunch-and-learn where the AI feature is demoed once and never referenced again. That format checks a box and produces no behavior change.</p><p>The vendor ships the feature. The rollout sequence above is how a church actually lands it. The Friday pillar on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/chms-vendors-ai-2026">the vendor-by-vendor AI rollout map</a> covers the supply side; the five stages here are the demand side, the implementation work that turns a feature switch into a tool the team trusts.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Short, role-relevant training sticks. Generic feature walkthroughs fade by the next staff meeting.</p></blockquote><h2>Frequently Asked Questions</h2><h3>How long should a church AI pilot run inside a ChMS?</h3><p>Thirty days is the right default for a single feature with a single team. Shorter than that and the staff have not had time to hit the tasks that only show up every few weeks. Longer than that and the pilot drifts into permanent "we are trying it" status with no decision point. The thirty-day mark is the review meeting, not the end of use.</p><h3>Who should own AI rollout in a church?</h3><p>The senior pastor sponsors it. A staff champion who will use the tool most often runs it. For ChMS AI features, that usually means a connections director, a finance lead, a children's pastor, or a small-groups coordinator. Sponsorship and championship are distinct jobs, and conflating them is one of the most common reasons a rollout stalls inside the first sixty days.</p><h3>Do we need a formal AI policy before turning on vendor AI features?</h3><p>Yes, in the lightest possible form. A thirty-minute staff conversation that produces a shared understanding of what data goes where, what gets disclosed, and who has final sign-off is a policy. We do not recommend waiting on a comprehensive document. We do recommend never running the pilot without something written down, because retroactive rules after a problem surface always feel like a takeaway.</p><h3>What is the first ChMS AI feature most churches should pilot?</h3><p>Whichever feature aligns with the staff role most starved for time on lookup work. For most churches that is people search or giving data lookups, both of which appear in Pushpay's beta as the highest-frequency query categories. The pilot's value is in collapsing a task that takes minutes into a task that takes seconds, with the same staff member doing it.</p><h3>How do we measure whether the AI pilot worked?</h3><p>Write the success metric down before the pilot starts. Pick a specific question that has a yes or no answer at day thirty. "Our connections director can produce next-step lists for new attendees in under ten minutes" is a good metric. "We are using the new feature" is not, because it measures activity, not value. The metric is the pilot's reason to exist.</p><h3>How do we keep AI rollout from feeling impersonal to staff and congregation?</h3><p>The rollout sequence answers this by design. Inventory respects what staff are already doing. The policy is built with the team, not handed to them. The champion is one of them. The pilot is small and reversible. Trainings are short and role-specific. Each step keeps the human relationship in front of the technology, which is the point.</p><h3>What about churches using a ChMS whose vendor has not shipped AI yet?</h3><p>The same sequence still applies, with the inventory and policy stages doing the heaviest work. Most staff are using AI tools outside the ChMS regardless of vendor pace. Running the inventory and writing the short policy now means that when the vendor does ship, the rollout starts at stage three with the policy already in place and the staff conversation already had.</p><h2>Key Takeaways</h2><ul><li><p><strong>Vendor AI inside the ChMS is no longer an adoption question. It is an implementation question, and the implementation work belongs to the church.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>The rollout sequence has five stages, in this order: inventory, policy, champion, pilot, expand. Skipping or reordering any of them is the most common cause of staff-trust failure.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>A thirty-minute shared policy beats a comprehensive policy that never gets written. Light is acceptable; absent is not.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>The AI champion is the heaviest user, not the most senior leader. Sponsorship and championship are different jobs.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>A pilot has one feature, one team, thirty days, and a success metric written down before it starts. The old workflow runs in parallel until the review.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>Training that sticks is short, role-relevant, and built around three to five real use cases. Generic AI workshops do not move the work.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>Pushpay's beta data shows the win is access expansion, not novelty. Five times more staff using complex searches means the right rollout removes barriers for people who would already do the work if they could.</strong></p></li></ul><h2>Sources and References</h2><ol><li><p>Jonathan Louvis, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">"Find anyone in your church database fast"</a>, Pushpay, April 8, 2026.</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/the-2026-state-of-ai-in-the-church-93-of-pastors-are-in-now-what/">"The 2026 State Of AI In The Church: 93% Of Pastors Are In. Now What?"</a>, ChurchTechToday, April 27, 2026.</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">"How Church Leaders Are Using AI (And What Concerns Them Most)"</a>, Barna Group in partnership with Pushpay, March 26, 2026.</p></li><li><p>Hannah Hansen, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://resources.concordiatechnology.org/resource-center/how-to-train-staff-on-new-church-management-software">"How to Train Staff on New Church Management Software"</a>, Concordia Technology Solutions, November 25, 2025.</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.ministrybrands.com/podcasts/ep-173-kira-brown-how-to-use-ai-in-ministry-without-losing-the-human-touch">"Ep. 173 | Kira Brown: How to Use AI in Ministry Without Losing the Human Touch"</a>, Ministry Brands Amplify, June 10, 2026.</p></li><li><p>Jonathan Louvis, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/church-ai-policy-planning/">"Most pastors use AI. Almost none have a plan for it"</a>, Pushpay, March 16, 2026.</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">"ACST Launches Ministry Platform AI, Bringing Secure, Ministry-Aware AI to Churches"</a>, Business Wire / ACST press release, May 7, 2026.</p></li></ol><h2>Where We Go From Here</h2><p>Vendor AI inside the church management system is a real shift, and the implementation work is what decides whether it builds staff trust or quietly erodes it.</p><p>The next question most leaders run into is which feature to pilot first, and how to write the success metric in a way that gives the team a clean yes or no at day thirty.</p><p>If your ministry is working through how to roll out AI inside your existing ChMS, we would be glad to think it through with you. We offer no-pressure consultations where we listen first, then share what we have learned helping ministries navigate the same questions. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/consultation">Schedule a consultation</a>.</p>
+<p><strong>TL;DR:</strong> Vendor AI features are no longer something a church is choosing whether to adopt. They are switching on inside the church management system the church already pays for. We have watched the staff-trust failures cluster around the same five missing steps, so we wrote them out as a sequence any church can run, regardless of which ChMS sits underneath.</p><p><strong>Core Insights:</strong></p><ul><li><p>The first real implementation data is in: <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">Pushpay logged 15,185 AI search prompts across 331 accounts during beta</a>, and the win was access, not novelty.</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/the-2026-state-of-ai-in-the-church-93-of-pastors-are-in-now-what/">Only nine percent of churches have a formal AI policy</a>, even though 93.5 percent of leaders are now engaging with AI in some form.</p></li><li><p>The right champion is not the senior pastor; it is the staff member who will use the tool most.</p></li><li><p>A thirty-day pilot with one feature, one team, and a written success metric prevents permanent "we are trying it" limbo.</p></li><li><p>Short, role-relevant training beats generic AI workshops. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/the-2026-state-of-ai-in-the-church-93-of-pastors-are-in-now-what/">Sixty-one percent of leaders say they want tutorials for specific tools, not theory</a>.</p></li></ul><p>A church rolls out AI inside its existing ChMS in five stages, in this order: inventory what staff are already using, write a short shared policy, name a single champion who is not the senior pastor, pilot one feature with one team for thirty days against a written success metric, then expand with short role-relevant trainings. The sequence works because the technical setup is no longer the hard part. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">Pushpay's AI People Search beta logged 15,185 prompts across 331 accounts with five times more staff using complex searches</a>, and <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">ACST's Ministry Platform AI sets up in under thirty minutes</a>. The trust work belongs to the church, not the vendor.</p><h2>Table of Contents</h2><ul><li><p>Why Vendor AI Is Now an Implementation Question, Not an Adoption Question</p></li><li><p>Stage One: Take an Honest Inventory Before You Turn Anything On</p></li><li><p>Stage Two: Write the Thirty-Minute Policy Before the Pilot, Not After</p></li><li><p>Stage Three: Pick One Champion Who Is Not the Senior Pastor</p></li><li><p>Stage Four: Pilot One Feature With One Team for Thirty Days</p></li><li><p>Stage Five: Expand With Short Role-Relevant Trainings, Not Feature Walkthroughs</p></li><li><p>Frequently Asked Questions</p></li><li><p>Key Takeaways</p></li><li><p>Sources and References</p></li><li><p>Where We Go From Here</p></li></ul><h2>Why Vendor AI Is Now an Implementation Question, Not an Adoption Question</h2><p>Through the first half of 2026, AI moved from a thing a church chooses to adopt into a thing that switches on inside the church management system the church already uses. The question on the table is no longer whether to bring AI in. It is how to roll it out without breaking trust with the staff who have to use it.</p><p>The vendor landscape made the shift hard to miss. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">Pushpay opened AI People Search to all ChMS customers in April 2026</a> after a beta that ran across 331 accounts. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">ACST announced Ministry Platform AI in May with a Pioneer Program for the first fifty churches</a>. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.ministrybrands.com/podcasts/ep-173-kira-brown-how-to-use-ai-in-ministry-without-losing-the-human-touch">Ministry Brands shipped its Equip AI tool on June 10, 2026</a>. We are tracking similar movement across Planning Center and Subsplash. For a fuller view, our pillar on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/what-church-management-software-vendors-are-actually-doing-with-ai-in-2026/">what church management software vendors are actually doing with AI in 2026</a> maps the vendor-side picture in depth.</p><p>We will note up front that several of the numbers throughout this piece come from vendor-published research. Pushpay, ACST, and Ministry Brands have an interest in the story landing well. We cite them because the implementation detail they publish is the best available right now, and we read it accordingly.</p><p>The technical setup is no longer the hard part. ACST's Ministry Platform AI configures in under thirty minutes through standard API credentials. Pushpay's People Search activates with a switch inside the existing ChMS. The work that decides whether the rollout holds, or breaks, is the staff conversation around it. That work belongs to the church.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The vendor handles the switch. The church handles the trust. AI rollout in a ChMS is now an implementation problem, not an adoption decision.</p></blockquote><h2>Stage One: Take an Honest Inventory Before You Turn Anything On</h2><p>Before activating any vendor AI feature, find out what AI tools the staff are already using on their own. The inventory is the foundation; without it, a new vendor feature gets layered on top of an unmapped landscape, and the staff who have been quietly using ChatGPT for their newsletter draft go further underground.</p><p>The gap is real. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/church-ai-policy-planning/">Pushpay's research with Barna found that around sixty percent of pastors use AI personally each month, while only five percent of churches have a formal policy in place</a>. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">The same Barna and Pushpay collaboration reported that sixty-four percent of leaders say a policy is important, but only that same five percent actually have one</a>. A majority of staff are using AI somewhere. Almost no one has mapped it.</p><p>The inventory conversation works best as a curious one, not an audit. The four questions we ask:</p><ol><li><p>Which AI tools are you already using in your work, on any device?</p></li><li><p>What tasks are you using them for, week to week?</p></li><li><p>What information do those tools see when you use them?</p></li><li><p>What would make your current AI use easier or safer to do openly?</p></li></ol><p>The point of the inventory is not enforcement. It is the input the next stage needs. The policy cannot account for what the team is doing if the team's actual practice is invisible. As Pushpay frames it, the answers to those questions will often surprise leadership, and that surprise is the value of asking.</p><p>There is a sequencing reason to inventory before the vendor switch. Turning on Pushpay AI People Search or ACST Ministry Platform AI without knowing the existing AI footprint sets up the exact trust failure the rollout is supposed to prevent. The staff member who has been using a personal ChatGPT workflow now has to choose between continuing in the shadow or admitting it after the fact. Neither path builds trust.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Inventory first. The staff are already using AI somewhere. A vendor feature switched on without that map will compete with whatever is already in motion.</p></blockquote><h2>Stage Two: Write the Thirty-Minute Policy Before the Pilot, Not After</h2><p>The policy does not need to be a twelve-page document. It needs to exist. A thirty-minute staff conversation that produces a shared understanding of where AI is appropriate, where it is not, and who is responsible for what is enough to start. Light is acceptable; absent is not.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/church-ai-policy-planning/">Pushpay's writing on church AI policy makes the same point: a thirty-minute staff conversation that produces shared understanding counts as a policy</a>. The bar is shared clarity, not legal documentation. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/the-2026-state-of-ai-in-the-church-93-of-pastors-are-in-now-what/">ChurchTechToday's 2026 State of AI in the Church report found that only nine percent of churches currently have a formal AI policy, while forty-seven percent of leaders say they want ethical guidance from a trusted source</a>. The appetite is there. The document is not.</p><p>There are four decisions the thirty minutes needs to produce:</p><ol><li><p>What data is allowed to touch which AI tools. Congregational records, giving data, pastoral conversations, and counseling notes sit at different sensitivity levels and need different rules.</p></li><li><p>What gets disclosed to the congregation. If AI helped draft a sermon outline, a newsletter, or a follow-up message, is that named?</p></li><li><p>Who has final sign-off on AI-generated communication before it goes out.</p></li><li><p>What gets retained, and what gets deleted, after an AI tool produces output.</p></li></ol><p>Write the answers down, even informally. A shared doc that the team can point to is the asset.</p><p>Policy before pilot matters because retroactive rules feel like a takeaway, not a guardrail. Once a feature is live and a staff member is using it daily, a new rule introduced afterward reads as a correction of that person. Setting the rule before the switch reads as a shared standard. The difference is enormous in a small staff culture.</p><p>Vendor-side guarantees reduce technical risk but do not write the policy for the church. ACST's role-based permissions, audit trails, and assurance that data is not used to train external models address the infrastructure layer. The policy layer, what the team decides to do with the tool, is still on the church to set. For a deeper walk-through, our piece on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/why-64-of-church-leaders-say-they-need-an-ai-policy-but-only-5-have-one/">the AI policy questions every church staff needs to answer</a> covers the full set we work through with ministries.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> A short, written, shared policy beats a comprehensive policy that never gets written. The policy is a precondition for the pilot, not its output.</p></blockquote><h2>Stage Three: Pick One Champion Who Is Not the Senior Pastor</h2><p>The AI champion should be the staff member who will use the new feature most, not the senior pastor. For ChMS AI features like people search, giving lookups, or attendance trends, that means a connections director, a finance lead, a children's pastor, or a small-groups coordinator. The senior pastor sponsors the rollout; someone closer to the work runs it.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">Pushpay's beta data on AI People Search shows the user pattern clearly</a>. The six query categories that dominated the beta were driven by connections staff, children's ministry leads, finance directors, small-groups coordinators, attendance trackers, and volunteer coordinators. Senior leadership rarely appeared in the heavy-use cohort. The features were built for the people doing the daily lookup work, and they used them.</p><p>The senior pastor is often the wrong champion because the senior pastor is often the lightest user. The champion needs daily use to surface real friction, because friction is what the pilot review needs to evaluate. A leader who tries the tool twice a month will not have the data to make the call at day thirty.</p><p>The champion's job has three parts:</p><ol><li><p>Be the first daily user of the new feature.</p></li><li><p>Collect what is working and what is not, in plain notes.</p></li><li><p>Run the thirty-day pilot review meeting.</p></li></ol><p>The profile we look for: someone trusted by the team, technically curious enough to try things, honest enough to say when something does not work. Title does not matter. Trust does.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.ministrybrands.com/podcasts/ep-173-kira-brown-how-to-use-ai-in-ministry-without-losing-the-human-touch">Ministry Brands' conversation with Kira Brown on using AI in ministry without losing the human touch</a> lands a related point: the people running the tool day to day are the ones who translate vendor framing into specific staff conversations. The champion is the translator.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The champion is the heaviest user, not the most senior leader. Sponsorship and championship are different jobs.</p></blockquote><h2>Stage Four: Pilot One Feature With One Team for Thirty Days</h2><p>A pilot has one feature, one team, a thirty-day window, and a success metric written down before it starts. The old workflow keeps running in parallel during the pilot. The success metric is the question the pilot answers, and it should be specific enough that a yes or no answer is possible at day thirty.</p><p>Pick the feature most aligned with current staff pain. From Pushpay's six beta query categories, the highest-leverage starters are usually connections lookups or giving questions. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">The beta data showed time per complex filter dropping from minutes to about five seconds, with five times more unique users running complex searches than before</a>. The story behind the numbers is access expansion: the staff who could not previously run a complex query suddenly could. That is the kind of outcome a pilot is built to confirm or rule out in a single church's context. Before locking the pilot scope, the questions in our piece on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/chms-vendor-ai-questions-before-renewal/">what to ask your ChMS vendor about AI before you renew</a> help clarify what the feature will and will not do.</p><p>One team only. Pulling three teams into a pilot at once dilutes the signal and triples the change-management load. The point of a pilot is a clean read on one variable.</p><p>Define the success metric in writing before turning the feature on. Examples that work:</p><ul><li><p>Our connections director can produce next-step lists for new-attendee follow-up in under ten minutes.</p></li><li><p>Our finance lead can answer board questions about giving without exporting to a spreadsheet.</p></li><li><p>Our children's ministry coordinator can pull current allergy and check-in notes for a Sunday in a single query.</p></li></ul><p>Each of these has a yes or no answer at day thirty. "We are using the new feature" does not, because it measures activity, not value.</p><p>Parallel running is part of the design. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://resources.concordiatechnology.org/resource-center/how-to-train-staff-on-new-church-management-software">Concordia Technology's guidance on training staff through a system transition recommends running old and new workflows side by side, and staying available for ongoing questions for months rather than relying on a single training session</a>. We apply the same principle to AI feature rollouts. The team needs the fallback, and the comparison itself is useful pilot data.</p><p>Where vendors offer structured pilot support, take it. ACST's Pioneer Program for the first fifty churches on Ministry Platform AI offers vendor-supported onboarding for cohort-style pilots, which can shorten the time to a clean review. Whether or not the vendor offers a program, the church's own thirty-day discipline is what produces the answer.</p><p>At day thirty, the champion answers the success metric, yes or no. The team votes on one of three outcomes: expand, extend the pilot by another thirty days with a refined metric, or stop. No fourth option. Drift into permanent trial is the failure mode this stage is designed to prevent.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> One feature, one team, thirty days, one written metric. The pilot's job is to answer a specific question, not to demonstrate enthusiasm.</p></blockquote><h2>Stage Five: Expand With Short Role-Relevant Trainings, Not Feature Walkthroughs</h2><p>Expansion happens only after the pilot review answers yes on its success metric. The training that follows is short, role-relevant, and focused on the three to five things each staff member will actually do with the tool. Generic feature walkthroughs do not stick; specific use cases do.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/the-2026-state-of-ai-in-the-church-93-of-pastors-are-in-now-what/">ChurchTechToday's 2026 report found that sixty-one percent of church leaders want tutorials for specific tools and fifty-nine percent want ministry-specific use cases</a>. They are not asking for AI theory. They are asking for the exact phrasing that gets the connections director the list of new attendees who missed last Sunday.</p><p>The training format we recommend: thirty minutes per role, focused on the three to five queries that staff member runs most often. The session is built around the actual tasks already on that person's calendar. A children's ministry coordinator's session is different from a finance lead's session, and that difference is the point.</p><p>Documentation matters as much as the live session. A one-page reference per role, in plain language, with the exact phrasings that worked during the pilot, gives the team something to return to. Without it, the training fades by the next staff meeting.</p><p>Ongoing support is the part most rollouts underestimate. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://resources.concordiatechnology.org/resource-center/how-to-train-staff-on-new-church-management-software">Concordia Technology's research on staff training through a software change makes clear that questions surface for months, not weeks, as staff hit tasks that only come up periodically</a>. The annual stewardship campaign, the back-to-school children's check-in surge, the year-end giving close, each one is a moment a new question shows up. Build the expectation that the champion stays available for those moments. The lessons here overlap meaningfully with what we wrote in <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/the-five-stages-of-chms-migration-nobody-warns-you-about/">what church management software migration actually involves</a>, because the human side of a feature rollout and a full migration share the same gravity.</p><p>What expansion should not look like: a single all-staff lunch-and-learn where the AI feature is demoed once and never referenced again. That format checks a box and produces no behavior change.</p><p>The vendor ships the feature. The rollout sequence above is how a church actually lands it. The Friday pillar on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/blog/what-church-management-software-vendors-are-actually-doing-with-ai-in-2026/">the vendor-by-vendor AI rollout map</a> covers the supply side; the five stages here are the demand side, the implementation work that turns a feature switch into a tool the team trusts.</p><blockquote><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Short, role-relevant training sticks. Generic feature walkthroughs fade by the next staff meeting.</p></blockquote><h2>Frequently Asked Questions</h2><h3>How long should a church AI pilot run inside a ChMS?</h3><p>Thirty days is the right default for a single feature with a single team. Shorter than that and the staff have not had time to hit the tasks that only show up every few weeks. Longer than that and the pilot drifts into permanent "we are trying it" status with no decision point. The thirty-day mark is the review meeting, not the end of use.</p><h3>Who should own AI rollout in a church?</h3><p>The senior pastor sponsors it. A staff champion who will use the tool most often runs it. For ChMS AI features, that usually means a connections director, a finance lead, a children's pastor, or a small-groups coordinator. Sponsorship and championship are distinct jobs, and conflating them is one of the most common reasons a rollout stalls inside the first sixty days.</p><h3>Do we need a formal AI policy before turning on vendor AI features?</h3><p>Yes, in the lightest possible form. A thirty-minute staff conversation that produces a shared understanding of what data goes where, what gets disclosed, and who has final sign-off is a policy. We do not recommend waiting on a comprehensive document. We do recommend never running the pilot without something written down, because retroactive rules after a problem surface always feel like a takeaway.</p><h3>What is the first ChMS AI feature most churches should pilot?</h3><p>Whichever feature aligns with the staff role most starved for time on lookup work. For most churches that is people search or giving data lookups, both of which appear in Pushpay's beta as the highest-frequency query categories. The pilot's value is in collapsing a task that takes minutes into a task that takes seconds, with the same staff member doing it.</p><h3>How do we measure whether the AI pilot worked?</h3><p>Write the success metric down before the pilot starts. Pick a specific question that has a yes or no answer at day thirty. "Our connections director can produce next-step lists for new attendees in under ten minutes" is a good metric. "We are using the new feature" is not, because it measures activity, not value. The metric is the pilot's reason to exist.</p><h3>How do we keep AI rollout from feeling impersonal to staff and congregation?</h3><p>The rollout sequence answers this by design. Inventory respects what staff are already doing. The policy is built with the team, not handed to them. The champion is one of them. The pilot is small and reversible. Trainings are short and role-specific. Each step keeps the human relationship in front of the technology, which is the point.</p><h3>What about churches using a ChMS whose vendor has not shipped AI yet?</h3><p>The same sequence still applies, with the inventory and policy stages doing the heaviest work. Most staff are using AI tools outside the ChMS regardless of vendor pace. Running the inventory and writing the short policy now means that when the vendor does ship, the rollout starts at stage three with the policy already in place and the staff conversation already had.</p><h2>Key Takeaways</h2><ul><li><p><strong>Vendor AI inside the ChMS is no longer an adoption question. It is an implementation question, and the implementation work belongs to the church.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>The rollout sequence has five stages, in this order: inventory, policy, champion, pilot, expand. Skipping or reordering any of them is the most common cause of staff-trust failure.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>A thirty-minute shared policy beats a comprehensive policy that never gets written. Light is acceptable; absent is not.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>The AI champion is the heaviest user, not the most senior leader. Sponsorship and championship are different jobs.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>A pilot has one feature, one team, thirty days, and a success metric written down before it starts. The old workflow runs in parallel until the review.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>Training that sticks is short, role-relevant, and built around three to five real use cases. Generic AI workshops do not move the work.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>Pushpay's beta data shows the win is access expansion, not novelty. Five times more staff using complex searches means the right rollout removes barriers for people who would already do the work if they could.</strong></p></li></ul><h2>Sources and References</h2><ol><li><p>Jonathan Louvis, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/ai-for-people-search-chms/">"Find anyone in your church database fast"</a>, Pushpay, April 8, 2026.</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/the-2026-state-of-ai-in-the-church-93-of-pastors-are-in-now-what/">"The 2026 State Of AI In The Church: 93% Of Pastors Are In. Now What?"</a>, ChurchTechToday, April 27, 2026.</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">"How Church Leaders Are Using AI (And What Concerns Them Most)"</a>, Barna Group in partnership with Pushpay, March 26, 2026.</p></li><li><p>Hannah Hansen, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://resources.concordiatechnology.org/resource-center/how-to-train-staff-on-new-church-management-software">"How to Train Staff on New Church Management Software"</a>, Concordia Technology Solutions, November 25, 2025.</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.ministrybrands.com/podcasts/ep-173-kira-brown-how-to-use-ai-in-ministry-without-losing-the-human-touch">"Ep. 173 | Kira Brown: How to Use AI in Ministry Without Losing the Human Touch"</a>, Ministry Brands Amplify, June 10, 2026.</p></li><li><p>Jonathan Louvis, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/church-ai-policy-planning/">"Most pastors use AI. Almost none have a plan for it"</a>, Pushpay, March 16, 2026.</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">"ACST Launches Ministry Platform AI, Bringing Secure, Ministry-Aware AI to Churches"</a>, Business Wire / ACST press release, May 7, 2026.</p></li></ol><h2>Where We Go From Here</h2><p>Vendor AI inside the church management system is a real shift, and the implementation work is what decides whether it builds staff trust or quietly erodes it.</p><p>The next question most leaders run into is which feature to pilot first, and how to write the success metric in a way that gives the team a clean yes or no at day thirty.</p><p>If your ministry is working through how to roll out AI inside your existing ChMS, we would be glad to think it through with you. We offer no-pressure consultations where we listen first, then share what we have learned helping ministries navigate the same questions. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/contact/">Schedule a consultation</a>.</p>

-<p><strong>TL;DR:</strong> Five attack patterns are responsible for nearly every confirmed church cyber incident we have reviewed in 2025: staff email account takeover, vendor email compromise, VIP spoofing of pastors and executives, church management system credential theft, and ransomware. The independent data lines up with what church-focused IT firms are reporting in the field, and the financial stakes are no longer abstract. This guide walks through each attack, the evidence behind it, and the controls that actually move the needle.</p><p><strong>What You Need to Know About Church Cyberattacks in 2025:</strong></p><ul><li><p>Business email compromise alone drove <strong>$3.046 billion in reported U.S. losses in 2025</strong>, second only to investment fraud, per the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.fbi.gov/news/press-releases/cryptocurrency-and-ai-scams-bilk-americans-of-billions">FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center's 2025 Annual Report</a>.</p></li><li><p>Incidents against religious, civic, and social organizations <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://blackpointcyber.com/blog/when-good-causes-become-big-targets-the-rise-in-attacks-on-mission-driven-organizations/">surged 555% year over year in the first seven months of 2025</a>, per Blackpoint Cyber's August 2025 report.</p></li><li><p>The five attack patterns we cover here come directly from <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://enableministry.com/resources/top-5-cyber-threats-churches-face-in-2025/">Enable Ministry Partners' September 2025 field summary</a>, the most credible church-specific source currently published.</p></li><li><p>Real church losses in 2025 included a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/business-email-compromise-and-how-to-avoid-it/">$1.75 million wire fraud at St. Ambrose Catholic Parish</a> and a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.comparitech.com/news/cybercriminals-give-indiana-megachurch-7-days-to-pay-600k-ransom-after-data-breach/">ransomware breach at First Baptist Church of Hammond affecting 5,217 people</a>.</p></li><li><p>Multi-factor authentication, out-of-band verification of financial requests, and tested offline backups defend against most of what we describe here.</p></li><li><p>These attacks are predictable and defensible. What is not defensible is treating cybersecurity as someone else's problem.</p></li></ul><p><strong>What are the five types of cyberattacks targeting churches in 2025?</strong> The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://enableministry.com/resources/top-5-cyber-threats-churches-face-in-2025/">five confirmed attack types are staff email account takeover, vendor email compromise, VIP spoofing of church leaders, church management system credential theft, and ransomware</a>. Each follows a documented pattern, and together they account for the overwhelming majority of church cyber incidents reported by Enable Ministry Partners and tracked independently by the FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center in 2025.</p><p>We cover the broader posture in our guide to <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="/blog/cybersecurity-and-data-stewardship-for-churches/">cybersecurity and data stewardship for churches</a>, and this post drills into the five specific attack patterns church leaders most need to recognize.</p><h2>Table of Contents</h2><ul><li><p>Why Churches Have Become a Primary Target</p></li><li><p>Attack One: Staff Email Account Takeover (Church Staff BEC)</p></li><li><p>Attack Two: Vendor and Partner Email Compromise</p></li><li><p>Attack Three: VIP Spoofing of Pastors and Executives</p></li><li><p>Attack Four: Church Management System Account Compromise</p></li><li><p>Attack Five: Ransomware</p></li><li><p>What Actually Reduces the Risk</p></li><li><p>Frequently Asked Questions</p></li><li><p>Key Takeaways</p></li><li><p>Sources and References</p></li><li><p>Where We Go From Here</p></li></ul><h2>Why Churches Have Become a Primary Target</h2><p>Churches are no longer collateral damage in untargeted phishing waves. They are now selected. Blackpoint Cyber's August 2025 report tracked a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://blackpointcyber.com/blog/when-good-causes-become-big-targets-the-rise-in-attacks-on-mission-driven-organizations/">555% year-over-year jump in confirmed incidents targeting "Institutions &amp; Organizations,"</a> the industry classification that includes religious organizations. BDO's <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.bdo.com/insights/industries/nonprofit-education/the-crucial-role-of-cybersecurity-for-nonprofit-organizations-in-2025">2025 nonprofit cybersecurity briefing</a> reported a 30% year-over-year increase in weekly cyberattacks against nonprofits in 2024, and an average breach cost approaching $2 million when recovery, legal exposure, and reputational damage are counted.</p><p>The targeting logic is straightforward. Churches hold giving records, member directories, counseling notes, and payment credentials. Staff and volunteers are wired to trust requests that sound pastoral or urgent. Budgets for security tooling are thin, and the same person who runs ChMS often also runs payroll, the livestream, and the office network. Enable Ministry Partners summarized the dynamic in their September 2025 report: attackers "understand our vernacular, business processes, and the overall trusting nature of our staff and lay leaders." That is the operating environment we work in now.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Church cybersecurity is no longer a question of whether a congregation is large enough to be attacked. It is a question of whether the congregation is prepared for the attacks that are already underway.</p><h2>How Does Staff Email Account Takeover Work (Church Staff BEC)?</h2><p>An attacker captures a staff member's email username, password, and in many cases the multi-factor authentication code, then logs in directly to the inbox and operates from inside it. Enable Ministry Partners lists this as the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://enableministry.com/resources/top-5-cyber-threats-churches-face-in-2025/">number one confirmed attack pattern they responded to in 2025</a>. Once inside, the attacker reads recent email threads, identifies banking and vendor relationships, and then sends instructions to banks or congregants from the legitimate mailbox.</p><p>Because the email actually comes from the staff member's address, traditional spoofing filters do nothing. The FBI's 2025 IC3 Annual Report recorded <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://rexxfield.com/bec-by-the-numbers-2025-ic3-report/">$3.046 billion in business email compromise losses across 24,768 complaints</a>, with 86% of those losses moving by wire transfer or ACH. Churches sit inside that count, and the IC3 figure is widely understood to undercount because most victims never file.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> If an attacker is inside a real church inbox, no email-based filter will save us. The only durable defenses are MFA with number matching, conditional access policies, and out-of-band verification of any financial instruction.</p><h2>How Does Vendor and Partner Email Compromise Target Churches?</h2><p>The attacker compromises a contractor, mission trip coordinator, landscaper, or security vendor, then sends the church a routine-looking invoice with new banking details. This is exactly what happened at <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/business-email-compromise-and-how-to-avoid-it/">St. Ambrose Catholic Parish, which lost $1.75 million</a> after attackers monitored email between the parish and its construction contractor, then inserted new wire instructions at the moment a real payment was due.</p><p>No filter caught it because the message routing was legitimate. Enable Ministry Partners reports vendor compromise <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://enableministry.com/resources/top-5-cyber-threats-churches-face-in-2025/">nearly always ends in financial loss</a> for the targeted church. The pattern is also a useful reminder that vendor security questions belong in every procurement conversation. We covered the broader vendor-selection discipline in our piece on <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="/blog/how-to-evaluate-church-management-software-without-getting-sold/">how to evaluate church management software without getting sold</a>, and the same out-of-band verification principle applies to any vendor handling money or member data.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Any change to a vendor's banking instructions must be verified by phone, on a number we already have on file, before a single dollar moves. Email confirmation of an emailed change is not verification.</p><h2>How Does VIP Spoofing of Pastors and Executives Work?</h2><p>Staff and members receive a message that appears to come from the senior pastor, executive pastor, or business administrator, often by text. The request is small at first: "Are you available? I need a quick favor." Once the target engages, they are asked to buy gift cards and send the codes.</p><p>Enable Ministry Partners notes this pattern <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://enableministry.com/resources/top-5-cyber-threats-churches-face-in-2025/">works far too often</a> in church settings. The FBI's 2025 IC3 report confirms phishing and spoofing as the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.fbi.gov/news/press-releases/cryptocurrency-and-ai-scams-bilk-americans-of-billions">single most reported complaint category</a>, with 191,561 reports nationally. Religion Unplugged's August 2025 reporting documented <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://religionunplugged.com/news/2025/8/21/email-phishing-scams-increasingly-target-churches">church teams experiencing up to 34% failure rates in phishing simulations</a>, among the highest of any sector. AI now makes this attack worse. The IC3 report's first dedicated section on artificial intelligence documented voice cloning being layered into business email compromise, with follow-up calls that sound like a real CFO or pastor reinforcing a written request.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> A standing policy that no leader will ever ask staff or congregants to buy gift cards, by text or email, defangs this attack almost entirely once it is communicated.</p><h2>How Does Church Management System Account Compromise Happen?</h2><p>A staff member or lay leader with broad access has their credentials stolen, often through password reuse from a breached third-party site. The attacker logs in and exports the directory. The damage is not just the data loss. The attacker now holds <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://enableministry.com/resources/top-5-cyber-threats-churches-face-in-2025/">verified contact information for every member of the congregation</a> and uses it to launch targeted scams against congregants in the church's name. The reputational fallout often outlasts the technical incident by months.</p><p>Community IT's <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://communityit.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/05/2025-April-Nonprofit-Cybersecurity-Incident-REPORT.pdf">2025 Nonprofit Cybersecurity Incident Report</a> documented 472 suspected and 32 confirmed account compromises across its nonprofit clients in 2024, with brute-force attempts and credential stuffing as the dominant access methods. The pattern is identical to what church-focused responders are seeing.</p><p>There is also a structural issue underneath this attack. Churches commonly hold the same identifying information in three or four systems that do not talk to each other, which multiplies the surfaces an attacker can hit. We unpacked that pattern in <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="/blog/your-church-tech-stack-isn-t-broken-it-s-built-this-way/">why your church tech stack isn't broken, it's built this way</a>, and consolidating the data picture is part of the cybersecurity story too.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> MFA on every ChMS account, role-scoped permissions so no single login exports the entire directory, and quarterly access reviews are the controls that actually reduce this risk.</p><h2>What Is Ransomware and How Does It Target Churches?</h2><p>Attackers gain access through an unpatched vulnerability or a phished credential, exfiltrate data first, encrypt the network second, and then demand payment in cryptocurrency. Many groups in 2025 now skip encryption entirely and run extortion-only attacks.</p><p>The most prominent 2025 church case was <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.comparitech.com/news/cybercriminals-give-indiana-megachurch-7-days-to-pay-600k-ransom-after-data-breach/">First Baptist Church of Hammond in Indiana</a>, where the Rhysida ransomware group demanded approximately $594,000 in July and ultimately compromised the personal data of 5,217 people. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.gcfa.org/resource/october-is-cybersecurity-awareness-month-why-your-church-can-t-wait-to-take-action">General Council on Finance and Administration of the United Methodist Church publicly noted in October 2025</a> that the LockBit group had also recently breached Relentless Church in South Carolina. Across the broader sector, Blackpoint Cyber tracked <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://blackpointcyber.com/blog/when-good-causes-become-big-targets-the-rise-in-attacks-on-mission-driven-organizations/">109 institutions and organizations posted to ransomware data leak sites in the first seven months of 2025</a>, a 23.9% increase over 2024. Aprio's nonprofit briefing documented <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.aprio.com/insights-events/how-can-nonprofits-manage-cybersecurity-risk-ins-article-np/">the Salvation Army losing names, Social Security numbers, and driver's license numbers</a> to the Chaos ransomware group in May 2025.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Tested, offline backups and a written incident response plan are the difference between ransomware as a weekend recovery and ransomware as an extinction event. Paying the ransom does not reliably get the data back.</p><h2>What Controls Actually Reduce Church Cybersecurity Risk?</h2><p>Four controls do most of the work across every credible source we reviewed:</p><ol><li><p><strong>Multi-factor authentication with number matching</strong> on every staff and lay leader account, including ChMS and giving platforms.</p></li><li><p><strong>Out-of-band verification</strong> for every change to banking instructions, every wire transfer, and every unusual request from leadership.</p></li><li><p><strong>Tested, offline backups</strong> of email, ChMS data, financial records, and critical files, restored on a schedule so we know they actually work.</p></li><li><p><strong>Security awareness training</strong> with phishing simulations run at least quarterly, including for volunteers with system access.</p></li></ol><p>These controls are not glamorous, and they are not expensive at the scale of most church budgets. The budget conversation usually reveals where the real bottleneck is, and we have written separately about <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="/blog/why-your-church-s-technology-budget-reveals-more-than-you-think/">why your church's technology budget reveals more than you think</a>.</p><h2>Frequently Asked Questions</h2><h3>Are small churches really being targeted, or is this only a large-church problem?</h3><p>Small churches are being targeted at high rates. Blackpoint Cyber's 2025 data shows attackers moving from "big game hunting" toward mid-tier and small targets, precisely because soft targets carry less law enforcement attention. The FBI's 2025 IC3 data on business email compromise does not stratify by victim size, but church-focused IT firms report that congregations under 500 in attendance are now common BEC victims.</p><h3>Does cyber insurance cover these losses?</h3><p>It depends entirely on the policy. Standard errors-and-omissions coverage frequently excludes social engineering losses, which is the category most church BEC incidents fall under. Cyber-specific policies often require documented controls such as MFA before they pay claims. We recommend reviewing both the existing policy and the application questions with an insurance broker who works specifically with cyber risk.</p><h3>How long does a typical recovery take after a ransomware attack on a church?</h3><p>Operational recovery commonly runs three to eight weeks even when backups exist, because rebuilding identity systems, validating data integrity, and notifying affected individuals all take time. Without tested backups, recovery can extend beyond six months, and in some cases the church never fully recovers operationally before donor confidence erodes.</p><h3>What does multi-factor authentication actually prevent?</h3><p>Properly configured MFA prevents the vast majority of credential-stuffing and password-reuse attacks. It also prevents most basic phishing attacks from succeeding. It does not prevent MFA fatigue attacks where users approve a flood of push notifications, which is why number matching, where the user types a code displayed on screen, is now the recommended configuration.</p><h3>Are denominational offices providing cybersecurity guidance?</h3><p>Some are. The General Council on Finance and Administration of the United Methodist Church published October 2025 guidance on cybersecurity awareness, and several denominational technology offices now offer assessments. Coverage is uneven across denominations, and most congregations still need to source guidance externally.</p><h3>Should we pay a ransom if attacked?</h3><p>Independent security guidance from the FBI and most insurers is to avoid payment. The 2025 IC3 report does not endorse payment, and BDO's nonprofit briefing notes that very few organizations that paid received complete data restoration. Payment also marks the organization as willing to pay, which raises the probability of future targeting.</p><h3>What is the single most cost-effective control we can implement this month?</h3><p>Enabling multi-factor authentication on every staff and lay leader account, including ChMS, email, giving platform, and any financial systems. The cost is generally zero with existing platforms, and the risk reduction is the largest of any single control available to a church under significant budget pressure.</p><h2>Key Takeaways</h2><ul><li><p><strong>The five attacks confirmed against churches in 2025 are staff email takeover, vendor email compromise, VIP spoofing, ChMS account compromise, and ransomware.</strong> All five are documented by independent reporting and church-focused IT firms.</p></li><li><p><strong>Business email compromise drove $3.046 billion in U.S. losses in 2025</strong>, per the FBI IC3 Annual Report, and 86% of those losses moved by wire or ACH.</p></li><li><p><strong>Religious and civic organizations saw a 555% year-over-year increase in confirmed incidents</strong> in the first seven months of 2025, per Blackpoint Cyber.</p></li><li><p><strong>Real 2025 church losses include $1.75 million at St. Ambrose Catholic Parish and 5,217 people affected by the ransomware breach at First Baptist Church of Hammond.</strong></p></li><li><p><strong>Multi-factor authentication, out-of-band verification of financial requests, tested offline backups, and quarterly phishing simulations</strong> defend against most of what attackers are using.</p></li><li><p><strong>Cybersecurity is a stewardship issue, not a technology issue.</strong> Treating it as IT plumbing is what produces the conditions these attacks rely on.</p></li></ul><h2>Sources and References</h2><ul><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.fbi.gov/news/press-releases/cryptocurrency-and-ai-scams-bilk-americans-of-billions">FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center, 2025 Annual Report press release</a> (April 2026)</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://rexxfield.com/bec-by-the-numbers-2025-ic3-report/">Rexxfield analysis of BEC statistics in the FBI IC3 2025 Annual Report</a> (April 2026)</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://enableministry.com/resources/top-5-cyber-threats-churches-face-in-2025/">Enable Ministry Partners, "Top 5 Cyber Threats Churches Face in 2025"</a> (September 2025)</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://blackpointcyber.com/blog/when-good-causes-become-big-targets-the-rise-in-attacks-on-mission-driven-organizations/">Blackpoint Cyber, "When Good Causes Become Big Targets"</a> (August 2025)</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.comparitech.com/news/cybercriminals-give-indiana-megachurch-7-days-to-pay-600k-ransom-after-data-breach/">Comparitech reporting on First Baptist Church of Hammond ransomware breach</a> (July 2025)</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/business-email-compromise-and-how-to-avoid-it/">Church Tech Today reporting on St. Ambrose Catholic Parish BEC case</a> (January 2026)</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.gcfa.org/resource/october-is-cybersecurity-awareness-month-why-your-church-can-t-wait-to-take-action">GCFA, "October Is Cybersecurity Awareness Month"</a> (October 2025)</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://religionunplugged.com/news/2025/8/21/email-phishing-scams-increasingly-target-churches">Religion Unplugged, "Email Phishing Scams Increasingly Target Churches"</a> (August 2025)</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.bdo.com/insights/industries/nonprofit-education/the-crucial-role-of-cybersecurity-for-nonprofit-organizations-in-2025">BDO, "The Crucial Role of Cybersecurity for Nonprofit Organizations in 2025"</a> (February 2025)</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://communityit.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/05/2025-April-Nonprofit-Cybersecurity-Incident-REPORT.pdf">Community IT, 2025 Nonprofit Cybersecurity Incident Report</a> (May 2025)</p></li><li><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.aprio.com/insights-events/how-can-nonprofits-manage-cybersecurity-risk-ins-article-np/">Aprio, "Cybersecurity Risk Management for Nonprofits"</a> (February 2026)</p></li></ul><h2>Where We Go From Here</h2><p>The five attack patterns above are now the working baseline of what every church leadership team should be able to recognize, prevent, and respond to. None of them are exotic, and none of them are unaffordable to defend against. The next question is usually where to start, which controls to sequence first, and how to bring the rest of the staff and lay leadership along without creating fear.</p><p>That is the conversation we work through in our broader guide to <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="/blog/cybersecurity-and-data-stewardship-for-churches/">cybersecurity and data stewardship in ministry settings</a>. If your ministry is working through how to harden against these specific attacks, we would be glad to think it through with you. We offer no-pressure consultations where we listen first, then share what we have learned helping ministries navigate the same questions.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/consultation">Schedule a consultation</a>.</p>
