-<p><strong>TL;DR:</strong> Most churches collect giving and membership data but struggle to answer basic leadership questions in real time. The problem isn't tools, it's integration. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://outbound.ministrybrands.com/hubfs/Ministry%20Brands%20Files/Annual%20Church%20Giving%20Report%202026/2026_Protestant_Annual_Church_Giving_Report.pdf">Recurring giving now accounts for 35 percent of digital giving</a>, but many finance committees can't tell you what share of their own budget comes from recurring donors. This article shows you which questions matter most, how to define metrics like lapsed givers, and what integrated reporting looks like when done right.</p><p><strong>Core Insights:</strong></p><ul><li><p>Finance committees need seven core reports: giving totals, recurring trends, lapsed givers (60 to 90 days for monthly donors), new giver conversion, and concentration risk.</p></li><li><p>Leadership boards need the overlap view: who gives and attends, who attends but doesn't give, who gives but rarely shows up, and who recently stopped both.</p></li><li><p>Lapsed giver detection is the highest leverage report most churches aren't running, especially when <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://afpglobal.org/sites/default/files/attachments/resource/FEP_Report_Q4_2024_Final.pdf">nonprofit donor retention sits at 42.9 percent</a>.</p></li><li><p>Integrated reporting means answering the same questions the same way every month without manual exports.</p></li><li><p>The integration project is usually smaller than churches think, most have more capability in current systems than they've configured.</p></li></ul><h2>Table of Contents</h2><ul><li><p>Why Most Churches Have Data but No Answers</p></li><li><p>What Questions Should Your Finance Committee Be Able to Answer</p></li><li><p>How Do You Define a Lapsed Giver</p></li><li><p>What Questions Should Your Elder Board or Leadership Team Ask</p></li><li><p>What Does Reliable Integrated Reporting Look Like</p></li><li><p>What Should You Know About Digital Giving Channels and Their Data</p></li><li><p>How Do You Know Whether Your Current Data Picture Is Healthy</p></li><li><p>Frequently Asked Questions</p></li><li><p>Key Takeaways</p></li></ul><p>A healthy data picture lets a finance committee, elder board, or staff team answer specific questions about who is giving, who is engaged, and who is drifting, in minutes rather than weeks. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2025/04/30/3071371/0/en/Pushpay-s-2025-State-of-Church-Tech-Report-Reveals-Digital-Tools-are-Strengthening-Faith-Fueling-Connection-and-Shaping-the-Future-of-Ministry.html">The 2025 State of Church Technology Report from Pushpay</a> found 86 percent of surveyed leaders now use church management software, and 70 percent say technology has increased generosity. The tools are in place. The reporting discipline often isn't.</p><h2>Why Most Churches Have Data but No Answers</h2><p>We work with finance committees and elder boards who collect plenty of data but struggle to answer basic questions about retention, engagement, and giving patterns.</p><p>The tools are in place. The reporting discipline often isn't.</p><p>Giving data sits in one platform. Attendance and small group data sit in another. Volunteer scheduling sits in a third. The only person who connects them is the staff member who knows how to export from each system.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2025/04/30/3071371/0/en/Pushpay-s-2025-State-of-Church-Tech-Report-Reveals-Digital-Tools-are-Strengthening-Faith-Fueling-Connection-and-Shaping-the-Future-of-Ministry.html">The 2025 State of Church Technology Report from Pushpay</a> found 86 percent of surveyed leaders now use church management software, and 70 percent say technology has increased generosity. The gap isn't tools. It's the reporting framework.</p><p>We see this clearly when data silos prevent the picture we're trying to assemble.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Reliable data isn't about having more dashboards, it's about answering the same leadership questions the same way every month without a manual export project each time.</p><h2>What Questions Should Your Finance Committee Be Able to Answer</h2><p>Finance committees ask the same questions almost every meeting. They're not exotic. They're the basic vitals of a church's financial picture.</p><p>Here's what we see asked most:</p><ul><li><p>What is our giving total this month, this quarter, and year to date compared to the same period last year?</p></li><li><p>How many giving units contributed this month, and how does that compare to attendance?</p></li><li><p>What percentage of total giving came from recurring gifts, and is that share growing or shrinking?</p></li><li><p>Who are our lapsed givers (people who gave consistently and then stopped within the last 60 to 180 days)?</p></li><li><p>Who are our new givers in the last 90 days, and how many gave a second time?</p></li><li><p>What is the concentration risk: what percentage of total giving comes from the top 10 percent of giving units?</p></li></ul><p>These aren't analytics questions. They're stewardship questions.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://outbound.ministrybrands.com/hubfs/Ministry%20Brands%20Files/Annual%20Church%20Giving%20Report%202026/2026_Protestant_Annual_Church_Giving_Report.pdf">The 2025 Ministry Brands data</a> shows recurring giving accounted for 35 percent of digital giving in 2025 and grew 5.4 percent year over year. If a finance committee doesn't know what share of its own giving is recurring, the committee is governing without a basic fact about how its budget is funded.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> A finance committee that doesn't define lapsed givers and pull a list of them isn't equipped to do donor care, and donor care is the most underused growth lever in church finance.</p><h2>How Do You Define a Lapsed Giver</h2><p>The definition has to fit the giving pattern. A lapsed annual donor and a lapsed monthly recurring donor aren't the same problem. Treating them the same produces a noisy, useless report.</p><p>The benchmark we work from comes from the broader nonprofit sector. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://afpglobal.org/sites/default/files/attachments/resource/FEP_Report_Q4_2024_Final.pdf">The Fundraising Effectiveness Project found donor retention at 42.9 percent in 2024</a>, the fifth consecutive year of decline. Repeat donors retain at 69.2 percent. New donors retain at 20 percent.</p><p>Lapse signals look different by segment:</p><ul><li><p><strong>Recurring monthly givers:</strong> Lapse window is 60 to 90 days from the last expected gift.</p></li><li><p><strong>Annual givers:</strong> Lapse window stretches to 12 to 18 months.</p></li><li><p><strong>First-time givers:</strong> The most important window is the next 90 days because the probability of a second gift drops sharply after that.</p></li></ul><p>The point of defining these windows isn't technical. It's so a pastor has a pastoral conversation, not a financial one, before a quiet drift becomes a permanent exit.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Lapsed giver definitions must be set per segment, written down, and applied consistently every month, otherwise the report tells you nothing about whether things are getting better or worse.</p><h2>What Questions Should Your Elder Board or Leadership Team Ask</h2><p>The elder board and senior leadership operate at a different altitude than the finance committee. They're looking at engagement health and the strength of the discipleship pipeline, not just the offering plate.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.covidreligionresearch.org/research/national-survey-research/signs-of-rebound-amid-uneven-recovery-the-changing-congregational-landscape/">The Hartford Institute's 2026 report on congregational rebound</a> found median in-person worship attendance has risen to 70, surpassing pre-pandemic levels. This marks the first positive gain in 25 years of tracking. Volunteer participation has rebounded, with 40 percent of congregants now volunteering regularly.</p><p>Boards that see those patterns in their own data, segmented by age, tenure, and ministry involvement, are positioned to act on them. Boards that don't are guessing.</p><p>Here are the questions a board should be able to ask:</p><ul><li><p>How many adults attended at least once this quarter, and how does that overlap with the giving roster?</p></li><li><p>What is the trend in weekly volunteer participation, and which ministries are growing or shrinking?</p></li><li><p>How many new members joined in the last 12 months, and how many are still active by month six?</p></li><li><p>Where are the gaps between attendance and engagement (people who attend regularly but never give, serve, or join a group)?</p></li><li><p>Which life stages or age cohorts are underrepresented relative to our community?</p></li></ul><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.baptistpress.com/resource-library/news/state-of-the-church-more-men-attending-than-women-volunteering-rebounding/">Barna's 2025 State of the Church</a> found weekly volunteering at 24 percent of U.S. adults, surpassing pre-pandemic 2019 levels. Gen Z and Millennials lead at 21 percent and 19 percent respectively.</p><p>A board that sees whether its own congregation matches that pattern or diverges from it has something to work with. Without integrated data, it has anecdotes.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> A leadership team that sees giving and attendance separately is missing the most important picture: the overlap.</p><h2>What Does Reliable Integrated Reporting Look Like</h2><p>We're deliberate about the word "reliable" because it has a specific meaning.</p><p>A report is reliable when it returns the same answer every time the same question is asked, regardless of who pulls it, and when the leadership team trusts the numbers enough to make decisions on them.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-2025-revealed-about-church-engagement/">The Pushpay 2025 year in review</a> found total giving increased 5 percent year over year across more than 14,000 customer churches. Recurring giving climbed 11 percent. There were 1.8 million first-time givers, with 59 percent using Apple Pay.</p><p>The churches getting the most value from those numbers aren't the ones with the most sophisticated dashboards. They're the ones who decided what the recurring opt-in rate, the first-time second-gift conversion rate, and the digital wallet adoption rate would mean for their own ministry and committed to tracking them.</p><p>Here's what we look for when evaluating a church's reporting setup:</p><ul><li><p>A single owner for each report, so the same person produces the same number every month.</p></li><li><p>A documented definition for every metric, written in plain language so a board member understands it.</p></li><li><p>A consistent reporting cadence: weekly for lapsed giver checks, monthly for the finance committee, quarterly for the board.</p></li><li><p>Source data that flows automatically from the system of record, with no spreadsheet copy-paste between the ChMS, the giving platform, and the report.</p></li><li><p>A change log, so if a definition shifts, the leadership team knows when and why.</p></li></ul><p>This is the discipline behind how to evaluate church management software without getting sold. The product question is downstream of the reporting question.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Reporting is reliable when the definition is documented, the owner is named, the cadence is fixed, and the data flows automatically from the source.</p><h2>What Should You Know About Digital Giving Channels and Their Data</h2><p>Digital giving isn't just a convenience question anymore. It's a data question.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.vancopayments.com/egiving/church-giving-trends">Vanco's 2025 Churchgoer Giving Study</a> found churchgoers are now evenly split between eGivers and traditional givers at 50 percent each. Recurring online givers contribute 120 percent more than non-recurring online givers.</p><p>That gap isn't marketing language. It's the durable economic reality of digital giving.</p><p>A church that hasn't stood up recurring giving or doesn't know what percentage of its donors are on it is leaving predictable revenue on the table and accepting more volatility in its monthly budget than it needs to.</p><p>Here's what the integrated data picture should tell you about digital giving:</p><ul><li><p>What percentage of giving units are on recurring, by age cohort.</p></li><li><p>What the average gift size is by channel: website, mobile app, text to give, in person, digital wallets.</p></li><li><p>How many recurring gifts failed this month due to expired cards or insufficient funds, and how many were recovered.</p></li><li><p>What percentage of first-time digital givers gave a second gift within 90 days.</p></li></ul><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Digital giving channels generate data, and that data is only useful when it flows into the same view as membership and engagement, not when it sits in a separate vendor dashboard.</p><h2>How Do You Know Whether Your Current Data Picture Is Healthy</h2><p>We ask church leaders to run a simple test.</p><p>Pull up the questions in each of the sections above and try to answer them with the systems you have in place. Note which questions get answered in under five minutes, which require an export and a spreadsheet, and which don't get answered at all without a manual data project.</p><p>The honest pattern we see: most churches answer four or five questions easily, eight or nine with effort, and another four or five not at all.</p><p>That gap is the integration project. It's rarely as big as it looks.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://news.lifeway.com/2025/03/18/half-of-churches-experiencing-post-pandemic-attendance-growth/">Lifeway Research's Becoming Five study</a> found 46 percent of churches reported 2024 giving higher than 2023, while 16 percent saw giving drop. The churches that acted on that movement are the ones who knew which donors changed their behavior and when.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The right test of your data picture isn't whether you have dashboards, it's whether your leadership team answers the questions it cares about on the day it needs them answered.</p><h2>Frequently Asked Questions</h2><h3>What is the most important single report a church finance committee should run every month?</h3><p>The lapsed giver report, segmented by giving frequency. For recurring givers, look at gifts that missed an expected cycle by 60 to 90 days. For annual givers, the window is 12 to 18 months. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://afpglobal.org/sites/default/files/attachments/resource/FEP_Report_Q4_2024_Final.pdf">Donor retention dropped to 42.9 percent in 2024</a>, so identifying lapse signals early is the highest leverage donor care activity.</p><h3>How do we know if our membership and giving data are integrated?</h3><p>The honest test is the five minute test. If your team answers "how many of our regular givers attended at least twice this quarter" without an export and a spreadsheet, the data is integrated for that question. If it requires merging two reports by hand, it's not integrated, even if the platforms claim to be.</p><h3>What share of church giving comes from recurring donors today?</h3><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://outbound.ministrybrands.com/hubfs/Ministry%20Brands%20Files/Annual%20Church%20Giving%20Report%202026/2026_Protestant_Annual_Church_Giving_Report.pdf">Recurring giving accounted for 35 percent of digital giving in 2025 according to Ministry Brands</a>, and <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://get.tithe.ly/blog/the-most-important-giving-trends-and-statistics">37 percent of total donations according to Tithely's 2025 trends report</a>. The figures vary by platform and church size, but the direction is consistent. Recurring is now a meaningful share of the offering, and tracking it as a distinct metric matters.</p><h3>How long should we wait before we consider a recurring giver lapsed?</h3><p>For most churches, a 60 to 90 day window from the last expected gift is the right trigger for a pastoral check-in. The threshold should be documented and applied consistently. The point isn't to flag every late payment as a crisis. It's to notice the quiet drift before it becomes a quiet exit.</p><h3>What should we do about digital wallets and giving data?</h3><p>Track adoption as a distinct metric. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-2025-revealed-about-church-engagement/">Pushpay reported 59 percent of first-time givers used Apple Pay in 2025</a>, and <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://outbound.ministrybrands.com/hubfs/Ministry%20Brands%20Files/Annual%20Church%20Giving%20Report%202026/2026_Protestant_Annual_Church_Giving_Report.pdf">Ministry Brands found digital wallet giving more than doubled to 5.1 percent of digital donations</a>. A church that doesn't know its digital wallet adoption rate doesn't know whether it's reaching younger givers or excluding them.</p><h3>How often should a leadership board review engagement data?</h3><p>We recommend quarterly for engagement and attendance trends, monthly for giving and recurring metrics, and weekly for lapsed giver flags handled at the staff level. The cadence matters more than the dashboard. Boards that review the same numbers every quarter develop pattern recognition that boards looking at fresh metrics each meeting never build.</p><h3>Do we need to replace our church management software to get integrated reporting?</h3><p>Often not. The first step is to define the reports you need and check whether your current systems support them with native integrations or scheduled exports. Many churches have more capability than they've configured. A clean reporting setup on existing software almost always beats a new platform with unclear definitions.</p><h3>What is the single biggest data mistake we see church boards make?</h3><p>Reporting on giving and attendance in isolation. The most important picture is the overlap: who attends and gives, who attends and doesn't give, who gives and rarely attends, and who has been doing both and recently stopped one. None of those segments are visible in a single channel report.</p><h2>Key Takeaways</h2><ul><li><p><strong>Questions answered, not dashboards produced:</strong> A healthy data picture is defined by the questions it answers. Reliable reporting means the same question returns the same answer every month, regardless of who pulls it.</p></li><li><p><strong>Lapsed giver detection drives retention:</strong> This is the highest leverage report most churches aren't running. Define lapse windows by giving segment and review the report weekly to turn a 42.9 percent industry retention rate into improvement opportunity.</p></li><li><p><strong>Recurring giving is now frontline, not back office:</strong> Recurring gifts account for 35 to 42 percent of digital giving, and recurring givers contribute 120 percent more than non-recurring online givers.</p></li><li><p><strong>The overlap view matters most:</strong> The most important integrated view is the overlap of giving, attendance, and engagement. Boards and leadership teams don't govern well when those data sets sit in separate reports.</p></li><li><p><strong>Discipline trumps tools:</strong> Reliable reporting requires a named owner, a documented definition, a fixed cadence, and source data that flows automatically. Tools matter less than discipline.</p></li><li><p><strong>Start with questions, not products:</strong> The integration project is usually smaller than churches think. Most have more reporting capability in current systems than they've configured. The right starting point is the list of questions, not the list of products.</p></li></ul><h2>Sources and References</h2><ul><li><p>Pushpay. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2025/04/30/3071371/0/en/Pushpay-s-2025-State-of-Church-Tech-Report-Reveals-Digital-Tools-are-Strengthening-Faith-Fueling-Connection-and-Shaping-the-Future-of-Ministry.html"><em>2025 State of Church Technology Report.</em></a> April 30, 2025.</p></li><li><p>Pushpay. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-2025-revealed-about-church-engagement/"><em>Behind the Numbers: What 2025 Revealed About Church Engagement.</em></a> January 26, 2026.</p></li><li><p>Ministry Brands. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://outbound.ministrybrands.com/hubfs/Ministry%20Brands%20Files/Annual%20Church%20Giving%20Report%202026/2026_Protestant_Annual_Church_Giving_Report.pdf"><em>2026 Annual Church Giving Report.</em></a> 2026.</p></li><li><p>Vanco. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.vancopayments.com/egiving/church-giving-trends"><em>A Decade of Church Giving Trends: 2015 to 2025.</em></a> 2025.</p></li><li><p>Barna Group, via Baptist Press. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.baptistpress.com/resource-library/news/state-of-the-church-more-men-attending-than-women-volunteering-rebounding/"><em>State of the Church: More Men Attending Than Women, Volunteering Rebounding.</em></a> March 25, 2025.</p></li><li><p>Lifeway Research. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://news.lifeway.com/2025/03/18/half-of-churches-experiencing-post-pandemic-attendance-growth/"><em>Half of Churches Experiencing Post-Pandemic Attendance Growth.</em></a> March 18, 2025.</p></li><li><p>Hartford Institute for Religion Research. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.covidreligionresearch.org/research/national-survey-research/signs-of-rebound-amid-uneven-recovery-the-changing-congregational-landscape/"><em>Signs of Rebound Amid Uneven Recovery: The Changing Congregational Landscape.</em></a> 2026.</p></li><li><p>Association of Fundraising Professionals. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://afpglobal.org/sites/default/files/attachments/resource/FEP_Report_Q4_2024_Final.pdf"><em>Fundraising Effectiveness Project Q4 2024 Quarterly Report.</em></a> 2025.</p></li><li><p>Tithely. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://get.tithe.ly/blog/the-most-important-giving-trends-and-statistics"><em>Church Giving Trends and Tithing Statistics, 2025 Data and Insights.</em></a> September 6, 2025.</p></li></ul><h2>Where We Go From Here</h2><p>Reliable giving and membership data isn't a software problem. It's a discipline problem, anchored in a written list of questions the leadership team agrees to answer the same way every month.</p><p>If your board, finance committee, or staff team has been asked questions about giving, retention, or engagement that nobody answered with confidence, that gap is the most useful map you have for the work ahead.</p><p>Our data, analytics, and church health metrics resource covers the broader framework this piece sits inside. If your ministry is working through what reliable reporting should look like in your context, and which questions matter most for your stage and size, we would be glad to think it through with you.</p><p>We offer no pressure consultations where we listen first, then share what we've learned helping ministries set up reporting they trust. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/consultation">Schedule a consultation</a>.</p>
+<p><strong>TL;DR:</strong> Most churches collect giving and membership data but struggle to answer basic leadership questions in real time. The problem isn't tools, it's integration. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://outbound.ministrybrands.com/hubfs/Ministry%20Brands%20Files/Annual%20Church%20Giving%20Report%202026/2026_Protestant_Annual_Church_Giving_Report.pdf">Recurring giving now accounts for 35 percent of digital giving</a>, but many finance committees can't tell you what share of their own budget comes from recurring donors. This article shows you which questions matter most, how to define metrics like lapsed givers, and what integrated reporting looks like when done right.</p><p><strong>Core Insights:</strong></p><ul><li><p>Finance committees need seven core reports: giving totals, recurring trends, lapsed givers (60 to 90 days for monthly donors), new giver conversion, and concentration risk.</p></li><li><p>Leadership boards need the overlap view: who gives and attends, who attends but doesn't give, who gives but rarely shows up, and who recently stopped both.</p></li><li><p>Lapsed giver detection is the highest leverage report most churches aren't running, especially when <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://afpglobal.org/sites/default/files/attachments/resource/FEP_Report_Q4_2024_Final.pdf">nonprofit donor retention sits at 42.9 percent</a>.</p></li><li><p>Integrated reporting means answering the same questions the same way every month without manual exports.</p></li><li><p>The integration project is usually smaller than churches think, most have more capability in current systems than they've configured.</p></li></ul><h2>Table of Contents</h2><ul><li><p>Why Most Churches Have Data but No Answers</p></li><li><p>What Questions Should Your Finance Committee Be Able to Answer</p></li><li><p>How Do You Define a Lapsed Giver</p></li><li><p>What Questions Should Your Elder Board or Leadership Team Ask</p></li><li><p>What Does Reliable Integrated Reporting Look Like</p></li><li><p>What Should You Know About Digital Giving Channels and Their Data</p></li><li><p>How Do You Know Whether Your Current Data Picture Is Healthy</p></li><li><p>Frequently Asked Questions</p></li><li><p>Key Takeaways</p></li></ul><p>A healthy data picture lets a finance committee, elder board, or staff team answer specific questions about who is giving, who is engaged, and who is drifting, in minutes rather than weeks. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2025/04/30/3071371/0/en/Pushpay-s-2025-State-of-Church-Tech-Report-Reveals-Digital-Tools-are-Strengthening-Faith-Fueling-Connection-and-Shaping-the-Future-of-Ministry.html">The 2025 State of Church Technology Report from Pushpay</a> found 86 percent of surveyed leaders now use church management software, and 70 percent say technology has increased generosity. The tools are in place. The reporting discipline often isn't.</p><h2>Why Most Churches Have Data but No Answers</h2><p>We work with finance committees and elder boards who collect plenty of data but struggle to answer basic questions about retention, engagement, and giving patterns.</p><p>The tools are in place. The reporting discipline often isn't.</p><p>Giving data sits in one platform. Attendance and small group data sit in another. Volunteer scheduling sits in a third. The only person who connects them is the staff member who knows how to export from each system.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2025/04/30/3071371/0/en/Pushpay-s-2025-State-of-Church-Tech-Report-Reveals-Digital-Tools-are-Strengthening-Faith-Fueling-Connection-and-Shaping-the-Future-of-Ministry.html">The 2025 State of Church Technology Report from Pushpay</a> found 86 percent of surveyed leaders now use church management software, and 70 percent say technology has increased generosity. The gap isn't tools. It's the reporting framework.</p><p>We see this clearly when data silos prevent the picture we're trying to assemble.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Reliable data isn't about having more dashboards, it's about answering the same leadership questions the same way every month without a manual export project each time.</p><h2>What Questions Should Your Finance Committee Be Able to Answer</h2><p>Finance committees ask the same questions almost every meeting. They're not exotic. They're the basic vitals of a church's financial picture.</p><p>Here's what we see asked most:</p><ul><li><p>What is our giving total this month, this quarter, and year to date compared to the same period last year?</p></li><li><p>How many giving units contributed this month, and how does that compare to attendance?</p></li><li><p>What percentage of total giving came from recurring gifts, and is that share growing or shrinking?</p></li><li><p>Who are our lapsed givers (people who gave consistently and then stopped within the last 60 to 180 days)?</p></li><li><p>Who are our new givers in the last 90 days, and how many gave a second time?</p></li><li><p>What is the concentration risk: what percentage of total giving comes from the top 10 percent of giving units?</p></li></ul><p>These aren't analytics questions. They're stewardship questions.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://outbound.ministrybrands.com/hubfs/Ministry%20Brands%20Files/Annual%20Church%20Giving%20Report%202026/2026_Protestant_Annual_Church_Giving_Report.pdf">The 2025 Ministry Brands data</a> shows recurring giving accounted for 35 percent of digital giving in 2025 and grew 5.4 percent year over year. If a finance committee doesn't know what share of its own giving is recurring, the committee is governing without a basic fact about how its budget is funded.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> A finance committee that doesn't define lapsed givers and pull a list of them isn't equipped to do donor care, and donor care is the most underused growth lever in church finance.</p><h2>How Do You Define a Lapsed Giver</h2><p>The definition has to fit the giving pattern. A lapsed annual donor and a lapsed monthly recurring donor aren't the same problem. Treating them the same produces a noisy, useless report.</p><p>The benchmark we work from comes from the broader nonprofit sector. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://afpglobal.org/sites/default/files/attachments/resource/FEP_Report_Q4_2024_Final.pdf">The Fundraising Effectiveness Project found donor retention at 42.9 percent in 2024</a>, the fifth consecutive year of decline. Repeat donors retain at 69.2 percent. New donors retain at 20 percent.</p><p>Lapse signals look different by segment:</p><ul><li><p><strong>Recurring monthly givers:</strong> Lapse window is 60 to 90 days from the last expected gift.</p></li><li><p><strong>Annual givers:</strong> Lapse window stretches to 12 to 18 months.</p></li><li><p><strong>First-time givers:</strong> The most important window is the next 90 days because the probability of a second gift drops sharply after that.</p></li></ul><p>The point of defining these windows isn't technical. It's so a pastor has a pastoral conversation, not a financial one, before a quiet drift becomes a permanent exit.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Lapsed giver definitions must be set per segment, written down, and applied consistently every month, otherwise the report tells you nothing about whether things are getting better or worse.</p><h2>What Questions Should Your Elder Board or Leadership Team Ask</h2><p>The elder board and senior leadership operate at a different altitude than the finance committee. They're looking at engagement health and the strength of the discipleship pipeline, not just the offering plate.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.covidreligionresearch.org/research/national-survey-research/signs-of-rebound-amid-uneven-recovery-the-changing-congregational-landscape/">The Hartford Institute's 2026 report on congregational rebound</a> found median in-person worship attendance has risen to 70, surpassing pre-pandemic levels. This marks the first positive gain in 25 years of tracking. Volunteer participation has rebounded, with 40 percent of congregants now volunteering regularly.</p><p>Boards that see those patterns in their own data, segmented by age, tenure, and ministry involvement, are positioned to act on them. Boards that don't are guessing.</p><p>Here are the questions a board should be able to ask:</p><ul><li><p>How many adults attended at least once this quarter, and how does that overlap with the giving roster?</p></li><li><p>What is the trend in weekly volunteer participation, and which ministries are growing or shrinking?</p></li><li><p>How many new members joined in the last 12 months, and how many are still active by month six?</p></li><li><p>Where are the gaps between attendance and engagement (people who attend regularly but never give, serve, or join a group)?</p></li><li><p>Which life stages or age cohorts are underrepresented relative to our community?</p></li></ul><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.baptistpress.com/resource-library/news/state-of-the-church-more-men-attending-than-women-volunteering-rebounding/">Barna's 2025 State of the Church</a> found weekly volunteering at 24 percent of U.S. adults, surpassing pre-pandemic 2019 levels. Gen Z and Millennials lead at 21 percent and 19 percent respectively.</p><p>A board that sees whether its own congregation matches that pattern or diverges from it has something to work with. Without integrated data, it has anecdotes.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> A leadership team that sees giving and attendance separately is missing the most important picture: the overlap.</p><h2>What Does Reliable Integrated Reporting Look Like</h2><p>We're deliberate about the word "reliable" because it has a specific meaning.</p><p>A report is reliable when it returns the same answer every time the same question is asked, regardless of who pulls it, and when the leadership team trusts the numbers enough to make decisions on them.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-2025-revealed-about-church-engagement/">The Pushpay 2025 year in review</a> found total giving increased 5 percent year over year across more than 14,000 customer churches. Recurring giving climbed 11 percent. There were 1.8 million first-time givers, with 59 percent using Apple Pay.</p><p>The churches getting the most value from those numbers aren't the ones with the most sophisticated dashboards. They're the ones who decided what the recurring opt-in rate, the first-time second-gift conversion rate, and the digital wallet adoption rate would mean for their own ministry and committed to tracking them.</p><p>Here's what we look for when evaluating a church's reporting setup:</p><ul><li><p>A single owner for each report, so the same person produces the same number every month.</p></li><li><p>A documented definition for every metric, written in plain language so a board member understands it.</p></li><li><p>A consistent reporting cadence: weekly for lapsed giver checks, monthly for the finance committee, quarterly for the board.</p></li><li><p>Source data that flows automatically from the system of record, with no spreadsheet copy-paste between the ChMS, the giving platform, and the report.</p></li><li><p>A change log, so if a definition shifts, the leadership team knows when and why.</p></li></ul><p>This is the discipline behind how to evaluate church management software without getting sold. The product question is downstream of the reporting question.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Reporting is reliable when the definition is documented, the owner is named, the cadence is fixed, and the data flows automatically from the source.</p><h2>What Should You Know About Digital Giving Channels and Their Data</h2><p>Digital giving isn't just a convenience question anymore. It's a data question.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.vancopayments.com/egiving/church-giving-trends">Vanco's 2025 Churchgoer Giving Study</a> found churchgoers are now evenly split between eGivers and traditional givers at 50 percent each. Recurring online givers contribute 120 percent more than non-recurring online givers.</p><p>That gap isn't marketing language. It's the durable economic reality of digital giving.</p><p>A church that hasn't stood up recurring giving or doesn't know what percentage of its donors are on it is leaving predictable revenue on the table and accepting more volatility in its monthly budget than it needs to.</p><p>Here's what the integrated data picture should tell you about digital giving:</p><ul><li><p>What percentage of giving units are on recurring, by age cohort.</p></li><li><p>What the average gift size is by channel: website, mobile app, text to give, in person, digital wallets.</p></li><li><p>How many recurring gifts failed this month due to expired cards or insufficient funds, and how many were recovered.</p></li><li><p>What percentage of first-time digital givers gave a second gift within 90 days.</p></li></ul><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> Digital giving channels generate data, and that data is only useful when it flows into the same view as membership and engagement, not when it sits in a separate vendor dashboard.</p><h2>How Do You Know Whether Your Current Data Picture Is Healthy</h2><p>We ask church leaders to run a simple test.</p><p>Pull up the questions in each of the sections above and try to answer them with the systems you have in place. Note which questions get answered in under five minutes, which require an export and a spreadsheet, and which don't get answered at all without a manual data project.</p><p>The honest pattern we see: most churches answer four or five questions easily, eight or nine with effort, and another four or five not at all.</p><p>That gap is the integration project. It's rarely as big as it looks.</p><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://news.lifeway.com/2025/03/18/half-of-churches-experiencing-post-pandemic-attendance-growth/">Lifeway Research's Becoming Five study</a> found 46 percent of churches reported 2024 giving higher than 2023, while 16 percent saw giving drop. The churches that acted on that movement are the ones who knew which donors changed their behavior and when.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The right test of your data picture isn't whether you have dashboards, it's whether your leadership team answers the questions it cares about on the day it needs them answered.</p><h2>Frequently Asked Questions</h2><h3>What is the most important single report a church finance committee should run every month?</h3><p>The lapsed giver report, segmented by giving frequency. For recurring givers, look at gifts that missed an expected cycle by 60 to 90 days. For annual givers, the window is 12 to 18 months. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://afpglobal.org/sites/default/files/attachments/resource/FEP_Report_Q4_2024_Final.pdf">Donor retention dropped to 42.9 percent in 2024</a>, so identifying lapse signals early is the highest leverage donor care activity.</p><h3>How do we know if our membership and giving data are integrated?</h3><p>The honest test is the five minute test. If your team answers "how many of our regular givers attended at least twice this quarter" without an export and a spreadsheet, the data is integrated for that question. If it requires merging two reports by hand, it's not integrated, even if the platforms claim to be.</p><h3>What share of church giving comes from recurring donors today?</h3><p><a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://outbound.ministrybrands.com/hubfs/Ministry%20Brands%20Files/Annual%20Church%20Giving%20Report%202026/2026_Protestant_Annual_Church_Giving_Report.pdf">Recurring giving accounted for 35 percent of digital giving in 2025 according to Ministry Brands</a>, and <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://get.tithe.ly/blog/the-most-important-giving-trends-and-statistics">37 percent of total donations according to Tithely's 2025 trends report</a>. The figures vary by platform and church size, but the direction is consistent. Recurring is now a meaningful share of the offering, and tracking it as a distinct metric matters.</p><h3>How long should we wait before we consider a recurring giver lapsed?</h3><p>For most churches, a 60 to 90 day window from the last expected gift is the right trigger for a pastoral check-in. The threshold should be documented and applied consistently. The point isn't to flag every late payment as a crisis. It's to notice the quiet drift before it becomes a quiet exit.</p><h3>What should we do about digital wallets and giving data?</h3><p>Track adoption as a distinct metric. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-2025-revealed-about-church-engagement/">Pushpay reported 59 percent of first-time givers used Apple Pay in 2025</a>, and <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://outbound.ministrybrands.com/hubfs/Ministry%20Brands%20Files/Annual%20Church%20Giving%20Report%202026/2026_Protestant_Annual_Church_Giving_Report.pdf">Ministry Brands found digital wallet giving more than doubled to 5.1 percent of digital donations</a>. A church that doesn't know its digital wallet adoption rate doesn't know whether it's reaching younger givers or excluding them.</p><h3>How often should a leadership board review engagement data?</h3><p>We recommend quarterly for engagement and attendance trends, monthly for giving and recurring metrics, and weekly for lapsed giver flags handled at the staff level. The cadence matters more than the dashboard. Boards that review the same numbers every quarter develop pattern recognition that boards looking at fresh metrics each meeting never build.</p><h3>Do we need to replace our church management software to get integrated reporting?</h3><p>Often not. The first step is to define the reports you need and check whether your current systems support them with native integrations or scheduled exports. Many churches have more capability than they've configured. A clean reporting setup on existing software almost always beats a new platform with unclear definitions.</p><h3>What is the single biggest data mistake we see church boards make?</h3><p>Reporting on giving and attendance in isolation. The most important picture is the overlap: who attends and gives, who attends and doesn't give, who gives and rarely attends, and who has been doing both and recently stopped one. None of those segments are visible in a single channel report.</p><h2>Key Takeaways</h2><ul><li><p><strong>Questions answered, not dashboards produced:</strong> A healthy data picture is defined by the questions it answers. Reliable reporting means the same question returns the same answer every month, regardless of who pulls it.</p></li><li><p><strong>Lapsed giver detection drives retention:</strong> This is the highest leverage report most churches aren't running. Define lapse windows by giving segment and review the report weekly to turn a 42.9 percent industry retention rate into improvement opportunity.</p></li><li><p><strong>Recurring giving is now frontline, not back office:</strong> Recurring gifts account for 35 to 42 percent of digital giving, and recurring givers contribute 120 percent more than non-recurring online givers.</p></li><li><p><strong>The overlap view matters most:</strong> The most important integrated view is the overlap of giving, attendance, and engagement. Boards and leadership teams don't govern well when those data sets sit in separate reports.</p></li><li><p><strong>Discipline trumps tools:</strong> Reliable reporting requires a named owner, a documented definition, a fixed cadence, and source data that flows automatically. Tools matter less than discipline.</p></li><li><p><strong>Start with questions, not products:</strong> The integration project is usually smaller than churches think. Most have more reporting capability in current systems than they've configured. The right starting point is the list of questions, not the list of products.</p></li></ul><h2>Sources and References</h2><ul><li><p>Pushpay. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2025/04/30/3071371/0/en/Pushpay-s-2025-State-of-Church-Tech-Report-Reveals-Digital-Tools-are-Strengthening-Faith-Fueling-Connection-and-Shaping-the-Future-of-Ministry.html"><em>2025 State of Church Technology Report.</em></a> April 30, 2025.</p></li><li><p>Pushpay. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-2025-revealed-about-church-engagement/"><em>Behind the Numbers: What 2025 Revealed About Church Engagement.</em></a> January 26, 2026.</p></li><li><p>Ministry Brands. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://outbound.ministrybrands.com/hubfs/Ministry%20Brands%20Files/Annual%20Church%20Giving%20Report%202026/2026_Protestant_Annual_Church_Giving_Report.pdf"><em>2026 Annual Church Giving Report.</em></a> 2026.</p></li><li><p>Vanco. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.vancopayments.com/egiving/church-giving-trends"><em>A Decade of Church Giving Trends: 2015 to 2025.</em></a> 2025.</p></li><li><p>Barna Group, via Baptist Press. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.baptistpress.com/resource-library/news/state-of-the-church-more-men-attending-than-women-volunteering-rebounding/"><em>State of the Church: More Men Attending Than Women, Volunteering Rebounding.</em></a> March 25, 2025.</p></li><li><p>Lifeway Research. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://news.lifeway.com/2025/03/18/half-of-churches-experiencing-post-pandemic-attendance-growth/"><em>Half of Churches Experiencing Post-Pandemic Attendance Growth.</em></a> March 18, The Changing Congregational Landscape.</em></a> 2026.</p></li><li><p>Association of Fundraising Professionals. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" 2025.</p></li><li><p>Tithely. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://get.tithe.ly/blog/the-most-important-giving-trends-and-statistics"><em>Church Giving Trends and Tithing Statistics, 2025 Data and Insights.</em></a> September 6, 2025.</p></li></ul><h2>Where We Go From Here</h2><p>Reliable giving and membership data isn't a software problem. It's a discipline problem, anchored in a written list of questions the leadership team agrees to answer the same way every month.</p><p>If your board, finance committee, or staff team has been asked questions about giving, retention, or engagement that nobody answered with confidence, that gap is the most useful map you have for the work ahead.</p><p>Our data, analytics, and church health metrics resource covers the broader framework this piece sits inside. If your ministry is working through what reliable reporting should look like in your context, and which questions matter most for your stage and size, we would be glad to think it through with you.</p><p>We offer no pressure consultations where we listen first, then share what we've learned helping ministries set up reporting they trust. <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://goodshepherdinsights.com/contact/">Schedule a consultation</a>.</p>

-<p><strong>TL;DR:</strong> A Ministry Action System is a new category of church software that aggregates data from your ChMS, giving platform, and communication tools into a single engagement view — then surfaces who is drifting and what your team should do about it. Pushpay's acquisition of Nurture.io in April 2026 introduced the category to 14,000+ U.S. churches. This post explains what a MAS is, how it differs from a ChMS, and the three questions every church leader should ask before adding one to their stack.</p><p><strong>Core Insights:</strong></p><ul><li><p>A Ministry Action System (MAS) sits above your existing tools rather than replacing them. It aggregates data; it does not store it.</p></li><li><p>The average church uses 7 to 10 different technology tools, according to Pushpay's acquisition announcement. A MAS is designed to solve the data silo problem that creates.</p></li><li><p>The term "Ministry Action System" was coined by Nurture.io and Pushpay. It is not yet an industry-standard category label. Other vendors are building similar capabilities under different names.</p></li><li><p>Only 9% of church leaders say technology primarily helps with discipleship, per the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-technology-mission/">2026 Barna/Pushpay State of Church Tech report</a>. A MAS is designed to close that gap by routing pastoral follow-up, not just tracking attendance.</p></li><li><p>The same AI layer that makes a MAS powerful also raises data privacy questions your existing governance may not yet address.</p></li><li><p>Pushpay is not the only vendor entering this space. We track four platforms with comparable capabilities and what distinguishes each.</p></li></ul><hr /><p>A Ministry Action System (MAS) is a layer of software that sits above a church's existing technology tools — its ChMS, giving platform, and communication systems — and aggregates their data into a single unified profile for every person in the congregation. Unlike a church management system, which stores and organizes records, a MAS is designed to surface real-time disengagement signals and route specific pastoral follow-up actions to the right staff member at the right moment. Pushpay's <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/04/22/3279027/0/en/pushpay-announces-acquisition-of-nurture-io-adding-pastoral-care-and-engagement-intelligence-for-churches.html">April 2026 acquisition of Nurture.io</a> formally introduced the category to the U.S. church market.</p><h2>Table of Contents</h2><ul><li><p>Why Existing Church Technology Leaves a Data Gap</p></li><li><p>What a Ministry Action System Actually Does</p></li><li><p>How a Ministry Action System Differs from a Church Management System</p></li><li><p>Which Vendors Are Building Ministry Action System Capabilities in 2026</p></li><li><p>Three Questions to Ask Before Adding a Ministry Action System to Your Stack</p></li><li><p>What the Pushpay Staq Transformation Means for Churches Already on Pushpay</p></li><li><p>Frequently Asked Questions</p></li><li><p>Key Takeaways</p></li><li><p>Sources and References</p></li><li><p>Where We Go From Here</p></li></ul><h2>Why Existing Church Technology Leaves a Data Gap</h2><p>Most churches rely on separate tools for attendance, giving, and communication, so no single system knows whether someone is drifting toward disengagement. The Ministry Action System category exists specifically to close that gap by sitting above existing tools and unifying their signals.</p><p>The structural challenge is one every church administrator knows well: your ChMS holds attendance records, your giving platform tracks generosity trends, your communication tool logs who opened which email. Each system does its job. None of them talk to each other. So when a family quietly stops showing up, there is rarely a moment when a staff member receives a clear, timely signal that the relationship needs attention.</p><p>The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/04/22/3279027/0/en/pushpay-announces-acquisition-of-nurture-io-adding-pastoral-care-and-engagement-intelligence-for-churches.html">April 2026 Pushpay acquisition of Nurture.io</a> put precise language around a problem ministry leaders have felt for years. The announcement noted that the average church uses 7 to 10 different technology tools. That number is the data gap made concrete. When data lives in that many places, every staff member is working from an incomplete picture.</p><p>For context, we cover <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://app.pressmaster.ai/article/edit/88f11325-c7f5-4f17-8e03-0454492ef65d">our complete guide to what AI-powered tools are actually doing inside church management software in 2026</a> in a dedicated pillar. The Ministry Action System category is part of that larger shift, but it deserves its own definition before church leaders can evaluate it clearly.</p><p>The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-technology-mission/">2026 Barna/Pushpay State of Church Tech report</a> found that only 9% of church leaders say technology primarily helps with discipleship. That figure is not a technology failure. It reflects what technology was built to do: manage records, process payments, and communicate at scale. A Ministry Action System is built for the outcome those tools were not designed to produce.</p><p>As Carey Nieuwhof observed in a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://relevantmagazine.com/faith/carey-nieuwhof-the-church-is-having-the-wrong-ai-conversation/">June 2026 piece for RELEVANT Magazine</a>, churches are "having the wrong AI conversation" by focusing on efficiency-level features rather than systemic impact. The Ministry Action System category is an attempt to reframe that conversation around pastoral outcomes rather than administrative convenience.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The data gap is not a software failure. It is a structural problem caused by purpose-built tools that were never designed to talk to each other. A Ministry Action System addresses the gap rather than replacing any individual tool.</p><h2>What a Ministry Action System Actually Does</h2><p>A MAS aggregates data from your existing platforms, builds a unified engagement profile for each person in your congregation, scores engagement health in real time, and routes specific follow-up tasks to staff. It is designed to surface who is drifting and who is overdue for connection before they disappear entirely.</p><p>The mechanics work in layers. First, the MAS connects to your existing platforms via integrations. Nurture.io, for example, integrates with <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">more than 17 industry platforms</a>, pulling in attendance, giving, volunteer activity, and communication engagement. Second, the system builds a composite engagement profile for each person. A family that attended regularly, gave consistently, and volunteered occasionally now has a health score based on all three signals combined, not just one in isolation. Third, when that score drops, the system routes a specific action to a specific staff member: a phone call, a pastoral visit, a personal note.</p><p>Luke Denton, founder of Nurture.io, described the goal this way in a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.nurture.io/blog-posts/nurture-is-joining-pushpay">post on the Nurture blog</a>: "Ministry leaders shouldn't just have data about their congregation. They should have the insight, the context, and the tools to know every person by name." That framing is vendor voice, and we note it as such. But it captures the category's design intent accurately.</p><p>A June 16, 2026 <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-to-expect-from-pushpays-staq-transformation/">blog post from Pushpay CPO Gruia Pitigoi-Aron</a> made a related point honestly: only 1 in 4 Pushpay churches currently use Check-In, Volunteer Schedules, and Process Queues together. The MAS layer is partly designed to close that adoption gap by making cross-platform action visible from a single interface.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> A MAS does not replace your ChMS, your giving platform, or your communication tools. It connects them. If your existing tools are doing their jobs, a MAS reads their output and tells you what to do next.</p><h2>How a Ministry Action System Differs from a Church Management System</h2><p>A ChMS is a system of record: it stores member data, tracks attendance, manages giving, and handles event registration. A MAS is a system of action: it reads across those records and tells your team who needs a phone call, a visit, or a card this week.</p><p>The distinction matters because the two categories are often conflated in conversations about church technology. Church management systems have existed for decades and most ministry leaders have a working familiarity with them. The Ministry Action System category is new enough that the vocabulary is still being defined.</p><p>The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/04/22/3279027/0/en/pushpay-announces-acquisition-of-nurture-io-adding-pastoral-care-and-engagement-intelligence-for-churches.html">Pushpay acquisition press release</a> described the ambition as delivering "an intelligent pastoral care layer" on top of existing infrastructure. That phrase captures the functional difference. The ChMS is the infrastructure. The MAS is the intelligence layer that reads it.</p><p>Ed Stetzer framed a related dynamic in a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/addressing-loneliness-the-impact-of-ai-on-church-life-and-leadership-with-corey-alderin-ed-stetzer-and-kenny-jahng/">June 9, 2026 ChurchTechToday podcast</a>, noting that effective AI tools in ministry need to pursue both efficiency and deeper human connection. A ChMS optimizes for administrative efficiency. A MAS is built for the human connection side: it exists so that a pastor can walk into a conversation knowing why a specific family needs care right now.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The most useful way to think about the distinction is this: your ChMS answers "who is in our congregation?" A Ministry Action System answers "who needs attention from our team right now?"</p><h2>Which Vendors Are Building Ministry Action System Capabilities in 2026</h2><p>Pushpay with Nurture is the most prominent example, but three other vendors have launched comparable AI engagement layers in 2026, each with different integration depth and data governance approaches. The category is forming, not finished.</p><p>The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">June 8, 2026 GlobeNewswire announcement</a> confirmed that Nurture is now accessible directly from the Pushpay dashboard with a single point of entry. Single sign-on and deeper workflow integrations are on the roadmap. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-to-expect-from-pushpays-staq-transformation/">CPO blog post from June 16</a> laid out five committed deliverables by December 2026: Groups optimization (June), AI Involvement Summary (August), Single Staq Giving (September), Staq Data Lake (September), and expanded Process Queues (September).</p><p>Beyond Pushpay, three other platforms have moved into adjacent territory this year. ACST launched its Ministry Platform AI Pioneer Program in May 2026, as reported by <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">BusinessWire</a>, using an MCP-based architecture that allows plain-English queries across congregational data. Subsplash rolled out Trends AI in March 2026 with more than 25 chart types drawing from its engagement data hub. Rock RMS, paired with the 9 Embers Agent 9 integration, offers plain-English queries across families, attendance, giving, and serving records.</p><p>Here is the current landscape as we track it:</p><p>VendorProductStatus as of June 2026Key differentiatorPushpay + NurtureStaq MAS / NurtureLive — dashboard integration June 8, AI features August 2026Broadest integration depth (17+ platform connections)ACST (Realm + Ministry Platform)Ministry Platform AIPioneer Program live May 2026 (50 churches)MCP-based plain-English queryingSubsplashTrends AILive March 2026Consolidated analytics across Subsplash productsRock RMS + 9 EmbersAgent 9Live 2026Open-source ecosystem; cross-domain plain-English queriesPlanning CenterNo announcement—No MAS equivalent announced as of June 2026BreezeNo announcement—No MAS equivalent announced as of June 2026</p><p>Two things worth noting about this table. First, Planning Center and Breeze serve a large share of the church market and neither has announced a comparable product. Churches on those platforms are not excluded from the MAS conversation, but they would need to evaluate third-party integrations rather than a native offering. Second, the capabilities above are still evolving rapidly. We will update this comparison as announcements land.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> "Ministry Action System" is Pushpay and Nurture's term for a category that other vendors are building under different names. Before deciding whether to add a MAS to your stack, we recommend evaluating which tool integrates cleanest with your existing ChMS, regardless of what it is called.</p><h2>Three Questions to Ask Before Adding a Ministry Action System to Your Stack</h2><p>Before committing to any Ministry Action System, church leaders should ask about data ownership, integration depth with their existing tools, and how the AI layer handles sensitive pastoral data. These questions apply whether you are evaluating Nurture, a competing platform, or any future offering in this emerging category.</p><p>The governance dimension is not optional. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">2026 Barna/Pushpay State of Church Tech report</a> found that 83% of church leaders say data privacy in AI is a concern, yet only 5% of churches have a formal AI policy in place. A Ministry Action System that aggregates data across 17 integrations sits directly in that gap.</p><p>Joe Koehling, CEO of ACST, put the tension plainly in the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">May 7, 2026 BusinessWire announcement</a>: "What we heard from churches was clear: they want the benefits of AI, but they do not want to compromise trust, stewardship, or congregational privacy to get there." That describes the due diligence challenge exactly.</p><p>We recommend asking three questions before any MAS evaluation conversation:</p><p><strong>1. Who owns our congregational data, and can we export it if we cancel?</strong></p><p>A MAS that aggregates data from 17 integrations creates a consolidated profile of your congregation that did not exist before in any single place. Understand upfront what happens to that aggregated data if you switch platforms. The individual source systems (your ChMS, your giving platform) may still hold their records, but the unified profile that the MAS created is a new data asset. Clarify ownership in writing before signing.</p><p><strong>2. Does this tool integrate with our current ChMS, or does it require a platform migration?</strong></p><p>Nurture's 17 integrations and the other vendor offerings listed above have different levels of compatibility with different ChMS platforms. Churches on Planning Center or Breeze should verify the integration roadmap before committing. A MAS that requires switching your ChMS to function is a significantly larger decision than adding an engagement layer to your existing stack.</p><p><strong>3. What governance do we have in place for AI processing of pastoral data?</strong></p><p>Per the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">2026 Barna/Pushpay State of Church Tech report</a>, 83% of church leaders say data privacy in AI is a concern, but only 5% of churches have a formal AI policy. If your church has not addressed this yet, adding a MAS before you have a governance framework in place creates compounded risk. Pastoral data — attendance patterns, giving history, small group participation — is among the most sensitive information a church holds. A formal policy does not need to be long, but it needs to exist before that data begins flowing through an AI scoring layer.</p><p>We covered <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://app.pressmaster.ai/article/edit/f9333ef6-911c-479f-8229-3e6df59bdc61">the five questions to ask your ChMS vendor about AI before renewal</a> in a dedicated post. Many of those questions apply directly to Ministry Action System evaluation as well.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The governance question is the most commonly overlooked. Churches that have not yet addressed AI data policy will find that the MAS evaluation process surfaces the gap quickly — often at the contract stage.</p><h2>What the Pushpay Staq Transformation Means for Churches Already on Pushpay</h2><p>Pushpay's June 16, 2026 roadmap announcement from CPO Gruia Pitigoi-Aron lays out five specific deliverables by December 2026 that move the combined Pushpay/Staq/Nurture platform toward a full Ministry Action System. If your church is currently on Pushpay, this section explains what is coming and what it will require from your team.</p><p>The starting point is the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">June 8, 2026 integration milestone</a>: Nurture is now accessible directly from the Pushpay dashboard. For churches that already use both products, the immediate change is a single point of entry. The roadmap from there, per the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-to-expect-from-pushpays-staq-transformation/">CPO blog post</a>, moves in five phases:</p><ul><li><p><strong>June 2026:</strong> Groups optimization</p></li><li><p><strong>August 2026:</strong> AI Involvement Summary (the first AI-powered engagement scoring feature)</p></li><li><p><strong>September 2026:</strong> Single Staq Giving</p></li><li><p><strong>September 2026:</strong> Staq Data Lake</p></li><li><p><strong>September 2026:</strong> Expanded Process Queues</p></li></ul><p>The CPO's own acknowledgment that only 1 in 4 Pushpay churches currently use Check-In, Volunteer Schedules, and Process Queues together is worth sitting with. The Staq Transformation is partly a product consolidation initiative designed to make existing features more accessible. Churches that have not yet used the full Pushpay feature set will find the roadmap relevant beyond the Nurture integration specifically.</p><p>What this means in practical terms: churches already on Pushpay can begin evaluating Nurture now through the existing dashboard integration. The AI-powered engagement scoring features are four to five months out. That window is useful for the governance work described in the previous section, particularly building or updating a church AI policy before the AI Involvement Summary goes live.</p><p>We offer <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://app.pressmaster.ai/article/edit/d4748e17-2148-4ced-af47-a1a2232d0250">our guide to rolling out AI in church management software without breaking staff trust</a> as a companion resource for churches working through that preparation.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> If your church is on Pushpay and you have not yet explored Nurture, the June 8 integration milestone means it is now accessible directly from your existing dashboard. The AI-powered features arrive in August and September 2026. That gives your team roughly two months to evaluate whether your governance framework is ready.</p><h2>Frequently Asked Questions</h2><h3>What is a Ministry Action System in simple terms?</h3><p>A Ministry Action System is software that connects your existing church tools — your ChMS, giving platform, and communication system — and tells your team who needs pastoral attention right now. Unlike a church management system, which stores records, a MAS reads across those records and surfaces disengagement signals so your staff can act before someone drifts away entirely. The term was introduced to the U.S. church market by <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/04/22/3279027/0/en/pushpay-announces-acquisition-of-nurture-io-adding-pastoral-care-and-engagement-intelligence-for-churches.html">Pushpay's April 2026 acquisition of Nurture.io</a>.</p><h3>Does my church need a Ministry Action System if we already have a ChMS?</h3><p>Not every church needs one. A MAS adds the most value when a church already has a functioning ChMS, a dedicated pastoral follow-up team, and enough data volume that manual review is creating gaps. Smaller churches with a single pastor who knows every member by name are unlikely to see significant benefit from the additional complexity and cost. The return on investment scales with congregation size, data quality, and staff capacity to act on the signals the system surfaces.</p><h3>How does Nurture.io work with Pushpay?</h3><p>As of <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">June 8, 2026</a>, Nurture.io is accessible directly from the Pushpay dashboard for churches that use both platforms. Pushpay acquired Nurture in April 2026. The current integration provides a single point of entry to Nurture from within the Pushpay ChMS. Deeper integrations including single sign-on and the ability to assign a person to a Nurture Process Queue from within the ChMS are on the roadmap for later in 2026, per the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-to-expect-from-pushpays-staq-transformation/">CPO blog post</a>.</p><h3>What data does a Ministry Action System collect about our congregation?</h3><p>A MAS aggregates data your church is already collecting across existing platforms: attendance records, giving history, volunteer engagement, small group participation, and communication responses. Nurture, for example, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">integrates with more than 17 church technology platforms</a>. No new data collection occurs in that sense. The MAS reads what is already there. The critical governance question is who has access to that aggregated profile and what happens to the data if you switch vendors.</p><h3>What is the Pushpay Staq Transformation?</h3><p>The Staq Transformation is Pushpay's publicly announced platform roadmap, detailed by <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-to-expect-from-pushpays-staq-transformation/">CPO Gruia Pitigoi-Aron in a June 16, 2026 blog post</a>. It consolidates Pushpay's ChMS, giving, and Nurture engagement tools into a unified platform. Five deliverables are committed by December 2026: Groups optimization (June), an AI Involvement Summary (August), Single Staq Giving (September), a Staq Data Lake (September), and expanded Process Queues (September).</p><h3>Is a Ministry Action System the same as a CRM?</h3><p>They share some structural similarities, but the purposes are different. A CRM is designed for sales pipeline management — tracking the path from prospect to customer. A MAS is designed for pastoral care and congregational engagement — tracking the health of an existing relationship within a faith community. The data model, the actions it surfaces, and the outcomes it measures are all oriented around ministry rather than commercial conversion.</p><h3>Which churches should consider adding a Ministry Action System in 2026?</h3><p>Churches that will benefit most are those with congregations of roughly 200 or more attenders, an existing ChMS with reasonably clean data, and dedicated pastoral follow-up staff who currently rely on intuition or manual reports to identify who needs connection. If your team is regularly asking "who haven't we seen in a while?" and cannot answer that question systematically with your current tools, a Ministry Action System is worth evaluating.</p><h2>Key Takeaways</h2><ul><li><p><strong>A Ministry Action System sits above your existing technology, not instead of it.</strong> It aggregates data from your ChMS, giving platform, and communication tools into a unified engagement view — then routes specific pastoral follow-up actions to your team.</p></li><li><p><strong>The category was formally introduced to the U.S. church market in April 2026</strong> when Pushpay acquired Nurture.io. The term "Ministry Action System" is Pushpay and Nurture's own language; competing vendors are building similar capabilities under different names.</p></li><li><p><strong>The core problem a MAS solves is pastoral capacity, not administrative efficiency.</strong> Per the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-technology-mission/">2026 Barna/Pushpay State of Church Tech report</a>, only 9% of church leaders say technology primarily helps with discipleship. A MAS is designed to route specific pastoral follow-up actions, not just surface data.</p></li><li><p><strong>Data governance questions matter before you add a MAS.</strong> 83% of church leaders say AI data privacy is a concern, yet only 5% have formal AI policies. A MAS that aggregates 17 integrations raises those concerns directly.</p></li><li><p><strong>If your church is on Pushpay, Nurture is now accessible directly from your dashboard</strong> as of June 8, 2026. Deeper AI-powered features arrive on the roadmap in August and September 2026. Review <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://app.pressmaster.ai/article/edit/88f11325-c7f5-4f17-8e03-0454492ef65d">the full landscape of AI features across major ChMS vendors in 2026</a> to understand how the Staq Transformation fits the broader picture.</p></li><li><p><strong>Not every church needs a Ministry Action System.</strong> The value scales with congregation size, staff capacity, and data quality. Start by asking whether your current tools give your team a systematic answer to "who needs attention from our team this week?"</p></li><li><p><strong>The MAS category is forming, not We track four platforms with comparable capabilities and what distinguishes each.</p></li></ul><hr /><p>A Ministry Action System (MAS) is a layer of software that sits above a church's existing technology tools — its ChMS, giving platform, and communication systems — and aggregates their data into a single unified profile for every person in the congregation.
