Pushpay's <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/04/22/3279027/0/en/pushpay-announces-acquisition-of-nurture-io-adding-pastoral-care-and-engagement-intelligence-for-churches.html">April 2026 acquisition of Nurture.io</a> formally introduced the category to the U.S. church market.</p><h2>Table of Contents</h2><ul><li><p>Why Existing Church Technology Leaves a Data Gap</p></li><li><p>What a Ministry Action System Actually Does</p></li><li><p>How a Ministry Action System Differs from a Church Management System</p></li><li><p>Which Vendors Are Building Ministry Action System Capabilities in 2026</p></li><li><p>Three Questions to Ask Before Adding a Ministry Action System to Your Stack</p></li><li><p>What the Pushpay Staq Transformation Means for Churches Already on Pushpay</p></li><li><p>Frequently Asked Questions</p></li><li><p>Key Takeaways</p></li><li><p>Sources and References</p></li><li><p>Where We Go From Here</p></li></ul><h2>Why Existing Church Technology Leaves a Data Gap</h2><p>Most churches rely on separate tools for attendance, giving, and communication, so no single system knows whether someone is drifting toward disengagement. The Ministry Action System category exists specifically to close that gap by sitting above existing tools and unifying their signals.</p><p>The structural challenge is one every church administrator knows well: your ChMS holds attendance records, your giving platform tracks generosity trends, your communication tool logs who opened which email. Each system does its job. None of them talk to each other. So when a family quietly stops showing up, there is rarely a moment when a staff member receives a clear, timely signal that the relationship needs attention.</p><p>The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/04/22/3279027/0/en/pushpay-announces-acquisition-of-nurture-io-adding-pastoral-care-and-engagement-intelligence-for-churches.html">April 2026 Pushpay acquisition of Nurture.io</a> put precise language around a problem ministry leaders have felt for years. The announcement noted that the average church uses 7 to 10 different technology tools. That number is the data gap made concrete. When data lives in that many places, every staff member is working from an incomplete picture.</p><p>For context, we cover <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://app.pressmaster.ai/article/edit/88f11325-c7f5-4f17-8e03-0454492ef65d">our complete guide to what AI-powered tools are actually doing inside church management software in 2026</a> in a dedicated pillar. The Ministry Action System category is part of that larger shift, but it deserves its own definition before church leaders can evaluate it clearly.</p><p>The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-technology-mission/">2026 Barna/Pushpay State of Church Tech report</a> found that only 9% of church leaders say technology primarily helps with discipleship. That figure is not a technology failure. It reflects what technology was built to do: manage records, process payments, and communicate at scale. A Ministry Action System is built for the outcome those tools were not designed to produce.</p><p>As Carey Nieuwhof observed in a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://relevantmagazine.com/faith/carey-nieuwhof-the-church-is-having-the-wrong-ai-conversation/">June 2026 piece for RELEVANT Magazine</a>, churches are "having the wrong AI conversation" by focusing on efficiency-level features rather than systemic impact. The Ministry Action System category is an attempt to reframe that conversation around pastoral outcomes rather than administrative convenience.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The data gap is not a software failure. It is a structural problem caused by purpose-built tools that were never designed to talk to each other. A Ministry Action System addresses the gap rather than replacing any individual tool.</p><h2>What a Ministry Action System Actually Does</h2><p>A MAS aggregates data from your existing platforms, builds a unified engagement profile for each person in your congregation, scores engagement health in real time, and routes specific follow-up tasks to staff. It is designed to surface who is drifting and who is overdue for connection before they disappear entirely.</p><p>The mechanics work in layers. First, the MAS connects to your existing platforms via integrations. Nurture.io, for example, integrates with <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">more than 17 industry platforms</a>, pulling in attendance, giving, volunteer activity, and communication engagement. Second, the system builds a composite engagement profile for each person. A family that attended regularly, gave consistently, and volunteered occasionally now has a health score based on all three signals combined, not just one in isolation. Third, when that score drops, the system routes a specific action to a specific staff member: a phone call, a pastoral visit, a personal note.</p><p>Luke Denton, founder of Nurture.io, described the goal this way in a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.nurture.io/blog-posts/nurture-is-joining-pushpay">post on the Nurture blog</a>: "Ministry leaders shouldn't just have data about their congregation. They should have the insight, the context, and the tools to know every person by name." That framing is vendor voice, and we note it as such. But it captures the category's design intent accurately.</p><p>A June 16, 2026 <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-to-expect-from-pushpays-staq-transformation/">blog post from Pushpay CPO Gruia Pitigoi-Aron</a> made a related point honestly: only 1 in 4 Pushpay churches currently use Check-In, Volunteer Schedules, and Process Queues together. The MAS layer is partly designed to close that adoption gap by making cross-platform action visible from a single interface.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> A MAS does not replace your ChMS, your giving platform, or your communication tools. It connects them. If your existing tools are doing their jobs, a MAS reads their output and tells you what to do next.</p><h2>How a Ministry Action System Differs from a Church Management System</h2><p>A ChMS is a system of record: it stores member data, tracks attendance, manages giving, and handles event registration. A MAS is a system of action: it reads across those records and tells your team who needs a phone call, a visit, or a card this week.</p><p>The distinction matters because the two categories are often conflated in conversations about church technology. Church management systems have existed for decades and most ministry leaders have a working familiarity with them. The Ministry Action System category is new enough that the vocabulary is still being defined.</p><p>The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/04/22/3279027/0/en/pushpay-announces-acquisition-of-nurture-io-adding-pastoral-care-and-engagement-intelligence-for-churches.html">Pushpay acquisition press release</a> described the ambition as delivering "an intelligent pastoral care layer" on top of existing infrastructure. That phrase captures the functional difference. The ChMS is the infrastructure. The MAS is the intelligence layer that reads it.</p><p>Ed Stetzer framed a related dynamic in a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://churchtechtoday.com/addressing-loneliness-the-impact-of-ai-on-church-life-and-leadership-with-corey-alderin-ed-stetzer-and-kenny-jahng/">June 9, 2026 ChurchTechToday podcast</a>, noting that effective AI tools in ministry need to pursue both efficiency and deeper human connection. A ChMS optimizes for administrative efficiency. A MAS is built for the human connection side: it exists so that a pastor can walk into a conversation knowing why a specific family needs care right now.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The most useful way to think about the distinction is this: your ChMS answers "who is in our congregation?" A Ministry Action System answers "who needs attention from our team right now?"</p><h2>Which Vendors Are Building Ministry Action System Capabilities in 2026</h2><p>Pushpay with Nurture is the most prominent example, but three other vendors have launched comparable AI engagement layers in 2026, each with different integration depth and data governance approaches. The category is forming, not finished.</p><p>The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">June 8, 2026 GlobeNewswire announcement</a> confirmed that Nurture is now accessible directly from the Pushpay dashboard with a single point of entry. Single sign-on and deeper workflow integrations are on the roadmap. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-to-expect-from-pushpays-staq-transformation/">CPO blog post from June 16</a> laid out five committed deliverables by December 2026: Groups optimization (June), AI Involvement Summary (August), Single Staq Giving (September), Staq Data Lake (September), and expanded Process Queues (September).</p><p>Beyond Pushpay, three other platforms have moved into adjacent territory this year. ACST launched its Ministry Platform AI Pioneer Program in May 2026, as reported by <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">BusinessWire</a>, using an MCP-based architecture that allows plain-English queries across congregational data. Subsplash rolled out Trends AI in March 2026 with more than 25 chart types drawing from its engagement data hub. Rock RMS, paired with the 9 Embers Agent 9 integration, offers plain-English queries across families, attendance, giving, and serving records.</p><p>Here is the current landscape as we track it:</p><p>VendorProductStatus as of June 2026Key differentiatorPushpay + NurtureStaq MAS / NurtureLive — dashboard integration June 8, AI features August 2026Broadest integration depth (17+ platform connections)ACST (Realm + Ministry Platform)Ministry Platform AIPioneer Program live May 2026 (50 churches)MCP-based plain-English queryingSubsplashTrends AILive March 2026Consolidated analytics across Subsplash productsRock RMS + 9 EmbersAgent 9Live 2026Open-source ecosystem; cross-domain plain-English queriesPlanning CenterNo announcement—No MAS equivalent announced as of June 2026BreezeNo announcement—No MAS equivalent announced as of June 2026</p><p>Two things worth noting about this table. First, Planning Center and Breeze serve a large share of the church market and neither has announced a comparable product. Churches on those platforms are not excluded from the MAS conversation, but they would need to evaluate third-party integrations rather than a native offering. Second, the capabilities above are still evolving rapidly. We will update this comparison as announcements land.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> "Ministry Action System" is Pushpay and Nurture's term for a category that other vendors are building under different names. Before deciding whether to add a MAS to your stack, we recommend evaluating which tool integrates cleanest with your existing ChMS, regardless of what it is called.</p><h2>Three Questions to Ask Before Adding a Ministry Action System to Your Stack</h2><p>Before committing to any Ministry Action System, church leaders should ask about data ownership, integration depth with their existing tools, and how the AI layer handles sensitive pastoral data. These questions apply whether you are evaluating Nurture, a competing platform, or any future offering in this emerging category.</p><p>The governance dimension is not optional. The <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">2026 Barna/Pushpay State of Church Tech report</a> found that 83% of church leaders say data privacy in AI is a concern, yet only 5% of churches have a formal AI policy in place. A Ministry Action System that aggregates data across 17 integrations sits directly in that gap.</p><p>Joe Koehling, CEO of ACST, put the tension plainly in the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20260507886746/en/ACST-Launches-Ministry-Platform-AI-Bringing-Secure-Ministry-Aware-AI-to-Churches">May 7, 2026 BusinessWire announcement</a>: "What we heard from churches was clear: they want the benefits of AI, but they do not want to compromise trust, stewardship, or congregational privacy to get there." That describes the due diligence challenge exactly.</p><p>We recommend asking three questions before any MAS evaluation conversation:</p><p><strong>1. Who owns our congregational data, and can we export it if we cancel?</strong></p><p>A MAS that aggregates data from 17 integrations creates a consolidated profile of your congregation that did not exist before in any single place. Understand upfront what happens to that aggregated data if you switch platforms. The individual source systems (your ChMS, your giving platform) may still hold their records, but the unified profile that the MAS created is a new data asset. Clarify ownership in writing before signing.</p><p><strong>2. Does this tool integrate with our current ChMS, or does it require a platform migration?</strong></p><p>Nurture's 17 integrations and the other vendor offerings listed above have different levels of compatibility with different ChMS platforms. Churches on Planning Center or Breeze should verify the integration roadmap before committing. A MAS that requires switching your ChMS to function is a significantly larger decision than adding an engagement layer to your existing stack.</p><p><strong>3. What governance do we have in place for AI processing of pastoral data?</strong></p><p>Per the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-leaders-ai-usage-concerns/">2026 Barna/Pushpay State of Church Tech report</a>, 83% of church leaders say data privacy in AI is a concern, but only 5% of churches have a formal AI policy. If your church has not addressed this yet, adding a MAS before you have a governance framework in place creates compounded risk. Pastoral data — attendance patterns, giving history, small group participation — is among the most sensitive information a church holds. A formal policy does not need to be long, but it needs to exist before that data begins flowing through an AI scoring layer.</p><p>We covered <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://app.pressmaster.ai/article/edit/f9333ef6-911c-479f-8229-3e6df59bdc61">the five questions to ask your ChMS vendor about AI before renewal</a> in a dedicated post. Many of those questions apply directly to Ministry Action System evaluation as well.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> The governance question is the most commonly overlooked. Churches that have not yet addressed AI data policy will find that the MAS evaluation process surfaces the gap quickly — often at the contract stage.</p><h2>What the Pushpay Staq Transformation Means for Churches Already on Pushpay</h2><p>Pushpay's June 16, 2026 roadmap announcement from CPO Gruia Pitigoi-Aron lays out five specific deliverables by December 2026 that move the combined Pushpay/Staq/Nurture platform toward a full Ministry Action System. If your church is currently on Pushpay, this section explains what is coming and what it will require from your team.</p><p>The starting point is the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">June 8, 2026 integration milestone</a>: Nurture is now accessible directly from the Pushpay dashboard. The Staq Transformation is partly a product consolidation initiative designed to make existing features more accessible. Churches that have not yet used the full Pushpay feature set will find the roadmap relevant beyond the Nurture integration specifically.</p><p>What this means in practical terms: churches already on Pushpay can begin evaluating Nurture now through the existing dashboard integration. The AI-powered engagement scoring features are four to five months out. That window is useful for the governance work described in the previous section, particularly building or updating a church AI policy before the AI Involvement Summary goes live.</p><p>We offer <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://app.pressmaster.ai/article/edit/d4748e17-2148-4ced-af47-a1a2232d0250">our guide to rolling out AI in church management software without breaking staff trust</a> as a companion resource for churches working through that preparation.</p><p><strong>Key Point:</strong> If your church is on Pushpay and you have not yet explored Nurture, the June 8 integration milestone means it is now accessible directly from your existing dashboard. The AI-powered features arrive in August and September 2026. That gives your team roughly two months to evaluate whether your governance framework is ready.</p><h2>Frequently Asked Questions</h2><h3>What is a Ministry Action System in simple terms?</h3><p>A Ministry Action System is software that connects your existing church tools — your ChMS, giving platform, and communication system — and tells your team who needs pastoral attention right now. Unlike a church management system, which stores records, a MAS reads across those records and surfaces disengagement signals so your staff can act before someone drifts away entirely. The term was introduced to the U.S. church market by <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/04/22/3279027/0/en/pushpay-announces-acquisition-of-nurture-io-adding-pastoral-care-and-engagement-intelligence-for-churches.html">Pushpay's April 2026 acquisition of Nurture.io</a>.</p><h3>Does my church need a Ministry Action System if we already have a ChMS?</h3><p>Not every church needs one. A MAS adds the most value when a church already has a functioning ChMS, a dedicated pastoral follow-up team, and enough data volume that manual review is creating gaps. Smaller churches with a single pastor who knows every member by name are unlikely to see significant benefit from the additional complexity and cost. The return on investment scales with congregation size, data quality, and staff capacity to act on the signals the system surfaces.</p><h3>How does Nurture.io work with Pushpay?</h3><p>As of <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">June 8, 2026</a>, Nurture.io is accessible directly from the Pushpay dashboard for churches that use both platforms. Pushpay acquired Nurture in April 2026. The current integration provides a single point of entry to Nurture from within the Pushpay ChMS. Deeper integrations including single sign-on and the ability to assign a person to a Nurture Process Queue from within the ChMS are on the roadmap for later in 2026, per the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-to-expect-from-pushpays-staq-transformation/">CPO blog post</a>.</p><h3>What data does a Ministry Action System collect about our congregation?</h3><p>A MAS aggregates data your church is already collecting across existing platforms: attendance records, giving history, volunteer engagement, small group participation, and communication responses. Nurture, for example, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/06/08/3308161/0/en/Pushpay-Introduces-a-Streamlined-Customer-Experience-Between-Pushpay-and-Nurture-io-Platforms.html">integrates with more than 17 church technology platforms</a>. No new data collection occurs in that sense. The MAS reads what is already there. The critical governance question is who has access to that aggregated profile and what happens to the data if you switch vendors.</p><h3>What is the Pushpay Staq Transformation?</h3><p>The Staq Transformation is Pushpay's publicly announced platform roadmap, detailed by <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://pushpay.com/blog/what-to-expect-from-pushpays-staq-transformation/">CPO Gruia Pitigoi-Aron in a June 16, 2026 blog post</a>. It consolidates Pushpay's ChMS, giving, and Nurture engagement tools into a unified platform. Five deliverables are committed by December 2026: Groups optimization (June), an AI Involvement Summary (August), Single Staq Giving (September), a Staq Data Lake (September), and expanded Process Queues (September).</p><h3>Is a Ministry Action System the same as a CRM?</h3><p>They share some structural similarities, but the purposes are different. A CRM is designed for sales pipeline management — tracking the path from prospect to customer. A MAS is designed for pastoral care and congregational engagement — tracking the health of an existing relationship within a faith community. The data model, the actions it surfaces, and the outcomes it measures are all oriented around ministry rather than commercial conversion.</p><h3>Which churches should consider adding a Ministry Action System in 2026?</h3><p>Churches that will benefit most are those with congregations of roughly 200 or more attenders, an existing ChMS with reasonably clean data, and dedicated pastoral follow-up staff who currently rely on intuition or manual reports to identify who needs connection. If your team is regularly asking "who haven't we seen in a while?" and cannot answer that question systematically with your current tools, a Ministry Action System is worth evaluating.</p><h2>Key Takeaways</h2><ul><li><p><strong>A Ministry Action System sits above your existing technology, not instead of it.</strong> It aggregates data from your ChMS, giving platform, and communication tools into a unified engagement view — then routes specific pastoral follow-up actions to your team.</p></li><li><p><strong>The category was formally introduced to the U.S. church market in April 2026</strong> when Pushpay acquired Nurture.io. The term "Ministry Action System" is Pushpay and Nurture's own language; competing vendors are building similar capabilities under different names.</p></li><li><p><strong>The core problem a MAS solves is pastoral capacity, not administrative efficiency.</strong> Per the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.barna.com/research/church-technology-mission/">2026 Barna/Pushpay State of Church Tech report</a>, only 9% of church leaders say technology primarily helps with discipleship. A MAS is designed to route specific pastoral follow-up actions, not just surface data.</p></li><li><p><strong>Data governance questions matter before you add a MAS.</strong> 83% of church leaders say AI data privacy is a concern, yet only 5% have formal AI policies. A MAS that aggregates 17 integrations raises those concerns directly.</p></li><li><p><strong>If your church is on Pushpay, Nurture is now accessible directly from your dashboard</strong> as of June 8, 2026. Deeper AI-powered features arrive on the roadmap in August and September 2026. Review <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://app.pressmaster.ai/article/edit/88f11325-c7f5-4f17-8e03-0454492ef65d">the full landscape of AI features across major ChMS vendors in 2026</a> to understand how the Staq Transformation fits the broader picture.</p></li><li><p><strong>Not every church needs a Ministry Action System.</strong> The value scales with congregation size, staff capacity, and data quality. **Milestone 1 — Broken links (T004-T018, spec US1/P1)**: Apply the 14 blog-content-file diffs in the Exact File Changes section that touch `<a href>`/markdown-link values. Outcome: zero broken internal links. Proof: `blc` run against local dev server (quickstart.md) shows no `├─BROKEN─` lines; manually click the CTA from 3+ posts and confirm it lands on `/contact/`.

**Milestone 2 — Titles and descriptions (T019-T027, spec US2/P2)**: Apply the regression test first (must fail), then `textConverter.ts` + `SEO.astro`, then the three blog-template files, the two taxonomy-template files, the homepage frontmatter, and the `five-cyberattacks` description trim (already included in that file's Milestone-1 diff). Outcome: no oversized/duplicate titles or descriptions. Proof: `npm run test` passes; view-source spot checks per quickstart.md.

**Milestone 3 — Structural fixes (T028-T035 subset, spec US3/P3)**: Apply the H1-dedup diff, the 6 stray-`<h1></h1>`-removal diffs (5 already included in their Milestone-1 file edits; `chms-ai-data-governance-church-leaders-guide-2026.mdx` has no link fixes, only this), and the `HeroSection.astro` kicker fix. Outcome: every page has exactly one H1 and no heading-level skip. Proof: browser devtools heading inspection on each affected URL.

**Milestone 4 — Full validation (T039-T044, all stories)**: `npm run astro-check`, `npm run test`, `npm audit`/`pnpm audit`, deploy to preview, re-run the OpenSEO audit and Unlighthouse, confirm build parity across Netlify/Vercel/Cloudflare Pages.

**Not in this plan's Exact File Changes (see Risks and Decisions)**: T036 (trailing-slash platform confirmation), T037 (slow-response investigation), T038 (noindex confirmation) — each requires information this planning pass cannot obtain from the repository alone.

## Concrete Steps

Working directory for all commands: repository root (the branch created in Milestone 0, checked out from `origin/production`).

1. `git checkout -b 001-seo-audit-remediation origin/production` — expected: new branch created, working tree matches `origin/production`.
2. `npm install` — expected: clean install, no errors.
3. `npm run astro-check` — expected: zero errors/warnings on the unmodified baseline (confirms a clean starting point before any edit).
4. Apply each diff in Exact File Changes, in the order listed (template/component files first, then content files) — `git apply <diff>` per file, or apply via editor.
5. `npm run test` — expected: all tests pass, including the new `resolveTaglineSeparator.test.ts`.
6. `npm run astro-check` — expected: zero errors/warnings after all edits.
7. `npm run dev` then, in a second terminal, `blc http://localhost:4321/ -ovre --filter-level 0 > broken-links.txt && grep "├─BROKEN─" broken-links.txt` — expected: no output (no broken links found).
8. Manually visit `/blog/chms-vendor-ai-questions-before-renewal/`, `/blog/`, `/blog/page/2/`, `/blog/category/technology/`, one tag page, `/` (homepage), and `/blog/denominational-ai-statements-church-technology-decisions/` in a browser; view-source and inspect headings per quickstart.md.
9. `git add -A && git commit -m "fix(seo): remediate 2026-07-20 audit findings"` — one commit for the whole implemented plan, per this feature's task-list note to commit after each logical group (or per-milestone, at implementer's discretion).

## Validation and Acceptance

- **Behavior proof, not just compilation**: `npm run astro-check` passing proves the TypeScript/Astro compiles; it does NOT prove the links resolve or the titles render correctly — steps 7-8 above are required for that.
- **Success case**: every link in Milestone 1's files resolves 2xx when crawled; every title in Milestone 2's files is ≤60 chars (or explicitly justified) when viewed via view-source; every heading in Milestone 3's files descends one level at a time via devtools inspection.
- **Failure case**: if `blc` still reports a broken link after applying all Milestone 1 diffs, the drift check (T003) should be re-run first — the file may have changed on `origin/production` since this plan was written.
- **Boundary case**: the `five-cyberattacks...mdx` file is touched by both Milestone 1 (link removal) and Milestone 2 (description trim) — both edits are already combined into that file's single diff in Exact File Changes; do not apply it twice.
- **Regression case**: the new `resolveTaglineSeparator.test.ts` must fail against the current `textConverter.ts` (no such export yet) before the `textConverter.ts` diff is applied, and pass after — this is the Constitution Principle IV proof.
- **Compatibility case**: no page outside the 25 files listed changes behavior — `disableTagline` was already an optional, unused-by-default field, so pages not explicitly touched here are unaffected.

## Idempotence and Recovery

- All edits are plain content/text diffs to existing tracked files (plus one new test file) — safe to re-apply `git apply --check` before applying for real; if a diff fails to apply (e.g. due to drift since this plan was written), re-run the Foundational drift check (T003) rather than force-applying.
- No migrations, no generated files, no build artifacts are committed by this plan.
- Rollback: `git revert` the single commit from Concrete Steps step 9, or `git checkout origin/production -- <path>` per file if only some edits need reverting.

## Risks and Decisions

| Risk / Decision | Status | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| Homepage title rewrite is a brand/marketing call | **Flagged for review** | Proposed replacement (`"Fractional CTO Leadership for Mid-Sized Churches"`) is defensible and evidence-based (removes redundancy with the tagline, fits the length guideline) but is real visible brand copy on the site's highest-traffic page — recommend a stakeholder sign-off pass before this specific diff is applied, independent of the rest of the plan. |
| Homepage kicker `<h3>`→`<div>` touches the hero | **Styling gap found and corrected this pass; visual smoke-test still recommended** | First draft of this diff swapped the tag but kept only the element's own existing classes, silently dropping `font-secondary`, `leading-tight`, `font-bold`, and `wrap-break-word` — properties that were actually coming from a global bare `h1-h6` tag-selector rule in `src/styles/base.css:27-38`, not from the element's own classes, and that a `<div>` no longer matches. Corrected by adding those four classes explicitly onto the `<div>` (see Exact File Changes). The fix is now visually equivalent by construction, not just "should be fine" — but since this is the homepage hero, still recommend a visual smoke-test on a preview deploy before merging, as a confirmation rather than a hedge against a known gap. |
| T036 — trailing-slash platform confirmation | **Not resolved, needs investigation** | Which of Netlify/Vercel/Cloudflare Pages is authoritative for `goodshepherdinsights.com` DNS could not be determined from repository config alone (`vercel.json` sets `trailingSlash: true` explicitly; Netlify/Cloudflare equivalents were not confirmed). Action: whoever has DNS/hosting access confirms the authoritative platform, then internal links to `/about`, `/blog/church-digital-giving-strategy`, `/blog/recurring-giving-church-donation-math` (non-slash forms) get normalized in a follow-up diff. |
| T037 — slow-response root cause | **Not resolved, needs live investigation** | `astro.config.mjs` shows a fully static build with no server/adapter — nothing in the repository explains a 2-3s response time. Action: reproduce against the live production URLs (not local dev) and check CDN cache-hit headers before proposing a fix; guessing at a caching fix without reproduction risks not addressing the real cause (research R4). |
| T038 — taxonomy noindex intent | **Likely intentional, recommend confirming rather than treating as open** | New evidence found during this planning pass: `category/[category].astro:99` and `tag/[tag].astro:100` both carry a `<!-- ISSUES 3/5: noindex, follow + ISSUE 6: CollectionPage schema -->` comment, meaning a previous developer deliberately added `robots="noindex, follow"` to address specific, already-numbered issues. This is strong evidence it was intentional (thin/duplicate-content taxonomy pages are a common, deliberate noindex target). Recommend closing this as "confirmed intentional" with a one-line note in quickstart.md rather than removing the directive, but a explicit stakeholder yes/no is still the cleanest way to close spec FR-013 formally. |
| Two dead-link targets have no replacement content | **Resolved per spec FR-003, not re-litigated here** | `five-cyberattacks...mdx` (2 links) and `what-happens-to-your-church-s-member-data...mdx` (1 link) reference content that was never published; this plan removes/rewrites those links rather than authoring the missing pieces, per spec's explicit scope exclusion. |

**2026-07-20 — Second pass: Review Gate verification**

- Ran the full Review Gate: extracted all 25 fenced ` ```diff ` blocks from this document in file order via a Python regex pass, concatenated them, and ran `git apply --check` from the repository root of the `origin/production` detached-HEAD worktree used throughout this planning session. **Result: passed (exit 0).**
- Checked path consistency: for every one of the 25 file entries, confirmed programmatically that the `### \`path\`` heading, the diff's `--- a/<path>` line, and its `+++ b/<path>` line all use the exact same repo-relative path. **Result: 0 mismatches.**
- **Issue found and corrected in this pass**: initially added a `diff --git a/<path> b/<path>` header line to every block, matching the Exact-Change Contract's worked example formatting. This broke `git apply --check` for the one `Create` action (`src/__tests__/resolveTaglineSeparator.test.ts`) — `make-diff.sh`'s `create` mode produces `--- a/<path>` / `+++ b/<path>` (not `--- /dev/null`) by design, and once a `diff --git` line is added without a matching `--- /dev/null` or `new file mode` marker, `git apply` stops treating it as a new-file creation and instead looks for an existing file to modify, failing with "No such file or directory". Reverted to the original, unmodified output of `make-diff.sh` for all 25 blocks (no `diff --git` line) and re-ran both checks above against the reverted content — both passed. This confirms the skill's own bundled script's output format should be used as-is rather than reformatted to match the contract's worked example cosmetically.
- All 25 files in the directory map, modification table, and Exact File Changes section were confirmed to appear exactly once across all three, per the Plan Contract's requirement.
- Milestones re-checked against the current goal: still correctly ordered (link fixes first as the only `critical`-severity issue type, then title/description mechanism fixes, then structural cleanup, then full validation); each milestone's proof step still matches what quickstart.md actually validates.
- No new material issues found this pass beyond the one already corrected above.
- Result: **Prepared — awaiting explicit approval.** This is the first pass to independently verify a prior revision (the diff-header correction) without introducing a new one, satisfying this skill's requirement that a later, separate pass confirm a revision before `Prepared` can be claimed.

**2026-07-20 — Third pass: material issue found during human review of `Prepared` plan**

- While explaining the "Homepage kicker touches the hero" risk entry to the plan's reviewer, re-inspected the actual `HeroSection.astro` diff against `src/styles/base.css` and found the diff's own **Impact** line ("Visual appearance is unchanged... all Tailwind classes preserved verbatim") was false: `base.css:27-38` applies `font-secondary text-dark leading-tight font-bold tracking-wide wrap-break-word` to every bare `h1`-`h6` tag site-wide. The kicker element's own hardcoded classes only separately covered `text-dark`, sizing, and `tracking-wider` — `font-secondary`, `leading-tight`, `font-bold`, and `wrap-break-word` were being supplied *only* by that global tag-selector rule and would have silently disappeared once the tag became a `<div>`.
- Corrected the diff to add those four classes explicitly onto the `<div>` (kept `tracking-wider`, not `tracking-wide`, since the element's own `tracking-wider` utility class already outranked the base-layer `tracking-wide` before this change too — Tailwind v4's `utilities` layer beats its `base` layer regardless of tag or source order — so adding `tracking-wide` now would have no effect).
- Updated the Impact line, the Pattern Audit table row, and the Risks and Decisions table row to state the corrected, accurate picture instead of the original (wrong) "visually inert" claim.
- Result: **Revised — review again.** A real correctness defect in a plan already marked `Prepared` was found and fixed; per this skill's rule, this pass cannot also claim `Prepared`.

**2026-07-20 — Fourth pass: independent re-verification of the third-pass revision**

- Re-extracted all 25 diff blocks from the document (regex pass, file order) and re-ran the concatenated `git apply --check` against the `origin/production` worktree. **Result: passed (exit 0).**
- Re-ran the path-consistency check across all 25 heading/diff pairs. **Result: 0 mismatches.**
- Explicitly confirmed the `HeroSection.astro` diff block now contains all four restored classes (`font-secondary`, `font-bold`, `leading-tight`, `wrap-break-word`) rather than trusting the edit was applied correctly by assumption.
- No further material issues found this pass.
- Result: **Prepared — awaiting explicit approval.** This is an independent pass verifying the third pass's revision, not the same pass that made it.

## Approval

Implementation must not begin until this plan reaches `Prepared — awaiting explicit approval` status and a human explicitly approves it (via `/speckit-implementation-planning-approve`). Reaching `Prepared` requires a subsequent review pass that runs the concatenated `git apply --check` per the Review Gate and finds no material issue. Even after approval, the three flagged items in Risks and Decisions (homepage title copy, homepage hero change, and T036-T038) warrant explicit attention during implementation review, not silent pass-through.
